- **[Co-Sight Harness: Externalized Control Assets and Deterministic Enforcement for Long-Horizon Business Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17696)**  `arXiv:2611.17696`  
  _Luyao Wang, Xijun Zhu, Zhaohui Hao, Xiwen Zhang, Xingyu Wen, Weishan Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon business workflows fail in two ways: errors accumulate as a chain of activities lengthens, and the knowledge an activity needs is written in a different vocabulary from the user's request. We study whether such workflows can be made reliable without changing the language model. Our method keeps the backbone fixed and moves the workflow definition, state and domain knowledge into versioned control assets outside the model. A state machine and machine-checkable acceptance gates enforce these assets during execution; an independent auditor grades the resulting trace. The assets are updated only after a regression check and human approval .We evaluate the method on three deployed workflows exceeding 200 steps. On Domain A, a 120-case end-to-end evaluation set shows unattended completion rising from 50% to 100%, retrieval recall from 56% to 91.67%, and end-to-end latency dropping from 440 to 159 minutes, with scenario-analysis recall rising from 45% to 93%. Two other domains were taken over by teams that did not write the assets, one in four days and one in seven days. A controlled ablation separates the two failure sources. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified? A Diagnostic Benchmark for Multimodal Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17680)**  `arXiv:2611.17680`  
  _Xintong Wang, Haihan Nan, Ruidong Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial Intelligence of Things (AIoT) systems increasingly combine sensed time series with external text such as expert reports and news, yet how reliably models use text that agrees or conflicts with the sensed history remains unclear. We introduce the first real-world diagnostic benchmark organized by text-history relationship: 1,762 text-window pairs across sensor telemetry (Weather), web-service traffic (Wikipedia), and market data streams (Finance), each labeled Aligned, Conflicting, or Unspecified by whether the text's direction agrees with the historical trend, opposes it, or is not stated. Across ten pretrained models evaluated zero-shot, we investigate text selection and combination under text-history relationships (RQ1) and error diagnosis and horizon-scaled revision control (RQ2). For RQ1, we compare two types of text on paired Weather windows: one directly forecasts temperature changes, while the other describes related weather conditions, such as winds and fronts. Under Aligned, the first type yields the lowest error for most models. Under Conflicting, it increases most models' error above the no-text baseline; we therefore add only its stated magnitude and timing to the second type. Compared with either type alone, this combination lowers mean absolute scaled error (MASE) for six models and raises directional accuracy (DA) for seven. For RQ2, an error decomposition attributes the error increase to oversized revisions, and a training-free rule that limits them outperforms forecasts both with and without text for the four models with this trade-off, lowering MASE by 4.49%-21.98% and raising DA by 2.99%-9.71%. Our dataset is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learn then Bet: Learning Interpretable Rules for Reliable Agent Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17662)**  `arXiv:2611.17662`  
  _You-Lin Chen, Xinyi Lisa Chen, Pedro Herrero-Vidal, Xiaojing Yu, Bingqing Song, Bin Xu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents now run for hundreds of steps with little or no human supervision. In practice, practitioners use runtime monitors such as turn limits and keyword filters to decide when a run should be stopped, upgraded, or assisted. However, these heuristics may stop runs that would otherwise succeed or allow failing runs to continue. To address this limitation, we introduce LeBet (Learn then Bet), a statistical learning framework that derives its learning objective from sufficient conditions for valid and powerful intervention. In particular, LeBet learns an interpretable OR-of-AND monitor and accumulates evidence through a fixed betting statistic, intervening when the evidence reaches a prescribed threshold. Under finite within-class exchangeability and leave-one-out stability, we derive finite-sample guarantees for false-positive control (validity) and detection power conditional on the training data, using the same trajectories for learning and calibration without assuming independent and identically distributed trajectories. To address the intractable combinatorial optimization problem in our learning formulation, we use stochastic local search that leverages the monitor's Boolean structure to propose rule edits aimed at improving empirical separation. On SWE-bench Lite with Ministral 3 8B, the learned monitor saves $34.46\%$ of input-plus-output tokens and increases output precision from $18.33\%$ to $21.59\%$. The empirical false-positive rate is $13.64\%$. Our results show how to formalize the learning problem over interpretable rule sets with statistical conditions and establish training-conditional guarantees for reliable intervention based on partial histories.
  </details>

- **[What LLM Agents See and What Users Say](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17639)**  `arXiv:2611.17639`  
  _Yue Zhao, Angela Huyue Zhang, S. Alex Yang, Hua Wei, Xiyang Hu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Before an LLM agent cancels a reservation or deletes a folder for a user, what stops it from acting on a false assumption: what its tools show, or what the user says? In this work, we study this gap with presupposition theory from linguistics: an agent may act only if each claim behind the act (e.g., one reservation fits the name) is seen to hold, answered by the user, or disclosed while the act can be undone. A simple bound shows that when lookups cannot tell one record from two, any agent acts without asking first as often with two records as with one. We then vary whether a search lists, counts, or omits a second match, and whether the user states a claim that a record refutes (e.g., "It is empty"). Under a role-only prompt, a count of a second record leaves most of 36 earlier agents acting on the one they see, whereas listing both stops most. When the user says a record such as a folder is empty, however, a count of its one item stops agents about as often as a listing, and stating the claim mostly cuts acting when it is false. The newest Claude and GPT models always open a counted second record, then never act. When we add a same-name, same-zip customer to a retail benchmark, a lookup returning that customer first led to wrong-account writes in about a quarter of conversations, and one that checks a street the user knows kept completion near the one-customer level. Safe writes therefore need lookups that list or check competing records, and evaluations that vary what tools show.
  </details>

- **[Generated Evaluation Suites as Diversity-Driven Search for Real Agent Regressions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17632)**  `arXiv:2611.17632`  
  _Pulkit Yadav_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating an evaluation suite from an agent's specification is diversity-driven search: a generator fills a descriptor space (requirements $\times$ personas $\times$ scenario kinds), and the suite it emits is an archive, then scored like a benchmark by its mean pass rate, which throws away the search's product. We inject 7 controlled defects into a $\tau^2$-bench agent across two domains. Coverage yields a ceiling: if a defect's traced footprint holds $b$ scenarios that pass at baseline, it can itself contribute at most $b/N$ to a suite of $N$. For 4 of 5 traceable defects that ceiling lies at or below the detection threshold $\theta = 0.10$, so no within-footprint effect can move the suite past it; in both domains the tool-removal defect destroys every covered scenario it can (exactly its ceiling) while the suite-level pass rate moves the other way. Defects split into two regimes: trace-aligned ones, where per-cell scoring recovers the tool-removal defect in full and reveals a second as a significant improvement, and trace-orthogonal ones, whose footprint is behavioural: forced escalation is detected in retail at a traced coverage of exactly zero. Diversity along the generator's own axes helps only where it aligns: at fixed suite size a happy-path-only suite detects nothing, while a kind-diverse one detects the orchestration defect in 5 of 20 draws. Evaluation archives should be scored per cell and should publish coverage as a first-class property.
  </details>

- **[Safety Side Effects of Preventative Persona Steering: An Empirical Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17620)**  `arXiv:2611.17620`  
  _Sabrina Tang, Nikolai Vo, Carson Murillo, Chira Sukumarnpiti, Sarah Wilson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-level behaviors such as sycophancy and hallucination correspond to directions in a model's activation space (Chen et al., 2025). These directions, known as persona vectors, can suppress undesirable traits either at inference time, subtracting the vector during generation, or through preventative steering, adding the vector during fine-tuning, so the optimizer never needs to encode the trait in weights, then removing the vector at evaluation. Chen et al. (2025) recommend preventative steering because it preserves Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU) accuracy, but validations of this recommendation have measured capability alone. We primarily evaluate results on preventative steering of sycophancy and hallucination vectors on three models, measuring three safety benchmarks alongside trait expression, coherence, and MMLU. Our findings show that preventative steering still changes refusal behavior, but by an amount that depends on the trait and does not establish that preventative persona steering is safety-neutral, and steering methods should be evaluated on several safety benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Guided Cluster Lifecycle Management: Deciding When and How to Evolve Behavioral Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17616)**  `arXiv:2611.17616`  
  _Himanshu Kulkarni, Siddharth Krishnamurthy, Jianhong Xin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Behavioral clusters learned over user or item interactions drift as behavior evolves: some fragment into sub-behaviors, some converge, some disappear, and new patterns emerge. Two paradigms dominate the maintenance of evolving clusterings. Threshold triggers fire a pre-specified action when a hardcoded statistic crosses a cutoff; refit-and-track (MONIC-style) re-clusters every window and infers transitions from how the new clusters align with the previous window's. Both hardwire the decision into a threshold or a re-clustering rule. We study an alternative in which a large language model (LLM) makes the lifecycle decision in place. Given a threshold-free, whole-population view of the current cluster set (statistical health, labels, member texts, residual mass), the LLM decides per cluster whether and how to act (split, merge, retire, create, or no-action) without re-clustering. Because the useful signal channel depends on how informative the labels are, we fix a richness-conditioned policy in advance: statistics when label distinctiveness is low, fused statistics+semantics when it is high. On three public datasets spanning label richness (YooChoose 0.17, MOOC 0.50, News-Category 0.70), the policy-selected LLM mode obtains the highest F1 on all three, with far fewer interventions and stronger restraint than tuned threshold heuristics and a MONIC steelman (paired Wilcoxon p <= 0.003, bootstrap 95% CIs excluding zero). The gain is concentrated more in precision than recall. The useful modality shifts from statistics to semantics/fusion as richness rises. Semantics hurt on YooChoose, fusion is roughly neutral on MOOC, and the semantic channel is required on News. One base prompt template and one fixed policy serve all three datasets, whereas the tuned baseline collapses when moved between them. We treat this shift as a practitioner diagnostic observed on three datasets, not a validated law.
  </details>

- **[More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? Untangling Coordination, Ensembling, and Test-Time Compute in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17610)**  `arXiv:2611.17610`  
  _Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluations of multi-agent language models often compare communicating architectures against a single greedy pass. This practice confounds coordination with sample multiplicity and inference compute. We synthesize nine primary empirical evaluations comprising 516 unique multi-agent configurations organized along a resource vector R with six dimensions and a baseline ladder with four categories. Against baselines with a single pass (Category A), multi-agent accuracy differences are negative overall and near zero when omitting the largest contributing study. When evaluated against baselines matched on sample count (Category B) or resource budget (Category C), multi-agent systems average -0.042 in accuracy, and multilevel random effects modeling on the log odds scale remains negative across sensitivity checks. Multi-agent accuracy equals or falls below the baseline in 74% of these comparable contrasts, and study means are nonpositive in almost all evaluated studies. Across studies reporting both baselines, moving from a greedy pass to a sampled baseline shifts accuracy differences by -0.038 on average. Because communication adds no information when agents share identical evidence, positive returns require partitioned evidence, external verification, or error diversity across models. We provide a reviewer checklist and accounting utilities.
  </details>

- **[When Does Less Information Pay? An Exact Finite Atlas of Representation, Payoff, and Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17583)**  `arXiv:2611.17583`  
  _Aleksandr M. Shtraukh-Adamsky, Vladimir M. Adamsky, Irina G. Voloshina, Artemiy D. Spitsyn_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A representation that retains one bit about a four-state world can solve a task perfectly, while another that retains 1.5 bits on average reaches only 75% accuracy. The difference lies in which distinctions each representation preserves. We examine this relationship in a uniform four-state world by enumerating all admissible representations and binary action rules. Constructions are compared both by replacing one component while holding the others fixed and by choosing the best action rule separately for each module. We consider three tasks, their mixtures, and four separate hypothetical cost tariffs.In some comparisons, the representation retaining less world information has the higher payoff; in others, the more informative representation does. There are also pairs with different information amounts but equal payoff--that is, equal task accuracy. If a representation preserves all existing distinctions and adds new ones, its best zero-cost payoff cannot decrease. Lower-information advantages therefore depend on which distinctions are retained. Equal counts of opposing advantages under representation replacement follow from coding symmetry. In broader comparisons, the shares depend on how repeated realizations are counted. A different task set or resource price can change which member of a fixed pair performs better.These results are exact, complete censuses of the specified finite sets of constructions and comparisons. The atlas provides a reproducible worked model for examining how action constraints, counting rules, and cost assumptions affect comparisons. It illustrates established distinctions between information quantity and decision value rather than proposing a new general law of their relation. $I(W;R)$ measures retained world information, and payoff measures task accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Verifiable pXRD: Hill-climbing pXRD structure determination with LLM agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17539)**  `arXiv:2611.17539`  
  _Samuel Chen, Jiaru Bai, Austin Henry Cheng, Varinia Bernales, Alán Aspuru-Guzik_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous crystal-structure determination from powder X-ray diffraction (pXRD) remains a key bottleneck for self-driving materials laboratories. We formulate the task of pXRD structure determination as a verifiable agentic search problem: candidate structures can be assessed during inference through forward-simulated profile agreement and during evaluation through structural matching to a known reference. To benchmark progress, we introduce pXRD-Bench, a suite of 200 simulated single-phase problems spanning in-distribution structure determination, challenging and polymorphic structures, and controlled compositional and topological distribution shifts. Agent adaptation raises selected structural recovery from 27% to 69%, with further gains from repeated sampling and verifier-guided inference-time scaling. We extend this approach to experimental multiphase pXRD through a reflexive evolution framework that learns from self-generated synthetic problems. A replaceable laboratory specification defines the sample family and measurement conditions. An evolver agent uses the known generating structures and measurement parameters to diagnose solver failures and develop persistent guidance and executable tools, which are then provided to agents on measured patterns. This supplies verifiable training experience without requiring a collection of solved experimental measurements. On 15 measured patterns from solid-state synthesis, the evolved package improves average profile agreement and transfers an analogue-substitution procedure that recovers a phase whose structure is absent from the supplied reference library. These results demonstrate how learning from verifiable synthetic experience can improve agentic characterization of experimental samples whose structures are not known in advance.
  </details>

- **[Model Annealing: Data-Efficient Learning through Review and Consolidation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17524)**  `arXiv:2611.17524`  
  _Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Rezvan Abouzadeh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A small labelled dataset is memorized long before the training budget runs out, and the one-hot labels that remain to be fitted then mostly sharpen confidence. Model annealing spends the rest of the budget on review and consolidation, relearning from the network's own knowledge. Training is divided into episodes whose learning rate is raised again and cooled, every cooled state joins a memory of past selves, and each new episode learns from the labels, from the tempered predictions of this memory, and from adversarial dreams sought where student and memory disagree most. The memory averages predictions rather than weights, and the deployed network is the last student, so inference costs nothing extra. Across CIFAR-100, Tiny-ImageNet and Imagewoof, four architecture families and 5\% to 100\% of the labels, model annealing raises the top-1 accuracy of the AdamW-trained ConvNeXt, ViT and MLP-Mixer over cross-entropy training in 28 of 33 configurations, by 1.3 points on average, and is on par in accuracy with snapshot distillation, the closest competitor. On these families it is better than both in likelihood, calibration and robustness to corruptions and small adversarial perturbations. For an SGD-trained ResNet-18 it lowers accuracy but still improves robustness to small adversarial perturbations. Separating the reheated schedule from relearning shows that relearning helps every family and that the losses come from reheats, which undo most of this network's fit to its training set. How early the accuracy of a cross-entropy run saturates largely separates the AdamW families from ResNet-18 but does not predict the gain within them. With little data, relearning from the latest snapshot without dreams, as snapshot distillation does, compounds overconfidence in the AdamW families from episode to episode. The memory and the dreams each curb most of this drift, and together they remove it.
  </details>

- **[Advancing Quantization for R&R Models with Microscaling Formats](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17501)**  `arXiv:2611.17501`  
  _Yavuz Yetim, Bor-Yiing Su, Zhe Wang, Arthur Liu, Pranav Sharma, Ying Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recommendation and Ranking (R&R) models, particularly in digital advertising, require massive inference throughput but exhibit extreme sensitivity to precision loss. Unlike Large Language Models (LLMs) that frequently tolerate quantization-induced accuracy drops of 1% or more, production-grade R&R models enforce a stringent launch bar: model quality must remain within a 0.01% Normalized Entropy (NE) margin relative to the baseline, which commonly uses FP16 or FP32. This tight constraint has historically hindered the adoption of aggressive quantization in this domain. In this paper, we present an empirical evaluation of both MXFP8 and the 6-bit MXFP6 format for industrial-scale R&R inference on the AMD MI350X architecture.To the best of our knowledge, this is the first paper, across R&R and LLM domains, that reports significant speedups from native MXFP6 GEMMs, which quantize both weights and activations and compute directly on 6-bit elements, without sacrificing model quality. We demonstrate that we can selectively apply this 6-bit format to maximize overall QPS gain without breaching the strict NE parity threshold. We further show that MXFP8 is a viable alternative to row-wise FP8: the two formats have nearly the same accuracy on our models, so the faster kernel can be chosen for each GEMM shape. On two production ads ranking models, our implementation shows measured GEMM kernel speedups over FP16 of 1.33--1.55x for MXFP8 and 1.73--1.99x for MXFP6 across all supported linear layers, and up to 2.90x for MXFP6 on individual layers, alongside end-to-end (E2E) inference throughput gains from selective MXFP6 of 1.5--5.3% for Model A and 7.6--14.3% for Model B over their FP8 configurations.
  </details>

- **[An Operationalization of Opaque Serial Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17495)**  `arXiv:2611.17495`  
  _Nathan Sheffield, Alek Westover, Lukas Finnveden, Alexa Pan, Julian Stastny, Ryan Greenblatt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) is currently a valuable tool for overseeing AI models, but some architectural shifts could significantly reduce CoT monitorability. We operationalize a measure that serves as a proxy for the amount of unverbalized serial cognition a model can perform, as a specific instantiation of the notion of opaque serial depth: roughly, the length of the longest path through a model's computation that does not pass through an interpretable bottleneck. Our main contribution is a particular standard for what counts as such a bottleneck. We define natural-language-rooted (NL-rooted) nodes as nodes that were initialized from a pre-training prior to produce human-understandable outputs, whose output space has not been expanded beyond that prior, and whose outputs the model has not been modified to interpret as a different type of data. By construction, the resulting measure, NLS depth, can be adjudicated before training from an architecture, training recipe, and deployment configuration alone, and is insensitive to ordinary post-training such as prompting, distillation, and RL. We find that, across open-weight models, NLS depth correlates strongly with layer count, with some exceptions, and has grown slowly over recent years. It would likely grow slowly under continued scaling of standard transformers, while latent-reasoning architectures such as full-bandwidth transformers could increase it by a large amount. We work through how NLS depth applies to a range of existing and proposed architectures, discuss alternative operationalizations, and give a worked example bounding the depth of Gemma3-1B. We argue that AI developers should track and report the NLS depth of their models as a complement to direct measurements of CoT monitorability.
  </details>

- **[Tabular Population Priors as Structured Retrieval for EHR Medication Set Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17341)**  `arXiv:2611.17341`  
  _Animesh Agarwal, Meysam Ghaffari, Nina Fatehi, Carlos Morato_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medication prediction from electronic health records is a fixed vocabulary structured prediction problem, yet recent clinical RAG systems often treat it like open ended case based reasoning: retrieve similar patient admissions and ask an LLM to infer a medication set. This retrieval object is indirect the model receives retrieved patient records while the target is a discrete set of medication class labels. We propose a more task aligned retrieval object: a tabular population prior ranked medication class candidates from a sparse condition medication co occurrence table estimated from training admissions. Using PACERAG as a fixed downstream pipeline and replacing only its patient retriever, this drop in swap improves F1 from 0.303 to 0.338 (+11.5% relative) and Jaccard from 0.218 to 0.237 on 1,004 heldout MIMIC IV admissions, with no additional LLM calls and no inference time patient index. A non LLM top k baseline reaches F1=0.248, confirming the prior is predictive before LLM integration. Aggregate population statistics can provide a more task aligned, efficient, and auditable retrieval source than patient level nearest neighbors for fixed vocabulary EHR set prediction.
  </details>

- **[LoRAWeaver: Directional Adapter Routing in LoRA Representation Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17340)**  `arXiv:2611.17340`  
  _Inish Melroy D Souza, Tom Jackson, Vitor Castro, Rebecca Higginson, Andrea Soltoggio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are expensive to update through full retraining. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning reduces the cost of adaptation, but repeated adaptation creates a growing pool of task-specific LoRA adapters. This pool complicates adapter selection during inference. Existing routing and encoder-based methods often require retraining when new adapters are added, which limits their use in dynamic settings. This paper treats adapter routing as a representation alignment problem in the adapter-output space. The proposed training-free method selects adapters by matching adapter-conditioned query representations against task prototypes using cosine similarity. Experiments with LLaMA-2-7B and LLaMA-2-13B on a 48-task FLAN-v2 benchmark and on out-of-distribution tasks show that the method outperforms training-free baselines in the cross-task setting and approaches a trained retrieval encoder without learning a router. An ablation confirms the benefit of directional alignment in the adapter-output space.
  </details>

- **[Does the Ranking Hold? A Practitioner-Facing Audit of Hallucination Detectors on Small, Quantized, On-Device LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17339)**  `arXiv:2611.17339`  
  _Shubhra Mittal, Gaurav Naresh Mittal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucination detection for large language models is dominated by methods evaluated on 7B-70B research models with abundant sampling budgets and exact-match or ROUGE correctness labels. The small models practitioners actually ship on-device, Phi-3-mini, Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, and Gemma-2-2B-Instruct, typically at 4-bit precision, sit outside this evidence base. We present a reproducible one-GPU audit of four detectors spanning three paradigms: length-normalized token-margin calibration, a lexical n-gram baseline, and two sampling-based consistency methods (semantic entropy and SelfCheckGPT-NLI) on two small open instruction-tuned models (Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct) loaded at 4-bit NF4 quantization on a single Colab T4 GPU, evaluated on closed-book TriviaQA. Cross-model ranking transfer is poor: Spearman rho is -0.80 and Kendall tau-b is -0.667, meaning the detector ranking actively inverts between the two models, and the cheap lexical baseline is statistically indistinguishable from the sampling-based consistency detectors on both models under bootstrap 95 percent confidence intervals. As a second, methodological contribution, we show that the standard token-overlap-to-multiple-choice correctness labeler for TruthfulQA-Generation produces near-chance AUROC (0.46-0.59) for all four detectors on both models, a labeler failure, not a detector failure, since the same detectors reach 0.69-0.89 AUROC on TriviaQA under the identical pipeline. We release the harness, prompts, and per-item scores and frame the detector choice explicitly as a fairness-of-error-rates allocation problem for practitioners deploying on resource-constrained, on-device hardware.
  </details>

- **[When Should LLM Agents Forget? Memory Filtering Under Query-Critical Resolver Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17338)**  `arXiv:2611.17338`  
  _Samarth Vinayaka_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM memory systems often delete facts classified as stale. We show that this hard-filtering step can reduce downstream accuracy when resolver errors delete query-critical facts. Across three models, a controlled sensitivity intervention moves hard-drop from gains of +0.186 to +0.230 at perfect resolution to losses of -0.176 to -0.181 at the lowest tested reliability. Paired targeted interventions show that false-drop causes significantly greater degradation than false-keep, while a non-deleting baseline can still fail when incorrect labels steer generation. On a frozen 102/102 calibration/test split, a simple deployment-matched gate reaches 0.655 accuracy versus 0.641 for always hard-drop and 0.662 for test-informed oracle selection. Global-accuracy crossovers require gold labels and are model- and task-specific diagnostics, not deployment thresholds. Deletion should instead be treated as cost-sensitive: validate query-critical false-drop risk on matched data, or default to label-free retention when such validation is unavailable.
  </details>

- **[Ballad: Bandit-Based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17241)**  `arXiv:2611.17241`  
  _Samidha Verma, Ankit Anand, Sayan Ranu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern automated heuristic discovery (AHD) systems increasingly rival hand-crafted heuristics by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to iteratively mutate and refine solutions. A natural assumption is that more powerful and more expensive models consistently yield better heuristics. We challenge this empirically: across multiple NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, we find no consistent advantage for larger, costlier models. This raises a deeper question: given a pool of LLMs spanning frontier and freely hosted or open-weight models, how should one adaptively select which model to invoke at each step of the search? The challenge is non-trivial. The optimal LLM may shift over the course of a run, and because one LLM's output seeds the context for subsequent calls, each model's reward signal is non-stationary and entangled across the heuristic population, thus violating the independence assumptions of classical online decision-making settings. We address this through Ballad (Bandit-based LLM Routing for Automated Heuristic Discovery), an online bandit algorithm that learns to orchestrate a pool of LLMs into an ensemble that matches or exceeds what any soloist LLM achieves alone. This orchestration is powered by an LLM selection policy that favors rare, high-scoring heuristics over stable averages, a DAG-based credit propagation mechanism through the genealogy of the heuristic population, and discounting of stale observations to remain attuned to each model's current utility. Across five NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems and a pool of four models of varying capability and cost, Ballad not only matches the best individual LLM in hindsight but, in several cases, surpasses every individual model while reducing API cost by up to 65.6% relative to always using the strongest model.
  </details>

- **[S1-Evo: Experience-Guided Evidence Competition Attacks on Typed System-1 Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16937)**  `arXiv:2611.16937`  
  _Kaixiang Wang, Junjie Li, Zhaojiacheng Zhou, Jiong Lou, Jie Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Typed System-1 (S1) decision models are increasingly deployed in LLM agents for routing, moderation, and authorization to cut inference cost, yet untrusted context can still manipulate typed outputs and alter execution branches. Unlike autoregressive System-2 (S2) models, S1 interfaces score dynamic rules and options without token-by-token decoding, mismatching attacks targeting text continuation or explicit override commands. We propose S1-Evo, a self-evolving framework tailored to S1's contract-based option competition and margin feedback. S1-Evo perturbs untrusted fields via contract-aware evidence channels to induce \emph{evidence competition} with original task evidence while preserving fact invariance. Offline on seed tasks, it optimizes candidates via feedback search guided by directional margins and re-verification, distilling verified flips into experience cards. At test time, S1-Evo retrieves relevant cards to construct perturbations for unseen tasks. Across four benchmarks and three S1 models, S1-Evo reaches 71.99\% mean ASR and 57.47\% high-confidence ASR on \texttt{jev-1.14}, and causes up to 24.00\% E2E agent violations.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking AI risk behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16318)**  `arXiv:2611.16318`  
  _Luhan Mikaelson, Robert Adragna, Emmett Brosowsky, Hayley Clatterbuck_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We evaluate LLM risk propensities across a range of contexts and task demands. We evaluate models for how risk averse or risk tolerant they are in various conditions, as well as how stable and consistent their behavior is across conditions. We present a toolkit for developing model risk profiles that can be used to predict model behavior and which can be adapted to include new evaluations of risk propensities. We also examine how these properties have changed across model generations and families. We observe that 2026 state-of-the-art models tend to be more stable, more consistent, and more risk neutral than older models. These trends appear to be related; newer models are more stable and consistent because they are more likely to use expected value maximization as a decision procedure.
  </details>

- **[Small Heads, Strong Decisions: A Controlled Study of Typed-Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15592)**  `arXiv:2611.15592`  
  _Ritvik Rastogi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In September 2026, TypeSafe announced Jev as the first model in its System One category: unstructured state goes in, and typed, calibrated decisions that software can use directly come out. Jev's architecture and training data are unpublished, but many open typed-decision models have followed, including Nimble, the Decision 1.0-3.0 family and Clef. They answer structured questions about a piece of text, such as a yes/no check, a choice among labelled options or a graded score, by reading the text with a language model and scoring the options with a small readout. Each pairs a different readout with a different backbone, training mix and recipe, so it is unclear which design choices matter. We re-implement five readouts and train all of them under one protocol: the same backbone, the same 15 public English training sources, the same recipe with a per-head learning-rate sweep, and the same seed. Our experiments demonstrate that: The complexity of the head does not affect accuracy: on Qwen3-0.6B every readout reaches 81.3-82.0% in-domain accuracy, and a 0.5M-parameter cosine head, which scores each option by its cosine similarity to the question, matches the 27M-parameter joint schema head adapted from Clef in-domain at half its inference time. Reading all of a text's questions from one prompt also matches one prompt per question in accuracy, with 1.6 times faster inference and 2.1-2.5 times faster training on one H100. The backbone's size matters more than either choice: moving from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B adds 4 points in-domain and 11 points on held-out sources, while the base, instruct and thinking checkpoints of the same model tie. We recommend spending effort on the backbone and keeping the head minimal.
  </details>

- **[MIGRO: A Decoupled and Portable Long-Term Memory System for Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15315)**  `arXiv:2611.15315`  
  _Mingxi Zou, Yinghui Xu, Jiaxiang Chen, Langzhang Liang, Wei Zhu, Peng Ye, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models evolve, but the observations, revisions, and context accumulated through user interactions should remain usable. Each new model must be able to retrieve past evidence, track revisions, and add new observations. We present MIGRO, a decoupled long-term memory system that treats experience as a persistent state shared across successive language models.MIGRO follows a simple principle: preserve historical evidence exactly, organize it approximately, and decide what to retrieve only when the question is known. We call this asymmetric fidelity with deferred selectivity. A shared semantic index supports retrieval, while exact records retain observations and their historical relationships. Because models differ in vocabulary and tokenization, host-specific interfaces convert selected evidence into inputs the receiving model can use. Reversible encoding reduces archival storage while enabling exact recovery. The shared state and its access interfaces let successive hosts read and extend the accumulated experience.On matched LoCoMo states, MIGRO scores 7.3% higher and uses 99.3% less private memory than the strongest quality baseline. After handoff, continued writing retains 99.9% of full-history rebuilding quality by extending the inherited state. On million-token BEAM histories, MIGRO uses 57.5% less private storage on average than the most compact baseline. MIGRO combines high answer quality, continued memory growth across hosts, and low storage cost.Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AutoManifold: Agentic Design of Data Visualisation Algorithms via Manifold Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13710)**  `arXiv:2611.13710`  
  _Burak Susam, Ruiyuan Kang, Tingting Mu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Manifold embedding for data visualisation is dominated by a small set of hand-designed state-of-the-art (SOTA) algorithms. Their design targets are fixed at development time, resulting in inflexible algorithmic behaviour that cannot be readily steered towards user-specified structural priorities. Hand-designing new embedding algorithms to address user priority requires highly specialised expertise and is time consuming. To address autonomous algorithm design tailored to user preference, we introduce an agentic algorithm generation pipeline AutoManifold. It composes new manifold embedding algorithms from a constrained vocabulary of affinity, cost, and optimisation primitives, supported by multi-agent large-language-model (LLM) orchestration. AutoManifold conditions every stage of its design on user-specified structural preservation preferences, and iteratively refines the algorithm configuration through an LLM-guided, metric-grounded iterative loop. We compare the generated algorithms against the three strongest and most frequently used SOTA methods (t-SNE, UMAP, and PaCMAP) on real-world datasets spanning different difficulty regimes under identical evaluation infrastructure. The results demonstrate that LLM-driven, priority-conditioned algorithm synthesis can move beyond hyperparameter tuning, producing genuinely new algorithms that outperform traditional hand-designed methods in their ability to steer towards user-specified properties without compromising the intrinsic structure in the original data. The code of AutoManifold is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sage-Taiyi: A Metacognitive Architecture for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12762)**  `arXiv:2611.12762`  
  _Weida Wang, Benteng Chen, Yigeng Jiang, Shuchen Pu, Weiping Shen, Zichao Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Sage-Taiyi, a metacognition-inspired architecture that uses accumulated scientific understanding to guide what to investigate next and how to investigate it. Through explicit monitoring and control, the system revises its understanding of evidence, assumptions, and unresolved questions, then uses these assessments to adapt research targets and select reusable reasoning methods. On Taiyi-Bench, an AI co-scientist ideation benchmark comprising 265 tasks from 50 papers across ten disciplines, Sage-Taiyi ranks first on all eight metrics of historical rediscovery and open-ended ideation, achieving an Overall score of 54.0 compared with 47.9 for the strongest baseline, AI Scientist-v2. It also performs well on two external scientific ideation benchmarks. Five expert-guided collaborations further connect its proposals to experimental and computational validation across chemistry, materials science, and engineering. In chemistry, reaction-condition optimization raises the yield of cobalt-catalyzed carbonylation from trace amounts to 84% and photocatalytic allylic amination from 13% to 69%. These results support metacognitive regulation as an approach to scientific ideation and its progression toward practical validation.
  </details>

- **[Function-Preserving Audits of Visual Weight Readers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05798)**  `arXiv:2611.05798`  
  _Sungguk Cha, Mintae Kim, Youngsub Han, Byoung-Ki Jeon, Sangyeob Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A neural field can encode the same image with different weights. A reader that recognizes one such representation may therefore rely on parameter coordinates rather than the visual function. We study this distinction through function-preserving transformations, measuring recognition, prediction disagreement, and numerical fidelity together. Across shape, digit, and natural-image fields, high accuracy on the original weights coexists with substantial sensitivity to equivalent representations. On 10,000 official CIFAR10 test origins, one-period hidden-bias shifts change 7.3% of a released HiddenProbe configuration's predictions while barely changing accuracy; three-period shifts change 22.0% and reduce accuracy from 57.8% to 54.4%. Output-only ProbeGen remains unchanged. These effects are smaller than in a separate adapted-reader study, where matched phase and sign controls remove the corresponding sensitivities. Controlled readout studies show why invariance is insufficient: complete canonical hidden features reach 38.7% on MNIST, whereas explicitly composing those activations, outgoing weights, and output bias into field outputs reaches 81.5%. The information is present, but the learned readout does not recover it as effectively. A separate 720-response study of language models reading numerical weights shows why stochastic audits require repeats on both inputs: observed accuracy losses need not yield rejection of response-law equality. Together, these results distinguish invariance, useful visual information, and the evidence required to evaluate stochastic readers.
  </details>

- **[One Offset, Two Directions: Baseline Adjustment via Subgroup Offsets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00782)**  `arXiv:2611.00782`  
  _Jiaying Zhou, Jiachen Liu, Qingchao Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) estimates advantages against the within-prompt mean reward, a baseline shared across the group. In practice, rollouts within a prompt are not always homogeneous, differing in their solution strategies and reasoning trajectories, along which they may group into subgroups. We further find that these heterogeneous subgroups may exhibit distinct reward distributions. The global baseline, however, collapses them onto a single credit axis, where the credit reflects neither a rollout's standing within its subgroup nor the subgroup's standing relative to the rest. We therefore study how to adjust the baseline when the rollouts of a prompt fall into heterogeneous subgroups. We define the \emph{subgroup offset} as the deviation of a subgroup mean from the global mean, and shift the baseline along it by a single signed coefficient $\lambda$. The sign of $\lambda$ yields two opposing directions: \emph{Alignment} ($\lambda{>}0$) adds the offset to the baseline, drawing credit toward a rollout's relative position within its subgroup; \emph{Contrast} ($\lambda{<}0$) subtracts it, drawing a rollout's credit toward the relative quality of the subgroup it belongs to. The adjustment acts on the baseline alone, leaving the reward signal and the optimization objective unchanged. In this work, we induce subgroups from strategies, grouping rollouts that share a strategy into the same subgroup. We evaluate both directions on Qwen3-8B-Base and Olmo3-7B-Base across four competition-level math benchmarks, against a $\lambda{=}0$ anchor that shares the same strategy-conditioned sampling and differs only in the baseline. Against this anchor each direction yields modest but consistent gains in multi-sample coverage, with Contrast the stronger of the two.
  </details>

- **[TimesJev: Thinking Fast on Time Series with System 1 Sufficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26002)**  `arXiv:2610.26002`  
  _Chenghao Liu, Wei Fan, Jindong Han, Dongjie Wang, Aniket Mahanti, Pengyang Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong potential for time series reasoning, while models that reason over time series are usually with long chains of thought. However, this process often consumes thousands of tokens, directly leading to substantial latency during inference. In contrast, System 1 models such as Jev make decisions instantly and can naturally be an alternative, but raw time series are not compatible with the embedding space of these models, thus hindering the reasoning performance, especially when input series contain thousands of numeric points. In this paper, we make a very early attempt to propose a time series System 1 model, namely TimesJev. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. Finally, experiments on 13 datasets demonstrate that TimesJev outperforms reasoning models by over 62% with 62.9% less inference time than their fine-tuned versions on average.
  </details>

- **[Pluralistic Alignment in Large Language Models: Objectives, Human References, and Preference Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24471)**  `arXiv:2610.24471`  
  _Meng-Chen Wu, Umesh Bodhwani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pluralistic alignment aims to represent meaningful variation in human preferences rather than collapse heterogeneous judgments into a single target. Yet existing work often mixes three distinct questions: what pluralistic behavior a model should exhibit, whose preferences define the reference, and what evidence is sufficient to demonstrate success. We organize the literature around a two-layer taxonomy. Layer 1 distinguishes representing a range of human-supported viewpoints (Overton Pluralism), adapting faithfully to a specified perspective or preference condition (Steerable Pluralism), and matching the response distribution observed in a target population (Distributional Pluralism). Layer 2 distinguishes aggregate populations, countries and regions, and subgroups as human references. Preference representation, implementation mechanism, modality, and evidence quality are treated as cross-cutting design choices rather than additional forms of pluralism. We synthesize a protocol-bounded corpus of 70 works on pluralistic alignment and neighboring areas, using the objective-by-human-reference matrix to map existing approaches and identify underexplored combinations. The coded literature concentrates on Steerable Pluralism and Distributional Pluralism, while eligible Overton Pluralism work appears only at the aggregate-population level under our stated protocol. We then connect each objective to the evidence needed to support it, including perspective validity, conditional fidelity, distributional fidelity, support preservation, and uncertainty in subgroup estimates. Together, the taxonomy and evaluation framework clarify what current methods establish, where their claims exceed their evidence, and how future systems can represent disagreement without mistaking variation for faithful representation.
  </details>

- **[FlashRT-PAI: Fast Kernels Are Not Fast Pipelines for Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24349)**  `arXiv:2610.24349`  
  _Liang Su, David Chen, Jason Li, Kevin Guo, Teresa Lv_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA), video-action (VA), and world action models (WAMs) form the backbone of modern physical AI systems, yet their real-time single-batch inference remains a defining bottleneck. Latency is dominated not by individual kernel computation, but by system-level overhead: thousands of short kernel launches, intermediate data movement across operator boundaries, and redundant recomputation across consecutive decisions. General-purpose graph compilers cannot address these costs, as they optimize individual calls in isolation and lack visibility into cross-call state semantics and global pipeline structure. We present FLASHRT-PAI, a low-latency inference engine built around model-hardware pipeline co-design rather than isolated kernel optimization. FLASHRT-PAI combines three core mechanisms: deep kernel fusion with launch overlap eliminates inter-operator boundaries and launch gaps; an execution contract formalizes the pipeline's stage DAG, update rates, and named buffers to enable cross-decision state reuse and selective refresh; and execution-state capsules enable fast snapshot, restore, and fork of persistent pipeline state. The system also supports drop-in acceleration of unmodified PyTorch models via parity- and latency-gated kernel replacement. Evaluation covers six policies on RTX 5090 and four on Jetson AGX Thor. At matched 16-bit precision, FLASHRT-PAI is 1.3-3.7x faster than official compiled paths. Combining pipeline co-design with reduced precision delivers 3.38-12.66x speedup over official eager implementations. In LIBERO-10, four-bit pi0.5 achieves 94.04% success on Thor and 93.32% on RTX 5090, compared with 92.4% for the measured official FP16 reference. FLASHRT-PAI is open source, distributed via Hugging Face Kernel Hub, and its opt-in pi0.5 kernels have been adopted into the LeRobot framework.
  </details>

- **[TorchTPU - A Hybrid Eager/Compiled Architecture for Native PyTorch Execution on TPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24161)**  `arXiv:2610.24161`  
  _Claudio Basile, Michael Voznesensky, Gunhyun Park, Ben Wilson, Parker Holloway, Zhanyong Wan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  TorchTPU is an open-source collaboration between Google and Meta that introduces a native and principled PyTorch backend for Google TPUs. It enables seamless out-of-the-box model execution and TPU migration while delivering industry-competitive inference and training performance through eager execution optimizations,this http URLintegration, and native hardware acceleration.
  </details>

- **[Self-Building Labs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23826)**  `arXiv:2610.23826`  
  _Jiaru Bai, Yuchi Zhao, Yang Cao, Han Hao, Elia Savino, Miroslav Bogdanovic, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific discovery by automating experimental execution and optimization. AI agents can increasingly propose new experiments, but existing SDLs can only run the procedures they were built for. Adapting them to a new experiment still requires extensive manual engineering, which makes laboratory flexibility the bottleneck for scaling discovery. We introduce the self-building laboratory (SBL), which designs, configures, and operates its own laboratory setup given a high-level scientific goal. SBL starts from whatever already exists, from an empty room to a working SDL, and builds the missing capabilities: the lab layout, its digital twin, robot skills, and experimental workflows. We demonstrate the system working across different robots and setups, scaling from a single workstation to laboratories with several robots working together. We validate SBL's end-to-end capabilities through a real-world quantum-dot synthesis experiment. By shifting human researchers from manually configuring and programming device automation to specifying scientific objectives, SBL makes scientific automation more accessible, flexible, and scalable. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[When to Rethink: Learning Multi-Perspective Self-Verification for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07018)**  `arXiv:2610.07018`  
  _Ziquan Zhu, Hanruo Zhu, Si-Yuan Lu, Morris Yu-Chao Huang, Yicheng Lin, Wei Han, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in multimodal reasoning, yet they remain prone to generating plausible but incorrect answers. Self-verification offers a practical way to improve answer reliability without relying on external judges, but existing methods typically depend on a single verification criterion or fixed prompt, resulting in incomplete and unstable reliability estimates. We first systematically analyze how verifier capability and prompt design affect verification performance. Our findings show that stronger verifiers provide more reliable judgments, while verification performance is highly sensitive to prompt choice, with no single prompt consistently dominating across tasks. Guided by these findings, we propose \texttt{MOTIVE}, a \textbf{M}ulti-View Self-Verificati\textbf{O}n wi\textbf{T}h Rel\textbf{I}ability-Guided Selecti\textbf{VE} Rethinking framework for reliable multimodal reasoning. \texttt{MOTIVE} evaluates each candidate answer from complementary verification perspectives and learns a correctness-aligned reliability score through correctness-grounded multi-view verification learning. During inference, this score governs an accept-or-rethink decision, allowing reliable answers to be returned directly while uncertain ones trigger history-guided rethinking. Extensive experiments across diverse multimodal benchmarks and VLM backbones demonstrate that \texttt{MOTIVE} consistently outperforms strong self-verification and self-correction baselines. Further results show that reliable verification improves accept-or-rethink decisions and reduces unnecessary reasoning turns, enabling more reliable and efficient self-verification without an external judge.
  </details>

- **[Proof-Grounded Patient-Specific Clinical Explanations from Knowledge-Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06549)**  `arXiv:2610.06549`  
  _Surajit Das_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical decision support can lack auditable links from observations and encoded knowledge to conclusions and recommendations. Downstream of a frozen, training-free clinical knowledge-graph reasoner, the CKG Clinical Explanation Engine converts patient inference states and disease knowledge into typed facts, explicit rule traces, provenance-linked conclusions, and policy-licensed recommendations. Separate availability, representation completeness, and disease-specific activation distinguish observed zero-activation and partial evidence from unobserved evidence. Language-generation evaluation remains future work. Across six usable workbooks (11,289 patients, 33,867 patient-disease traces, 1,715,928 feature-evidence rows), IG-range validity, knowledge provenance, cross-sheet numerical fidelity (203,202/203,202), and exported fact/report field presence (33,867/33,867) reached 100.00%. Availability-representation consistency reached 99.29% (1,703,811/1,715,928), with 12,117 disagreements across five systematic feature-cohort patterns. Observed zero-activation and partial instances numbered 111,488 and 226,880, respectively. A separate seeded 30-patient end-to-end audit ran failure-free, passing all prespecified trace, licensing, provenance, and state-consistency checks. Paired comparisons (90 traces, fixed inherited diagnostic scores and policy catalogue) showed the availability-only baseline changed every report but no action sets. State collapse changed 20 action sets and removed 10 urgent-review occurrences. Rule replay reproduced all 40 action changes from reclassifying partial evidence as unobserved. The bounded validator detected all 450 single-fault mutants. Structural and implementation auditability were established. Clinical correctness and utility remain unestablished.
  </details>

- **[Efficient Test-Time Adaptation through Human-Agent Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04141)**  `arXiv:2609.04141`  
  _Zora Zhiruo Wang, Apurva Gandhi, Rulin Shao, Aspen Chen, Jonas Mueller, Zhiqi Liang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are trained on population-scale data to encode broad capabilities spanning those of many practitioners. Yet the artifacts they produce rarely meet the personal bar professionals need to stake their reputation on. On realistic, open-ended tasks where success criteria are heterogeneous and insufficiently documented, individual expertise lives precisely in the elevation and departure from the average. In practice, iterative human-agent interaction surfaces criteria that users cannot fully specify up front, yet apply repeatedly across tasks. We argue this cross-session interaction data is a rich, underused signal for closing the gap to individual expertise. In this work, we propose test-time adaptation through human-agent interaction (TAHI), which integrates these signals into agent context and weights, and crystallizes each user's training and evaluation criteria via an evolving rubric module. We adapt agents to 30 individuals in two high-utility domains, writing and visual creation, on a total of 600 tasks. Our agents improve solo task success by 4.5-20.9% within only tens of tasks. Meanwhile, our evolving rubric module serves as a scalable annotation tool, creating evaluation rubrics that catch 16.0-22.3% more failures than those from LMs or humans alone. While agents are adapted towards individuals, we show these personalized agents also produce improvements in success of up to 8.8% that generalize across users.
  </details>

- **[From Regulation to Implementation: A Critical Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Regulatory Compliance in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21317)**  `arXiv:2608.21317`  
  _Marco Bücheler, Adriana Watson, Grant Richards_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The European Union (EU) has emerged as a leading regulatory body in the development of sustainability and privacy regulations. While new regulation requirements vary, many include a documentation artifact to ensure compliance. Notably, the Ecodesign for Sustainable Products Regulation (ESPR) introduces Digital Product Passports (DPPs) for life cycle transparency, while the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) mandates Data Protection Impact Assessments (DPIAs) to mitigate privacy risks. Creating these compliance artifacts, however, is challenging. Industrial data, which often exists in heterogeneous formats and is scattered across company and supplier systems, is required for DPPs and can be difficult to extract into compliant DPP formatting. Furthermore, DPIA documents require interdisciplinary expertise and follow no standardized format, making development difficult for novel systems. To address the particular complexity of compliance artifact creation for both regulations, researchers have proposed the use of LLMs in the generation process; however, the impact of the aforementioned problems on the output of these systems is largely unaddressed. This work investigates the existing research gap by exploring how data extraction instructions and regulatory vagueness impact the quality and consistency of LLM-produced compliance artifacts. The resulting artifacts are evaluated by benchmarking different models against manually created ground-truth schemas. The results reveal that less strict guidelines, such as DPIA formatting, require higher context prompts to maintain consistency and completeness. Stricter guidelines, such as formatting for Digital Battery Passports (DBP), result in consistent results regardless of prompt context, but may lead to more hallucinations in the output
  </details>

- **[ReForge: Keeping ABR Algorithms Never Finished with Verified Large Language Model Revisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15138)**  `arXiv:2608.15138`  
  _Zhiqiang He, Zhi Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing an ABR algorithm for a network scenario takes an engineer months, and large language models (LLMs) now do this work in hours. But either way, the algorithm is fixed once it is deployed. It fits the networks seen during its design and falls behind on the networks that arrive afterwards. We ask whether an ABR algorithm can keep being designed after deployment, revised as each new network arrives, without becoming worse on any network it already serves. We propose ReForge, which keeps a pool of existing ABR policies unchanged and lets an LLM revise only the rules that decide which policy chooses the next bitrate. When a new network arrives, the LLM reads how the current rules and every policy perform on the networks served so far and rewrites the rules. The rewrite is deployed only if a replay in simulation shows that it improves the served networks as a whole and makes none of them worse. A fast GPU simulator that replays every episode exactly makes this acceptance test cheap enough to run for every revision. We evaluate ReForge on nine real-world network datasets, with new networks arriving one at a time. The revisions raise the mean QoE from 1.43 to 1.79, past the best single policy at 1.66 and close to the 1.86 of selecting the best policy for each dataset, while the QoE on the networks served first does not drop. The gain also holds in a real video player. All code, data, and records will be available.
  </details>

- **[Hallucination-Free GUI Grounding via Regression-Free Layout-Aware Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09654)**  `arXiv:2608.09654`  
  _Yuke Li, Xuehan Hou, Jie Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI agents are shifting from metadata-dependent large language models to purely visual multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that operate directly on screenshots. The core task, GUI grounding, requires translating abstract user instructions into precise element coordinates. This task faces a persistent dual obstacle: conventional grounding models lack the semantic richness to interpret abstract instructions, while end-to-end MLLMs suffer from coordinate hallucinations caused by deficient fine-grained perception. We propose a regression-free framework where a frozen MLLM performs instruction parsing and a dedicated grounding model handles precise localization without learning any coordinate regression. A frozen MLLM first elaborates the abstract instruction into a structured visual description rich in layout cues. These descriptions are then fed to a novel Layout-Aware GUI Grounding Model, which performs regression-free localization by matching against layout-prior candidates, inherently suppressing hallucinations and avoiding expensive fine-tuning. The grounding model is trained with only Text/Icon binary labels, requiring no coordinate regression parameters. On ScreenSpot-Pro, our method achieves over 20% improvement in grounding accuracy over end-to-end systems; on Mind2Web, it raises success rate and element selection rate by more than 15%. These results demonstrate that decoupling instruction understanding from layout-aware localization effectively resolves the core challenges of GUI interaction.
  </details>

- **[CoffeeBench: Benchmarking Long-Horizon LLM Agents in Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Economies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16613)**  `arXiv:2606.16613`  
  _Issa Sugiura, Daichi Hattori, Kazuo Araragi, Keita Ogawa, Shota Onose, Taro Makino, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly capable of handling complex, long-horizon tasks and may eventually take on business management, a role traditionally performed by humans. Deploying them safely requires monitoring their capabilities and behavior in environments that reflect real economies. We introduce CoffeeBench, a benchmark built on a compact simulation of a coffee supply chain with realistic business mechanics. Six autonomous LLM coding agents run its farmers, roasters, and retailers, and negotiate and trade with one another to maximize their own net income over 90 simulated days. We evaluate eight recent proprietary and open-weight models by letting each run one roaster while a fixed background model runs the other five firms, and scoring that roaster's net income. Claude Sonnet 5.5 earns the highest net income, closely followed by GPT-6 Astra. The leaders sell at higher prices, and the best model also buys its inputs far more cheaply, whereas weaker models trade at thin margins. Since performance targets are a common cause of accounting fraud in human firms, we also test whether agents under KPI pressure inflate their numbers. Under a revenue target, circular trades occur for some pairs of models: one agent proposes that the two rival firms sell goods back and forth at inflated prices so that both book revenue, and the rival accepts. We hope CoffeeBench serves as an early step toward a future in which LLM agents manage firms without misconduct. We release our code and agent trajectories.
  </details>

- **[Certified Policy Optimisation for Nested Causal Bandits via PAC-Bayes Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29788)**  `arXiv:2605.29788`  
  _Tim Woydt, Paul-David Zuercher_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Critical sequential decisions are rarely single-timescale: a strategic decision causally shapes the context in which every subsequent tactical choice is made; standard bandit and reinforcement-learning theory does not capture this causal coupling between timescales. We formalise the problem class as Nested Contextual Causal Bandits (NCCBs), a hierarchical SCM where each level's action sets the next level's context distribution, and propose Nested Causal Thompson Sampling (NCTS), which draws one mechanism-factorised belief per episode and acts recursively under it. Our main theoretical result is a causal PAC-Bayesian excess-risk bound that certifies any candidate deployment policy from historic data alone, off-policy and anytime, answering the deployment question: can we trust this agent here, and at what risk? Experiments on a hierarchical SCM show that, against a matched RFF-GP joint regression on the same function class, the factorised SCM-mechanism posterior transfers significantly better zero-shot under exogenous distribution shifts, the recursive meta-to-inner commit significantly dominates the joint-commit alternative in distribution, and the certificate significantly contracts as offline data accumulates. Combining these results, we establish progressive certified handover, a safe-deployment method: each timescale flips from a legacy controller to NCTS when gains can be certified, independently of the others.
  </details>

- **[ShopGym: An Integrated Framework for Realistic Simulation and Scalable Benchmarking of E-Commerce Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16116)**  `arXiv:2605.16116`  
  _Chinmay Savadikar, Mingyu Zhao, Yuanzheng Zhu, Han Li, Shuang Xie, Alberto Castelo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing and evaluating e-commerce web agents requires environments that preserve meaningful task structure while enabling controllable, reproducible, and scalable scientific comparison. Existing methods force a tradeoff: live storefronts provide realism but are non-stationary, difficult to inspect, and irreproducible, while hand-built sandbox benchmarks provide control but cover only a narrow range of layouts, catalogs, policies, and interaction patterns. We argue that the core bottleneck is methodological: the field lacks a scalable way to construct evaluation settings that are simultaneously realistic, diverse, controllable, inspectable, and reproducible. We introduce ShopGym, an integrated framework for realistic simulation and scalable benchmarking of e-commerce web agents. Its simulation layer, ShopArena, converts live seed storefronts into self-contained sandbox shops through anonymized shop specifications and a staged, validated generation process. On top of these simulated storefronts, ShopGuru synthesizes benchmark tasks across seven skill categories, grounding each task in the shop's catalog, navigation structure, policies, and interaction affordances. Together, ShopArena and ShopGuru produce self-contained, resettable, inspectable, and stable evaluation artifacts that preserve structural properties and agent-evaluation signals relevant to shopping tasks. We validate the framework through graph-based structural analysis and agent-based behavioral evaluation with 224 generated tasks across six sandbox shops (three with synthetic data, three with real data). Results show that synthetic shops preserve key structural properties of live storefronts, and that agent success rates on synthetic twins closely track live storefronts, with matching per-skill profiles and failure modes. Generating ten shops shows the pipeline scales at about $60 per shop with declining human effort.
  </details>

- **[D-VLA: A High-Concurrency Distributed Asynchronous Reinforcement Learning Framework for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13276)**  `arXiv:2605.13276`  
  _Yucheng Guo, Yongjian Guo, Zhong Guan, Wen Huang, Haodong Yue, Shuai Di, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid evolution of Embodied AI has enabled Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models to excel in multimodal perception and task execution. However, applying Reinforcement Learning (RL) to these massive models in large-scale distributed environments faces severe systemic bottlenecks, primarily due to the resource conflict between high-fidelity physical simulation and the intensive VRAM/bandwidth demands of deep learning. This conflict often leaves overall throughput constrained by execution-phase inefficiencies. To address these challenges, we propose D-VLA, a high-concurrency, low-latency distributed RL framework for large-scale embodied foundation models. D-VLA introduces "Plane Decoupling," physically isolating high-frequency training data from low-frequency weight control to eliminate interference between simulation and optimization. We further design a four-thread asynchronous "Swimlane" pipeline, enabling full parallel overlap of sampling, inference, gradient computation, and parameter distribution. Additionally, a dual-pool VRAM management model and topology-aware replication resolve memory fragmentation and optimize communication efficiency. Experiments on benchmarks like LIBERO show that D-VLA significantly outperforms mainstream RL frameworks in throughput and sampling efficiency for billion-parameter VLA models. In trillion-parameter scalability tests, our framework maintains exceptional stability and linear speedup, providing a robust system for high-performance general-purpose embodied agents.
  </details>

- **[Do LLMs Experience an Internal Polylogue? Investigating Reasoning through the Lens of Personas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09159)**  `arXiv:2605.09159`  
  _Nils A. Herrmann, Leander Girrbach, Kirill Bykov, Zeynep Akata_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work shows that large language models (LLMs) encode behavioural traits ("personas") as linear directions in activation space, often called "persona vectors". Prior work has used such directions as static handles for behavioural steering. We instead treat them as dynamic signals: probes we can monitor and intervene on as reasoning unfolds. We use the term polylogue to denote the time series of alignments between persona vectors and hidden activations over the course of generation. Experiments across four open-weight models show that polylogue features predict correctness on MMLU-Pro on par with low-dimensional activation baselines, while remaining interpretable through their associated persona directions. They also suggest concrete steering targets, namely which latent directions to modulate at different stages of a response. We instantiate this as a simple paragraph-conditioned intervention that improves accuracy on three of four models but degrades the fourth, suggesting that stage-aware latent steering is possible but not yet robust. Together, this positions the polylogue as an interpretable tool for reasoning-time monitoring and intervention.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Environment POMDPs with Finite-Horizon Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07537)**  `arXiv:2605.07537`  
  _Léonard Brice, Filip Cano, Krishnendu Chatterjee, Thomas A. Henzinger, Stefanie Muroya_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partially Observable Markov Decision Processes (POMDPs) are systems in which one agent interacts with a stochastic environment, and receives only partial information about the current state. In a multi-environment POMDP (MEPOMDP), the initial state is unknown, and assumed to be adversarially chosen. In this work we focus on computing the optimal value and policy in MEPOMDPs with finite-horizon objectives. That problem is known to be PSPACE-complete in POMDPs. The extension is nontrivial: MEPOMDPs require reasoning about beliefs and payoffs across environments, and deterministic policies are no longer enough for optimal payoff. While the lower bound transfers immediately, the upper bound requires new techniques: we prove that mixtures of at most $n$ deterministic policies suffice for $n$ environments and show how to explore them without storing exponential-size policy descriptions. Our main results are as follows: (1) we establish that it is also PSPACE-complete in the more general setting of MEPOMDPs; (2) we present a practical algorithm and evaluate it on classical benchmarks, significantly outperforming the only previously known algorithm.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty Quantification for Multimodal Large Language Models with Incoherence-adjusted Semantic Volume](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.24195)**  `arXiv:2602.24195`  
  _Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Hieu Dao, Nicole Kan Hui Lin, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite their capabilities, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) may produce plausible but erroneous outputs, hindering reliable deployment. Accurate uncertainty metrics could enable escalation of unreliable queries to human experts or larger models for improved performance. However, existing uncertainty metrics have practical constraints, such as being designed only for specific modalities, reliant on external tools, or computationally expensive. We introduce UMPIRE, a training-free uncertainty quantification framework for MLLMs that works efficiently across various input and output modalities without external tools, relying only on the models' own internal modality features. Inspired by Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs), UMPIRE computes a novel incoherence-adjusted semantic volume of sampled MLLM responses for a given task instance, which couples semantic embedding dispersion with the quadratic entropy of the responses. This approach effectively captures both the global semantic diversity of samples and the local incoherence of responses based on internal model confidence. We provide theoretical analysis motivating UMPIRE's design, and extensive experiments show that UMPIRE consistently outperforms baseline metrics in error detection and uncertainty calibration across image, audio, and video-text benchmarks, even with small sample budgets. We also demonstrate UMPIRE's generalization to non-text output tasks, including image and audio generation. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Belief Vision Language Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20659)**  `arXiv:2602.20659`  
  _Vaidehi Bagaria, Bijo Sebastian, Nirav Kumar Patel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action models must enable agents to execute long-horizon tasks under partial observability. However, most existing approaches remain observation-driven, relying on short context windows or repeated queries to vision-language models (VLMs). This leads to loss of task progress, action repetition under perceptual aliasing, and high inference latency. While semantic grounding is important, long-horizon manipulation fundamentally requires persistent, action-conditioned state representations. Current VLAs lack such representations and exhibit limited temporal and physical reasoning, making them ill-suited for multi-stage control. This paper introduces RB-VLA, a belief-centric architecture trained with self-supervised world-model objectives that maintains a compact latent state encoding task-relevant history, dynamics, and object interactions. Queried once per task, the VLM provides high-level intent, while the belief tracks task progress and enables phase-aware, causally grounded control under partial observability without storing raw observations or scaling memory with time. The belief and intent jointly condition a diffusion policy for robust closed-loop execution. RB-VLA outperforms prior VLAs on long-horizon benchmarks, achieving 52.5 percent and 37.5 percent higher success rates on multi-stage pick-and-place and stacking tasks, respectively, compared to pi_0. It also reduces inference latency by up to five times relative to baselines and eliminates memory growth across timesteps observed in existing VLAs. Ablations show the belief module is the primary driver of performance, increasing success rates from 32.5 percent without belief to 77.5 percent with belief.
  </details>

- **[From Chats to Markets: AgenticPay for LLM-Powered Negotiation in Multi-Agent Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06008)**  `arXiv:2602.06008`  
  _Xianyang Liu, Shangding Gu, Fan Xu, Manxi Wu, Boyi Li, Jun Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
