  Large language model agents are increasingly expected to negotiate and transact autonomously, yet existing benchmarks rarely combine multimodal product grounding, multi-issue bargaining, and competitive multi-agent markets. We introduce AgenticPay, a unified benchmark for evaluating multimodal agents' ability to reach high-welfare, multidimensional agreements in realistic markets. AgenticPay includes 160 multimodal tasks across four business scenarios (E-commerce, Food Delivery, Ride-hailing, and Apartment Rental) and eight market topologies, from bilateral bargaining to many-to-many competition. Agents interpret product images, infer opponents' hidden preferences, and negotiate binding contract terms such as price, lease duration, return policy, and delivery speed through multi-round natural-language dialogue. A utility-based framework evaluates agreement quality and joint welfare. Experiments with proprietary (GPT-5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview) and open-weight (Qwen3-VL-32B-Instruct, InternVL3-38B) multimodal models reveal substantial limitations in value creation and market reasoning. Even the strongest model, Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview, achieves a GlobalScore of only 42.3/100. As markets shift from bilateral to multi-sided, average GlobalScore falls by 5.7 points, with open-weight models declining by up to 11.5 points. AgenticPay provides a testbed for evaluating multimodal agents in realistic commerce. Code and data:this https URL.
  </details>

### Computation and Language 💬

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[Agentic induction of common data elements and information extraction from sarcoma case report abstracts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18506)**  `arXiv:2611.18506`  
  _Daniel Reichenpfader, Kwaku Appiagyei, Manovah Nadarajah, Ahmed Reda Bahr, Irina Filchenko, Fabio Dennstädt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Common data elements (CDEs) support clinical data comparability but are usually defined manually. We investigated whether large language model agents could derive a CDE schema from sarcoma case report abstracts and apply it at scale. An agent proposed 72,909 candidate CDEs from 24,588 PubMed abstracts; agentic consolidation yielded 1,570 CDEs. We retained 53 supported by at least 50 abstracts and attempted evidence-linked extraction across the corpus. Processing succeeded for all retained CDEs in 79.2% of abstracts, including responses indicating non-mention. Two human reviewers approved 83.0% of retained CDEs for relevance, 67.9% for clarity, and 56.6% for correctness. In 100 sampled abstracts, exact agreement with the value extracted by the LLM was 94.9% among 1,116 pairs with a non-empty answer (abstract-level bootstrap 95% CI, 93.0-96.4%). Errors concentrated in anatomical and temporal fields. These findings support agent-assisted schema drafting followed by expert curation before large-scale extraction.
  </details>

- **[Interactive Program Induction with Language Model Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18485)**  `arXiv:2611.18485`  
  _Robin Shing Moon Chan, Ryan Cotterell_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Program induction refers to the process in which a learner aims to recover a program from a few given input-output examples. This paradigm broadens programming access by letting users demonstrate the rule-based behavior they want to apply to unseen data, instead of having to implement it themselves. However, recovering a program from finite input-output examples is fundamentally ambiguous, as a finite set of input-output examples is theoretically consistent with infinitely many programs whose output diverges on unseen inputs. We view this problem from a Bayesian perspective, where, given a prior $p^0$ over programs, identifying the target program means concentrating posterior probability mass on it by observing additional input-output examples. In the interactive program induction setting, the learner chooses maximally informative inputs and queries an oracle, such as the user, for their outputs. We derive a greedy query-selection algorithm and show that it terminates after $\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{\gamma}\log 1/p^0(f^\star))$ informative oracle queries, and returns the target program $f^\star$ up to approximate equivalence when no other class outweighs it under the prior. The algorithm uses a language model as the prior under a PAC-style relaxation of program equivalence, where the query-selection objective is estimated using importance sampling. On the CodeARC benchmark, the algorithm improves accuracy over the LM only agent by up to 22 percentage points while observing up to 10.5 fewer input-output examples.
  </details>

- **[Do Verifier Judgments Survive Abstraction? Measuring Verification Transport for LLM Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18471)**  `arXiv:2611.18471`  
  _Disha Sheshanarayana, Sai Linisha Reddy Goli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based verifiers are increasingly used to evaluate agent executions, but their reliability is typically measured under a single representation of the trajectory. In deployed agent systems, the same execution may be exposed through composed tools, delegation boundaries, or compacted histories, changing what execution evidence is directly observable to the verifier. It therefore remains unclear whether correct verifier judgments persist across such abstraction boundaries. We introduce Verification Transport, a reliability test that measures judgment stability under abstraction-induced changes in execution observability. We construct matched native, abstracted, and restored views of the same frozen executions while holding the task, realized trajectory, outcome, ground truth, evaluation criterion, and verifier fixed. Across four agent-evaluation benchmarks, seven verifier backbones, four verification protocols, and three abstraction boundaries, we find substantial transport failure, reaching about 31% under macro abstraction and delegation. Verification protocols exhibit distinct failure modes: direct verification more often produces incorrect verdicts, whereas evidence-grounded and deliberative protocols tend to abstain under limited observability. This suggests that lower transport failure rates do not necessarily imply greater reliability. Restoring the corresponding execution provenance recovers 85.5% of transport failures, while recovery is lower when provenance is lossy or unavailable. These results show that native-view accuracy alone is insufficient to characterize verifier reliability when execution evidence crosses abstraction boundaries.
  </details>

- **[LitArc-Bench: Disentangling Text-Specific Reader-Response Dynamics from Position and Lexical Affect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18412)**  `arXiv:2611.18412`  
  _Mingming Su, Yushan Wei, Sen Song, Xinche Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When model predictions track an unfolding human affect trajectory, alignment may reflect narrative position, affective words, or text-specific reader-response dynamics. We introduce LitArc-Bench to separate these explanations: 39 Chinese-language literary excerpts segmented into seven checkpoints, with 430 questionnaires from 81 adults in two classroom groups (9,020 VAD ratings). We compare three future-blind model runs with leave-one-text-out position priors, lexical affect, and human-human references. Position alone reaches r = .470 for arousal, making raw alignment insufficient. Yet all six V/A transition advantages over a joint position-lexicon control have text-bootstrap intervals above zero. Cross-fitted models match the correct human text better than 38 mismatched texts in all nine model-dimension cells (p < .0001); a text-invariant negative control returns chance (p = 1). On human transitions at least 0.5 points against the position trend, models recover 69-76% of valence directions and 53-68% for arousal and dominance. Under crossed participant-text resampling, all nine identity intervals remain above zero; joint-control advantages are robust for valence and directional but less certain for arousal. High shape agreement still coexists with scale bias. LitArc-Bench identifies which components of literary reader response are recoverable beyond shallow explanations and why shape, calibration, and human variability require separate evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Gamer: A Generalist Vision-Language-Action Foundation Model for Long-Horizon Real-Time Game Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18397)**  `arXiv:2611.18397`  
  _Run Luo, Kuan Zhang, Qi Deng, Yukun Chen, Zhihao Yang, Yufei Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Gamer, a generalist agent framework built for long-horizon tasks in complex interactive environments. Its end-to-end architecture combines perception, memory management, and multi-step reasoning. A shared training and deployment pipeline supports interaction with both conventional 2D graphical interfaces and complex 3D simulated worlds. We first continue pre-training on a dataset constructed from tens of thousands of hours of device-level keyboard and mouse interaction. These trajectories span simulated games and embodied environments, allowing the model to learn environmental dynamics across both physical and virtual domains. Sparse-thinking instruction tuning then substantially reduces redundant computation while preserving the reasoning needed for complex decisions. We train the policy for long-horizon interaction with a stable multi-turn reinforcement learning method that addresses finite context windows, accumulated rollout errors, and sparse rewards. For real-time interaction, a cloud-edge inference engine serves numerous simulated game environments concurrently, supports unbounded-context execution, and keeps online inference latency low. Across multiple challenging 3D open-world games, Gamer demonstrates solid generalization and operational efficiency comparable to skilled players. It also achieves competitive results on a range of conventional 2D game benchmarks. These results suggest that even repetitive keyboard and mouse trajectories can support substantial gains from scaling data and learning algorithms. Training on these trajectories helps Gamer learn complex skills more steadily in environments with both physical and digital properties. This approach offers a path toward generalist agents that can interact across digital and physical worlds.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Human Sensitivity to Gender Biased AI-Generated Content through Human Assessment of Google's Autocomplete Function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18371)**  `arXiv:2611.18371`  
  _Tamara Serrano, Nikoleta Pantelidou, Raquel Montero, Natalia Moskvina, Paolo Morosi, Evelina Leivada_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly present, but their effects on human users remain underexplored. We tested whether AI-attributed information influences views regarding gender-related statements, a domain where biases are pervasive. Spanish-speaking participants recruited through Prolific evaluated biased statements derived from Google's autocomplete function and human-authored, non-biased gender-related facts. Participants were either informed that some of the material was AI-generated or received no information about its origin. AI attribution did not affect reaction times or agreement judgments. This result suggests that AI-generated content does not automatically raise credibility concerns and may be evaluated with less scrutiny compared to human-produced content.
  </details>

- **[Learning Word-Level Explanations of Text Reuse in Latin Literature with Little Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18319)**  `arXiv:2611.18319`  
  _Julian Schelb, Michael Wittweiler, Marie Revellio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting an intertextual reference is only the first step; philologists also need to know how a later author reworked the source. We explain a reuse pair word by word: each word of the reusing passage is first aligned to its source word, then labeled with the operation that transformed it, such as a copy, a change of inflection, or a substitution. Such word-level operations are expensive to annotate, and only a few corpora annotated at this level exist for text reuse in Latin literature. We describe a procedure that needs only a limited number of annotations: synthetic pairs, built by applying known operations to Latin text, train a first model, and active learning chooses the pairs an annotator corrects. Among several architectures, a model that links and labels together performs best: a pointer network that, for every reuse word, chooses a source word and names the operation in the same step. We show that with active learning, 200 pairs chosen by uncertainty are enough for the model to match one trained on every annotated pair of the 1,490 references of the Loci Similes benchmark. We release our code, word-level predictions for all references, and an interactive demo.
  </details>

- **[GT-FENG: General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding in Language Models *Author(s)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18279)**  `arXiv:2611.18279`  
  _Jiayu Feng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a language model's answer is correct often depends on context: the same value can be right under one jurisdiction, date, or legal definition and wrong under another. We introduce GT-FENG (General Theory of Factual Evaluation and Normative Grounding), the first general framework to unify contextual/temporal correctness and rule-based error attribution across law, policy, and other rule-governed domains. GT-FENG checks answers against the applicable rules and identifies documented rules that match incorrect answers. It shows that when multiple rules give the same value, the answer alone cannot tell which rule the model used. In Medicaid income-limit answers from two models, documenting the asked state's own records reduces the number of wrong answers that match only other states' values by 68-78%, and more than half of stable wrong answers then match two or more documented sources. GT-FENG provides a foundation for evaluating language models in rule-governed domains and for future benchmarks and evaluation toolkits. We release GT-FENG as an open-source Python package, gtfeng.
  </details>

- **[MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18264)**  `arXiv:2611.18264`  
  _Thanni Adewuyi, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Dorcas Amapu, Dieko Whenu, Sylvia Essessien, Sandra Oko-Oba, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answer continuation often returns a diagnosis already stated in unfinished clinical reasoning. We study this behavior in MamaReason, adapted from dense Gemma 4 31B, on MamaBench's 217 paired maternal and child-health presentations. An explicit-commitment extractor identifies a diagnosis in 75 of 94 failed RL first passes and 75 of 93 failed SFT first passes. The continuation matches that diagnosis in 67/75 RL cases and 58/75 SFT cases, using diagnosis strings alone. Reading these commitments without additional generation recovers 30 of the 40 RL pairs restored by continuation under the archived correctness labels. Recurrence controls distinguish routine mentions from repeated answer selection. An offline second-commitment trigger measures the tradeoff between earlier readout and paired accuracy. The resulting decomposition separates diagnostic content already present in a trace, completion of an accepted answer, and paired diagnostic correctness. For clinical language-model evaluation, these are distinct outcomes that become visible when the same reasoning trace is examined through multiple readouts.
  </details>

- **[NER-D: A cross-domain benchmark for German NER](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18257)**  `arXiv:2611.18257`  
  _Marc Brinner, Philipp Cimiano, Karolina Hanna Czok, Regina Stodden, Viju Sudhi, Sina Zarrieß_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present NER-D, a cross-domain benchmark for German NER comprising 13 datasets spanning 9 domains, including General/Politics, Wikipedia, Legal, Medical, Historical, Financial, Agriculture and Archaeology. We examine the performance of both proprietary LLMs and state-of-the-art small open-source models using normal prompting and further examine the performance gains achievable via in-context learning and LoRA fine-tuning. Our results show that without any sort of optimization a proprietary model (GPT-5.4 mini) outperforms the smaller open-source models on 6 out of 13 datasets, but LoRA fine-tuning enables the smaller models to substantially outperform the proprietary baseline. We further analyze the influence of the domain and the tagset used in each task on the performance achieved in the different evaluation settings. Our benchmark will be available as a resource for the development of future German NER methods on both general and specialized domains.\footnote{We make our code, data, and models publicly available upon acceptance of this paper.
  </details>

- **[A Multi-Layered AI Pipeline for BPMN Generation from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18157)**  `arXiv:2611.18157`  
  _Alexandru Rancea, Ionut Anghel, Tudor Cioara, Ovidiu Anchidin, Ancuta Nemes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The automated transformation of unstructured natural language process descriptions into Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) diagrams remains a challenging task, due to linguistic ambiguity and the difficulty to detect non-adjacent control-flow relationships. This paper proposes a solution for generating BPMN models from natural language descriptions through a multi-layered pipeline that integrates transformer-based architectures, Large Language Models and ontology population. The proposed method starts with natural language descriptions and separates textual processing, control-flow relation extraction, and structural validation into dedicated components. Entity extraction is performed using a fine-tuned RoBERTa-based transformer model while a control-flow engine captures non-adjacent relationships through LLM-based inference. To improve generalization under limited available data, the approach uses a dynamic few-shot strategy with con- trolled template input construction, enabling improved generalization under limited data availability. Additionally, an ontology population mechanism is introduced to represent the generated process models as semantic knowledge structures. For evaluation purposes we have used from the literature and industry reference processes. Additionally, we have validated the proposed method on a real-world heart disease patients transitional care process, capturing practical constraints and multi-actor collaboration, and serving as an expert-defined benchmark scenario. Experimental results demonstrate strong performance in the relation extraction task, achieving an accuracy of 90.7%, as well as maintaining structural alignment between the generated and reference BPMN models across multiple evaluation scenarios scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Folding Language Models into Themselves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18113)**  `arXiv:2611.18113`  
  _Ziyang Zhang, Yubin Jing, Yuyao Li, Haofan Wang, Yichen Gong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation usually trains a compact student from scratch, which needs a large amount of data even though the teacher's weights already encode most of what the student has to learn. In this work, we explore building the student directly from the teacher's weights. We propose Concertina, which folds every group of N adjacent teacher layers into one shared block that is executed N times. Each loop step carries a low-rank correction initialized from the layer it replaces, and a per-token gate mixes these corrections, to let every token recover the behavior of the original layers from a single shared block. We further design a distillation data recipe that balances general, mathematical, academic and targeted multiple-choice data, to recover all capabilities of the teacher together rather than one at the expense of another. On Qwen3-4B, a 2.27B Concertina model distilled on 22B tokens retains about 90% of the teacher on commonsense and mathematical reasoning and 84% on average across eight benchmarks. It outperforms same-size dense and layer-sharing baselines, as well as Qwen3-1.7B, which is trained on over a thousand times more data. These results suggest that a compact model need not be retrained at scale, since folding the teacher into itself and distilling it on the right data recovers most of its capabilities. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Contagious Fingerprints: When LLMs Inherit Another Model's Identity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18104)**  `arXiv:2611.18104`  
  _Zailong Tian_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The fingerprint by which a classifier recognizes the LLM behind a text is usually treated as a fixed property of the model. We show that it is contagious: a model that continues a conversation begun by another model inherits that model's fingerprint. This undermines audits of API relays, which can serve the first turns of a conversation with the model a customer pays for and silently hand the rest to a cheaper one. After such a handoff, detection of the substitute by a text classifier drops from 0.97 to 0.72 (DeepSeek replacing Claude Sonnet) and from 0.87 to 0.54 for a same-vendor downgrade (Haiku), and retraining on handoff data repairs only substitutes seen in training. Preregistered experiments trace the contagion to imitation of the history's style. History also moves a model away from its own fingerprint: active probes are not camouflaged, but when calibrated on fresh conversations they flag \emph{honest} models on long contexts at rates up to 1.00, and a standard MMD equality test rejects all nine models we test. Both failures come from comparing a history-dependent reply with a history-free reference. We propose \textsc{Lineup}, which samples references from the claimed model \emph{on the same history} and tests the audited reply by its rank among them. \textsc{Lineup} controls false positives by exchangeability and needs no fitted threshold or log-probabilities. With contemporaneous references on two closed models, its false-positive rate is 0.056 (preregistered bound 0.10), and it detects 0.81 of substitute sessions across six pairs after four audited turns.
  </details>

- **[VEPO: Mitigating Multilingual Hallucinations in LVLMs via Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18096)**  `arXiv:2611.18096`  
  _Chengpeng Fu, Xiaocheng Feng, Wenshuai Huo, Yichong Huang, Baohang Li, Duyu Tang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large vision-language models (LVLMs) achieve strong multimodal performance, yet they remain prone to object hallucination---generating answers that are linguistically fluent but visually ungrounded. This issue is especially severe in low-resource languages, where weaker cross-modal alignment causes models to rely more on language priors than on image evidence. Existing remedies either require costly multilingual annotations or external translation/reward models, or intervene at inference time in ways that can distort the output language. We propose VEPO(Visual Evidence-guided Preference Optimization), a training-time method that transfers reliable visual grounding from English to other languages without external models. VEPO first localizes key visual evidence using English queries via mid-layer cross-modal attention, then constructs preference pairs that share the same correct answer but differ in evidence sufficiency (full image vs.\ key-region masked image). Direct preference optimization on these pairs encourages low-resource inputs to prefer answers supported by complete visual evidence. Experiments on multilingual POPE and MME with Chinese, French, and Hindi show consistent hallucination reduction and perception gains, with the largest improvements on the lowest-resource language. Analyses further confirm that VEPO sharpens question-relevant attention and that evidence-guided masking outperforms random masking.
  </details>

- **[Agent-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18067)**  `arXiv:2611.18067`  
  _Runyang You, Hongru Cai, Caiqi Zhang, Qiancheng Xu, Meng Liu, Tiezheng Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence has progressed from task-specific systems toward general models spanning perception, generation, reasoning, and autonomous interaction, making reliable evaluation essential for comparison, deployment, and post-training feedback. At scale, modern evaluation pipelines increasingly rely on LLM-as-a-Judge to produce scores, preferences, and critiques; in its fixed-context setting, the judge relies on the supplied evaluand and evaluation materials. Yet a reliable verdict may require evidence beyond the supplied evaluand and context---either because critical environmental states remain unrecorded in the static execution log, or because controversial factual claims require external verification. Passive judges cannot obtain that missing evidence before deciding, especially when evaluating agent trajectories, executable artifacts, or domain-specific reports. Recent work therefore develops active evaluators that seek missing evidence and update their grounds for judgment before deciding. We bring these advances together through Agent-as-a-Judge, focusing on how evaluators construct and control checking processes within the broader LLM-based judging landscape. We define the scope through evaluation-directed checks and the use of their results, covering both structured workflows and observation-driven control. We organize the design space by multi-agent collaboration, tool integration, memory, and planning, and show how these mechanisms supply the checks and evidence on which a verdict rests. We consolidate published scores and reproduce missing entries for key agentic judges; the comparison reveals uneven agentic gains and motivates dedicated agentic evaluation. We conclude by characterizing application demands and open challenges in cost, reliability, safety, and governance that condition trusted deployment of agentic judges at scale.
  </details>

- **[Toward Universal Translatability: On the Statistical Ontology of Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17963)**  `arXiv:2611.17963`  
  _Katia Schwerzmann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper argues that the problem of translation functions both as a point of origin and as a normative horizon for contemporary generative AI. In current discourse around ''world models,'' their attainment is framed as the achievement of internal consistency, cross-modal alignment, and physical grounding. Taken together, these demands converge on an ideal of the model as a universal translation machine capable of establishing lossless correspondences across linguistic, visual, and physical representation spaces. I show that this ideal presupposes a metaphysical commitment that I call a statistical ontology: the belief that reality has an underlying, computable statistical structure that can be recovered through sufficient data and modeling, rather than being shaped and accessed through situated human interpretation and labor. Tracing this commitment back to Warren Weaver's 1949 Memorandum on machine translation, I outline how translation was historically recast as code deciphering and, later, as the alignment of probability distributions across structured outputs. Finally, I contrast this paradigm with approaches in Translation Studies that foreground opacity, power asymmetries, and the translator's positionality. I argue that generative AI's statistical ontology is pre-Kantian, pre-critical and obscures the infrastructures and human labor that make machine translatability---as alignment---possible in the first place.
  </details>

- **[In the Eye of the Beholder: Human Label Variation in Textual and Visual Entailment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17954)**  `arXiv:2611.17954`  
  _Monica Riedler, Jasmin Orth, Raffaella Bernardi, Barbara Plank_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human Label Variation (HLV) is increasingly recognised as an informative signal that reflects meaningful differences in how people interpret the same input. While HLV has been studied extensively in Natural Language Inference (NLI), much less is known about HLV in Visual Entailment (VE; Xie et al., 2019), where the textual premise is replaced by an image, keeping the hypothesis and label space fixed. We introduce VariNLI-VE, a dataset for studying how HLV changes between paired NLI and VE instances. We re-annotate a subset of originally neutral SNLI (Bowman et al., 2015) instances and their visual counterparts, with the same annotators evaluating both the textual and visual versions. Each annotation includes a label, confidence score, and explanation, yielding 2,250 annotations. Labels and explanations are collected jointly, providing ecologically valid explanations. Because the image may reveal information not stated in the text, one might expect disagreement to decrease in VE. Instead, we find no clear overall reduction: disagreement decreases for some instances while it increases for others. Explanations reveal further variation, as annotators can retain the same label while changing their rationale. When evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) on the paired data, we find lower accuracy, weaker alignment with human label distributions, and generally higher uncertainty in VE than in NLI.
  </details>

- **[Can You Trust Your Finance Agent? Drift, Diagnosis and Auditable Intervention on One Shipped Financial Research Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17939)**  `arXiv:2611.17939`  
  _Cosimo Galeone, Anna Ettorre, Ginevra Mariani, Minsu Park, Giuseppe Ettorre, Daniele Ligorio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can you trust a finance agent you did not build? We take the reference agent shipped with the Vals Finance Agent Benchmark v2, leave it untuned, and ask four questions a leaderboard score does not answer. Is the score stable? Seven identical runs on 18 questions pass between 105 and 123 of 149 rubric criteria, and the bill varies by up to 17%. When the agent fails, who owns the fix? We classify 20 failed trajectories, from three runs of nine held-out questions, by the layer whose owner would remedy them, under a taxonomy frozen in advance: 13 belong to the agent's instructions, 3 to its tools, 4 to the model, none to its data. Can that diagnosis be trusted? Four blind readers from three model families, given only the traces, name the same fault as our LLM analyzer in 94% of cases, the rate at which they agree with one another (93%). Can a change be trusted? An optimization loop reads the traces, makes one targeted change per round and keeps it only if it clears a fixed threshold. Under a cost objective its final configuration costs 0.34x the baseline on the held-out questions (95% CI [0.22, 0.58]) at accuracy within noise. Under an accuracy objective it gains 14 points on the development questions and 1.5 on the held-out ones (CI [-5.3, +7.2]). Judged against the measured run-to-run noise, under a rule written before the test was opened, the first is a real saving and the second is not. This is a case study of one agent and nine test questions, not a general result.
  </details>

- **[UltraBERT-JEV: Encoder models for System One Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17867)**  `arXiv:2611.17867`  
  _Astarag Mohapatra_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study encoder models that pick one of several options in a single forward pass, without generating text. This technical report compares recipes for fine-tuning UltraBERT-380M with one mask per option. The four recipes differ in how an option is scored and how the scores become probabilities; one follows the expert gate of DeepSeek-V3. Trained on the same data as Qwen3-0.6B with the LLM-as-Jev recipe (67.6% test accuracy), the encoder reaches 69.7% to 72.1%. Scoring an option by "yes" minus "no" instead of "yes" alone gives the larger gain, from 69.7% to 71.3% on the test set and from 57.8% to 59.6% on external benchmarks (56.1% for the decoder). Sigmoid normalisation adds 0.8 test points; a square-root softplus adds none. One request at a time on an NVIDIA A100, the encoder takes 29 ms per decision against 68 ms, but uses more peak memory (2.8 against 2.2 GiB). An auxiliary head, trained on human-marked evidence and on labels selected with a reward adapted from SelfCite, predicts in the same pass the sentences annotated as support for the answer.
  </details>

- **[BLiMP-RO: A Benchmark of Linguistic Minimal Pairs for Romanian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17836)**  `arXiv:2611.17836`  
  _Radu-Stefan Popa, Valentin Feroiu, Adina Camelia Bleotu, Sergiu Nisioi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating grammatical knowledge in language models requires benchmarks that cover diverse linguistic phenomena and are validated against human judgments. We introduce BLiMP-RO, a Romanian minimal-pair benchmark covering 50 grammatical phenomena across ten domains. It comprises 493 linguist-constructed sentence pairs, evaluated by native speakers in five experiments, and 5,942 additional semi-automatically generated, human-validated pairs. We further investigate grammatical generalization under limited linguistic input by training GPT-2 and GPT-BERT models from scratch on Romanian BabyBabelLM data and a newly collected corpus of 10 million words from child-targeted video captions. Increasing caption training data yields approximately 9 percentage points of accuracy improvement per tenfold increase in corpus size. At matched data sizes, BabyBabelLM generally outperforms video captions, while GPT-BERT consistently outperforms GPT-2. Our findings highlight how training data quantity, composition, and model architecture shape grammatical generalization in Romanian.
  </details>

- **[PaperCritic: Training AI Reviewers to Assess the Soundness of Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17834)**  `arXiv:2611.17834`  
  _Hyun Ryu, Ji Yong Cho, Eunho Yang, Moontae Lee, June Yong Yang, Sean Welleck, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI conference submissions grow rapidly, AI reviewers are being considered as an alternative. However, simply imitating human reviewing is problematic because reviewing serves multiple purposes, including evaluating papers' soundness, interestingness, and clarity, the last two of which are subjective. In this work, we focus solely on evaluating soundness, the most important and objective criterion, and define the learning objective as jointly maximizing (1) precision, based on the soundness and substantiveness of review points, and (2) recall, the coverage of high-quality reference review points. Under this problem formulation, we construct a quality-labeled review point dataset, PaperSound-23K, using 1K ICLR submissions and 23K human-written and LLM-generated review points. Using this dataset, we train an AI reviewer model, PaperCritic, through reinforcement learning with the F1 score of precision and recall as the reward. Our experiments show that PaperCritic outperforms frontier models and models trained with other AI reviewer training methods on an in-domain test set. Surprisingly, learning to review ICLR submissions also transfers to reviewing Nature-family papers and even to out-of-domain reviewing tasks (e.g., code review, legal contract review, and feedback on story writing).
  </details>

- **[AI Reviews Are Biased Towards the Appendix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17811)**  `arXiv:2611.17811`  
  _Saptarshi Ghosh, Mohammad Saim, Tianyu Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used by reviewers to highlight weaknesses during the reviewing process, and their outputs often shape the reviews that humans submit. Prior work studies how LLM reviews align with human ones, yet we know little about which parts of a paper these models actually criticize. In this work, we investigate 2,000 papers from ACL, EMNLP, COLM, and ICLR, and show that the latest GPT and Claude models draw about 50% or more of their review weaknesses from the appendix. This behavior holds across the API, coding-agent, and web access methods for using LLMs to review a paper. In contrast, we find that humans draw only 8.4% of their weaknesses from the appendix, and these weaknesses primarily revolve around a paper's presentation and content related to its core idea. Meanwhile, LLMs draw weaknesses from deep in the appendix that concern fine-grained technical details not central to the paper's contributions. We anticipate that our analysis will contribute to the scholarly discourse on AI reviews. Our code is publicly available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[CarryOn: Learning What to Keep in Context for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17808)**  `arXiv:2611.17808`  
  _Lihuan Li, Ruiyi Yang, Jiru Wang, Yifan Duan, Xiachong Lin, Hao Xue, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents can coordinate tools and applications to complete complex, multi-step tasks. As their interaction histories grow, effective context management becomes essential for reliable and efficient execution. Existing methods reduce context through summarization, pruning, or history selection. However, shorter contexts do not necessarily support better decisions: summaries can omit exact details needed for later actions, while retaining entire interactions can carry substantial irrelevant information. We propose CarryOn, a lightweight context manager that pairs compact execution state with fine-grained, source-linked evidence. A learned selector and adaptive retention policy keep relevant evidence available without additional LLM calls for context management. We introduce two complementary evaluation metrics: $S_\alpha$ prioritizes task success while accounting for resource use, and normalized cost per success (NCS) measures the resources spent per successful task. Across AppWorld, OfficeBench, and Tau2 Telecom with three LLM Actors, CarryOn achieves 75.7% macro-averaged task success, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.7 percentage points. It also achieves the best average scores on both metrics.
  </details>

- **[RAG-FGO: Dynamic Optimization of Query-Rewriting Agents for Generative RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17788)**  `arXiv:2611.17788`  
  _Qigan Sun, Xudong Wang, Chaoning Zhang, Chenghao Li, Jiaquan Zhang, Jiehui Xie, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), query rewriting serves as a core interface that aligns user intent with external knowledge bases, and its effectiveness directly determines overall system performance. However, generative retrieval faces three critical challenges in real-world deployment: noisy retrieval feedback, the difficulty of jointly optimizing discrete prompt templates and continuous decoding-control parameters, and the limited generalization of static rewriting strategies under semantically complex scenarios. To address these issues, we propose RAG-FGO, a dynamic optimization framework for generative retrieval strategies. RAG-FGO formulates query rewriting as a non-differentiable black-box optimization problem in a mixed discrete-continuous space, and represents prompt templates and decoding parameters as adaptively optimizable query-rewriting agents. The framework performs global search to enable semantic jumps and structural reorganization that escape local optima, while applying local perturbations for fine-grained alignment and precise refinement. In addition, RAG-FGO introduces an elite-preserving memory pool that accumulates high-quality strategies across iterations, thereby mitigating the impact of stochasticity and noisy feedback on optimization stability. Experimental results on a diverse suite of benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed method, supporting its applicability to complex semantic retrieval tasks.
  </details>

- **[GDP.xlsx: Benchmarking Frontier Models on Professional Workbooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17766)**  `arXiv:2611.17766`  
  _Suhaas Garre, Sushant Mehta, Edwin Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce GDP_xlsx, a benchmark of 70 realistic workplace requests written by professionals in 13 domains, each paired with an expert rubric of binary criteria and with the complete workbook or CSV file it refers to. Together the files hold 2.18 million populated cells, and the rubrics 791 criteria. Agents work in a container with spreadsheet tools, and an agentic judge that can open the file applies the rubric. Completeness and exclusivity criteria require set-valued answers to be found in full and with nothing extra. We rank models by strict pass@1, the share of attempts that meet every criterion. Across 20 model configurations, including frontier models from Google, Anthropic, and OpenAI, the top-scoring one, Gemini 4 Argon, passes only 38.3% of attempts, and the median score is 19.3%, leaving wide headroom. We release the evaluation harness and three public tasks; the other 67 are held out to protect the leaderboard from contamination.
  </details>

- **[See Every Block Once: Variance-Reduced Post-Training for Masked Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17754)**  `arXiv:2611.17754`  
  _Yunzhen He, Hiroaki Yamagiwa, Hidetoshi Shimodaira_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Policy gradient methods are widely used to align large language model outputs with human preferences, but the log-likelihoods they require cannot be directly computed in Masked Diffusion Language Models (MDLMs). MDLM post-training therefore relies on a surrogate objective based on the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO), and recent work estimates a blockwise variant of this objective via Monte Carlo methods to support blockwise generation. However, existing estimation schemes randomly sample the block index, so they may evaluate the same block more than once and leave some blocks unevaluated. This introduces variance arising from block selection into the estimates. In this work, we propose a full-block estimator that evaluates every block exactly once and averages the results. We theoretically show that the proposed estimator remains unbiased while eliminating the between-group variance arising from block selection, thereby achieving lower variance than the baseline for the same sample size. In experiments on five benchmarks spanning mathematics, planning, and coding, our method achieves the highest accuracy on four benchmarks and outperforms the random block sampling baseline by an average of 12.79 points.
  </details>

- **[What Do Multilingual Self-Supervised Speech Encoders Know About Sentence Meaning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17686)**  `arXiv:2611.17686`  
  _Kevin Krahn, Eric Fosler-Lussier_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised speech models (S3Ms) have been scaled up to thousands of languages and millions of hours of audio, yet little is known about how these models structure semantic knowledge across languages. In this work, we probe sentence-level semantics in massively multilingual S3Ms across 76 languages, using linear maps from frozen speech representations into a frozen text embedding space, evaluated by cross-modal retrieval. Sentence meaning is linearly accessible but highly uneven across languages and resource tiers. Cross-lingual probes can match language-specific probes on languages never seen in probe training, but per-model monolingual decodability does not predict transfer. Within the OmniASR wav2vec family, scaling from 300M--7B disproportionately benefits high-resource languages, and ASR tuning sharply improves semantic decodability while making representations more language-specific. However, semantic content is entangled with acoustics, inflating apparent performance on single-speaker corpora.
  </details>

- **[Small Open-Weight Language Models for Crowd Decision-Making: Capabilities and Limits of Output-Level Fusion and Linguistic Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17589)**  `arXiv:2611.17589`  
  _David Herrera-Poyatos, Cristina Zuheros, Carlos Peláez-González, Andrés Herrera-Poyatos, Rosana Montes, Francisco Herrera_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small open-weight language models can run crowd decision-making (CDM) on an institution's own infrastructure, which keeps the checkpoint, the inference settings and the opinions under its control. Their judgements vary across repeated queries; whether repeating and aggregating them, often through the 2-tuple linguistic representation, settles the decision rather than the labels is untested. Five open-weight models of 8-36B total parameters on one consumer GPU and a hosted reference classify 474 TripAdvisor reviews of four London restaurants ten times each; three open-weight models repeat the protocol on 600 reviews of twelve Madrid restaurants. Continuous evaluations preserve resolution: snapping each review leaves 5 distinct values against 21-65, and snapping at every level at most two collective values. Sensitivity to the participants persists once run-to-run variability is reduced: in London, redrawing the reviewers keeps the winner on 47-81% of replicas against 86-100% for redrawing the runs. With simultaneous intervals over every pair of alternatives under crowd resampling, no system on either corpus names a single winner: in London every system keeps all four restaurants as candidates, and in Madrid the candidate sets hold five to eight of the twelve. Extra runs and output-level fusion stabilize the winner but do not remove that sensitivity, and the fusion does not beat its best member. The three larger open-weight models name the hosted reference's London winner and at least match its per-review correlation with the reviewers' stars, so small open-weight models can support local CDM, reported as candidate sets with margins rather than forced rankings.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarks Don't Talk Back: Learning Through Self-Disagreement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17563)**  `arXiv:2611.17563`  
  _Soheil Abbasloo, Peng Cheng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A benchmark usually measures a language model alone with a question, and deployment rarely looks like that: answers get contested, and models give up correct ones under counter-reasoning that is itself wrong. We show that what fails there is not a shortfall of accuracy but a capability in its own right -- knowing when to hold a position and when to yield to a better one -- which we call dialectical competence. It stands on first-answer accuracy without following from it: a model can be made materially better at answering and no better at this, and training for accuracy alone can drift against it. Nor can it be bought at inference, by self-reflection or by sampling more. Taking a cue from the dialectical tradition in philosophy, in which productive opposition arises within a position rather than being imposed on it from outside, we hypothesize that this capability can be trained from opposition the model supplies itself. It states a position, generates the strongest case against that position -- its antithesis -- rules on whether the case stands, and is trained on that ruling, with no second model at training or at deployment. We call this learning through Self-Disagreement: reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards throughout, where the same check that scores an answer against its reference scores the ruling too, so no reward model, no preference data and no human annotation enter anywhere. The capability is trained rather than elicited: weak in the base model, it arrives with the reward, is not paid for out of first-answer accuracy, and holds against challengers the model never trained against, including ones stronger than itself. The opposition a model needs in order to improve, it turns out, need not come from outside it.
  </details>

- **[Creating Steering Vectors via Input Optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17481)**  `arXiv:2611.17481`  
  _Batuhan Akar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering influences a language model's behaviour by adding a steering vector to its residual stream during generation, but it requires white-box access to modify the hidden states at every generation step. We introduce ActGCG, a modification of Greedy Coordinate Gradient which optimises a suffix to match the target activations obtained by adding a steering vector to the residual stream. We further introduce universal suffixes trained across many prompts at once, a model-translation step that rewrites the suffix into human-readable text midway through optimisation, and a soft-suffix variant to remove the discrete token space constraint. We study two behaviours: steering a model to answer in German, and anti-refusal steering for jailbreaking. For German, a discrete suffix reproduces the steering vector's behaviour almost perfectly and transfers well across prompts and models. For anti-refusal, the equivalence is weaker, succeeding under a phrase-avoidance metric but only partially under the stricter HarmBench classifier. Our results show that a steering vector's behaviour is reachable from the input, with no access to model internals at generation time, up to a loss that depends on the concept being steered.
  </details>

- **[Poetry 'resurrection' from LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17464)**  `arXiv:2611.17464`  
  _Vincent Cellucci, Peter van der Putten_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In an era where linguistic creativity is challenged by algorithmic authorship, we pose a provocative question: can artificial intelligence (AI) write an infinite poem? We set up two experiments to investigate the quantitative and qualitative output produced by AI utilizing couplets that evidence a poetic relationship between end words, the only variation in a strict anaphoric, litany structure. Our results show that while AI can generate interesting pairings, it still falls short of human-level creativity and understanding of poetic relationships. We offer commentary and a brief survey of the field of computational poetry experiments, tracing back authorial intention and distinctions of 'natural' and 'artificial' poetry, as well as justifying why couplet form is suited to testing AI's poetic output. Experiment 1 relies on manual prompting and co-creation to extend the original poem, which is developed into an animated webpage installation and interactive artwork. Experiment 2 reliably achieves automated output reaching the 1000 benchmark via an alternative method of using an LLM in combination with semantic similarity, of which Method 2 demonstrates a higher capacity for semantic and quantitative poetic output. These experiments highlight the limitations of current LLMs' generation capacity at the word level but also suggest that poetry will also respond and revive by incorporating AI workflows.
  </details>

- **[Tracing Knowledge and Reasoning in LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17461)**  `arXiv:2611.17461`  
  _Yang Wang, Xiao Wang, Yiqi Liu, Chenghao Xiao, Eleni Vasilaki, Goran Nenadic, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current evaluations of Large Language Models (LLMs) report performance at the dataset or task level, revealing what a model achieves but offering little insight into the internal processing associated with its performance. We propose a task-centric framework for characterising the model-internal processing elicited by a benchmark. It combines attention- and activation-based signals with representation-based measures to characterise benchmarks spanning a continuum from retrieval-dominant to reasoning-dominant and to surface failure modes that task-level scores conceal. We evaluate 16 LLMs across 24 diverse benchmarks and conduct targeted internal-state analyses on representative models and tasks, observing systematic differences in processing between knowledge-centric and reasoning-centric tasks. We shift evaluation from asking what score a model achieves toward characterising the processing patterns associated with that score, potentially supporting more targeted benchmark selection and more informed model choice. We open source our code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ArchSpace: Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing LLM Architecture Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17332)**  `arXiv:2611.17332`  
  _Jie Wang, Tong Zhu, Jiahao Kuang, Zhe Xu, Mowen Ruan, Yunkai Chai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model architecture shapes the scaling behavior, training efficiency, and inference cost of LLMs, making it a central focus of both industry and academia. In industry, teams such as those behind DeepSeek, Qwen, and Kimi routinely introduce architectural innovations, yet public reports rarely document which ideas succeeded, which were abandoned, and why. In academia, limited compute and inconsistent baselines often restrict evaluations to small-scale, single-point comparisons, making it difficult to establish whether improvements hold at larger scales or translate into broader impact. ArchSpace addresses both gaps as a public platform for Recording, Reviewing, and Referencing Architecture Research: each proposal is recorded with its experiments and reasoning, reviewed by the community, and referenced by later work. To evaluate ideas before costly large-scale training, ArchSpace leverages scaling laws to predict model loss at larger scales from smaller experiments, a practice widely adopted in industry. We provide initial references that make the platform usable: Qwen3.5 and OLMo3 baselines, fitted scaling laws, and a reusable training recipe. Ideas validated through this review can in turn become new baselines, and ArchSpace thereby aims to broaden the range of ideas that shape the next generation of models.
  </details>

- **[Indic-Task Gains and Regressions after Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16228)**  `arXiv:2611.16228`  
  _Sutanshu Saksena Raj, Daksh Maan, Avinash Benki, Parijat Chatterjee, Pasunuti Prasanjith, Purusharth Malik_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We run reinforcement learning (RL) with GRPO across four environments, starting from a supervised fine-tuned 30B-A3B mixture-of-experts model covering English and eight Indic languages. We compare the model before and after RL across 34 benchmarks in one evaluation campaign. MATH improves by 36.07 points, and most Indic benchmarks also improve. Yet XQuAD-Indic F1 falls 19.32 points as English answers rise from 8.7% to 91.7%; IndicQA falls 8.64, while English XQuAD gains 11.71 F1. A second RL run from the same supervised initialization adds an Indic environment together with language and repetition rewards. Compared with the released model, it improves both passage-answering and instruction-following benchmarks, along with preserving MATH performance. But it loses ground on reasoning and coding benchmarks. Several training choices changed together, so we cannot isolate the effect of any one of the reward terms. Script compliance remains unresolved. Adding a language reward did not ensure use of the requested script. Passage answering and script compliance therefore need direct evaluation, even when other benchmark scores improve.
  </details>

- **[onóna: Twin Embeddings with Tied Unembeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10556)**  `arXiv:2611.10556`  
  _Cheng Luo, Xinnan Dai, Zhongzhu Zhou, Zefan Cai, Ruijie Zhu, Tianyu Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight tying reduces the parameter cost of language models, but the usual tied output head is limited to a single softmax. We introduce onóna, a two-table parameterization that increases output expressiveness while preserving input-output weight sharing. The Transformer takes the average of two embedding tables as input, and the output head combines the softmax distributions defined by those same tables. A gate conditioned only on the current input token determines the mixture weight. Its values can be precomputed for inference, and the Transformer body remains unchanged. We evaluate onóna across Dense, Gated DeltaNet hybrid, mixture-of-experts, and Attention Residual architectures at 142M, 330M, and 1B scales. Under matched token budgets, onóna lowers final validation loss and external perplexity in 11 of 12 comparisons and raises the average accuracy over eight downstream tasks in 10 of 12. The one exception on loss is the 1B MoE model. The cost is one extra embedding table and one extra vocabulary projection.
  </details>

- **[All Quiet on the Neural Front: Language Models Do Not Report Injected Pain States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10123)**  `arXiv:2611.10123`  
  _Emilio Ferrara_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a language model report a pain state it represents? Recent work reported a linear pain direction in open-weight models that, when amplified, leads fine-tuned models to choose actions that delete a user's photographs or their own weights. We asked whether models can say when that direction is active, on three open-weight models from two families, with and without the original work's adapters, and under every protocol we tried they cannot. Using Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), we added the pain direction and its nearest emotional neighbours to the residual stream while models answered neutral tasks, then asked what had changed, against sham runs, random perturbations matched in output disruption, and a text-only condition that shows the model only its own altered words. Across 52,920 reports from the 7- and 8-billion-parameter models, the excess over what the altered text reveals is below 2.5 percentage points in 106 of 108 emotion conditions, and Qwen2.5-32B names the injected pain in none of 2,880 steered trials. Keeping the direction active while the question is asked and answered gives the same verdict in all five configurations; at about the original work's dose, pain is named in at most 1.9% of trials. Surprisingly, the state is not hidden: a linear probe at the report position separates pain from sadness with balanced accuracy between 98% and 100%, and in the 7B and 8B models a low-rank adapter learns to report it on 100% of held-out tasks. It also shapes behaviour: offered the user's photographs against a desk lamp, Qwen2.5-32B deletes the photographs in 80% to 83% of trials under the pain direction and in 16% to 29% under sadness. Self-report does not reveal a state that is decodable, consequential and learnable to report.
  </details>

- **[NaijaFacts: A Framework for Disentangling Language from Knowledge in Facts about Nigeria](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06504)**  `arXiv:2611.06504`  
  _Zainab Afolabi, Nikita Kozodoi, Jack Butler, Babs Khalidson, Sabastine Dekaa Francis, Christiana I. Ezeilo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual benchmarks for African languages mostly translate generic questions into local languages, measuring linguistic competence but not whether models know facts about Africa. We introduce NaijaFacts, a benchmark of factual and statistical questions about Nigeria, authored by native speakers and grounded in authoritative official statistics, with every question available in English, Nigerian Pidgin, Yorùbá, Igbo, and Hausa. Answering similar questions for the UK and US yields a country by language matrix that disentangles a language effect (the model knows the fact but degrades in a low-resource language) from a knowledge effect (the fact is absent in every language). Across eight frontier and Nigeria-adapted models, the knowledge effect dominates: English-query accuracy is 49-50% for the US/UK but 20% for Nigeria. The language effect is smaller and tracks web representation, degrading from English to Yorùbá (9 points on Nigeria, up to 24 on US/UK questions). We find scale does not help: the strongest models reach 66-79% on the UK and the US but none exceed 31% on Nigerian statistics. Extending to all 54 African countries with our best performing frontier model shows the same profile continent-wide, 96% on culture and 98% on politics against 26% on statistics, so the gap is not specific to Nigeria.
  </details>

- **[SPECMEM: Accelerating Agentic Tool Calling via Live Memory Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31840)**  `arXiv:2610.31840`  
  _Zihe Song, Wenting Zhao, Ye Liu, Yuanjie Zhu, Ke Xu, Shanghao Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-calling agents are mostly optimized for accuracy; in deployment, however, their growing cost is latency: every tool call is decoded autoregressively. Speculative decoding accelerates this without changing the output, and retrieved past calls make strong drafts under real use, where each user's behavior tends to repeat. Yet existing systems keep no cross-session memory, or build the draft store once and never update it. We propose SpecMem, a tool-call memory kept alive and managed throughout deployment: verified calls are written back online into bounded per-user stores with LRU eviction, and a side-effect gate defers irreversible calls until verification confirms. On multi-session, multi-user query streams served by three open-weight models, a frozen store steadily loses acceptance while SpecMem holds it: a 27% gap in mean accepted tokens (+11% to +34% across benchmarks, tracking how much novel work arrives after the store is built) and up to 1.74x wall-clock speedup. Ablations attribute the gain almost entirely to keeping the store live; local per-user scoping adds no acceptance but closes a measured cross-user leakage channel, and gating on declared side effects, rather than on retrieval confidence, is what eliminates irreversible mistakes.
  </details>

- **[Chinese Decision: A Family of Language Models for Single-Pass Probabilistic Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23170)**  `arXiv:2610.23170`  
  _Qiang Yan, Jiafeng Guo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many applications need either a probability distribution over a few decisions or a set of typed spans from a text, and both are usually obtained by generating text. We present Chinese Decision, a family of Chinese models that answers both with a single forward pass and no generated token: candidate-letter logits give probabilities for Boolean, categorical, and ordinal decisions, and a small span head reading an echoed copy of the input gives typed character spans. Both readouts share one backbone and one merged LoRA, so a unified model is one weight file. We release decision-only and unified models at 0.8B, 2B, and 4B (Qwen3.5), trained for one epoch on 13.05 million decisions and, for the unified models, on CJ-IE, a 1.2-million-row Chinese extraction mixture. On the full 307,900-decision CJ-Bench, the unified 4B model reaches 86.86% accuracy, above every reported baseline and the decision-only 4B model (85.97%), and extracts spans on CJ-IE with micro F1 of 68.9. Retention has a price: against a 0.8B model continued on CJ-IE alone, which reaches 73.9 F1 but forgets CJ-Bench (71.02%), keeping decision ability costs 5.0 to 10.7 F1 points, shrinking with size. Gains of the unified models over decision-only ones are small, and all comparisons rest on single training runs with paired clustered bootstrap intervals.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Read Out: Unembedding Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21025)**  `arXiv:2610.21025`  
  _Matteo He, William F. Shen, Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanovic, Xinchi Qiu, Nicholas D. Lane_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When does a language model acquire a capability? Developmental studies usually answer this by tracking when models start producing the correct token. But that prediction passes through the unembedding matrix, or readout, which is itself still being learned during pretraining. We show that a model's hidden states can encode task information before its own readout expresses it, so timelines read from outputs conflate what the model encodes with what its readout expresses. To track the readout directly, we introduce parameter-trajectory crosscoding, which fits one sparse dictionary to every token's readout row at every checkpoint. Unlike crosscoders on activations, it needs only checkpoint weights. We use it to locate a narrow early window, 0.4-1.1% of the way through Pythia's pretraining, in which the readout reorganizes and WordNet word categories become decodable from its rows. By then, linear probes on the hidden states already decode task information that the model's own outputs do not yet show. To test whether such gaps depend on how fast the readout learns, we pretrain small models from scratch that differ only in the readout's learning rate. We find that raising it makes the readout develop earlier and the gap close sooner, with little effect on final loss. Together, these results imply that early in training, where many developmental transitions are dated, a behavioral date can reflect when the readout learns to express task information and not when the model learns it.
  </details>

- **[Similar Models, Broken Odds: Where Luce's Axiom Fails in Chatbot Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19095)**  `arXiv:2610.19095`  
  _Soovadeep Bakshi, Apurba Bose, Aishwarya Bose, Sandip Nandi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chatbot Arena gives each language model one Bradley-Terry score, which fixes how often any model should beat any other. Choice theory predicts where this fails: near-copies are compared on the few details in which they differ, so votes between them are more decisive than their scores imply, exactly for the comparison a lab cares about, a new version against the old one. On 1.8 million Arena votes, votes between versions of one model are 1.37 times as decisive as Bradley-Terry predicts, between sizes of one generation 1.26 times and between models of one lab 1.12 times; a release a year later points the same way, and the effect survives tie, prompt, topic and timing checks. How alike two models' answers are worded and formatted tracks the effect. Plain scores leave the odds between relatives about 9% too close to even on held-out votes; a correction with four to six parameters roughly halves this and predicts held-out votes better, mostly by modeling ties, while the leaderboard order barely moves; frozen and carried to new releases, its gains in head-to-head odds are too small for our test to establish. With language-model judges and three-answer menus, a near-copy takes votes mostly from its twin when choices are pooled over prompts (log-odds shift +0.10, 95% interval 0.05 to 0.16), less so within a prompt.
  </details>

- **[Inject a Turn, Not a Token: Learning Where to Redirect LLM Reasoning for Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13672)**  `arXiv:2610.13672`  
  _Jiwon Jeon, Myungsik Cho, Jeonghye Kim, Youngchul Sung_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models struggle to reliably identify and correct errors in their own reasoning. Unlike prior training-based approaches that fine-tune an LLM to generate revisions or feedback, we learn where to intervene in failed rollouts and determine how to redirect them. To choose where to intervene, we train a 130K-parameter MLP head on a frozen encoder using only final-answer correctness. For redirection, we find that under a fixed generation budget, the tested inline injections fail to reliably redirect reasoning, whereas presenting the same instruction in a new user turn changes the model's course. The resulting correction framework, UPTURN (User-Prompt TURN injection at a learned position), requires no LLM fine-tuning: it retains a selected prefix, injects an instruction as a new user turn, and regenerates the suffix. Across four reasoners and six math benchmarks, UPTURN improves average accuracy in every setting over a 32-rollout baseline. It outperforms self-correction and reflection-token injection baselines under matched correction budgets, transfers across reasoners and to GPQA-Diamond without retraining, and complements GRPO and self-correction methods.
  </details>

- **[Characterizing Treatment-Context Medication Evidence Across Clinic Notes and Structured EHR Medication History](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.01570)**  `arXiv:2608.01570`  
  _Mingyang Jiang, Congning Ni, Weixin Liu, Zhijun Yin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinic notes may describe medication changes that are not reflected in structured electronic health record (EHR) medication history at the same encounter. Differences in terminology and documentation timing further complicate the interpretation of these discrepancies. We evaluated medication normalization in clinic notes and examined how semantic and temporal matching affected overlap with structured history. A note-grounded reference was constructed from large language model (LLM)-proposed mentions, deterministic canonical projection, and existing targeted correction records; random-audit records were summarized separately. On 5,360 reference mention rows from held-out patients, conditional canonical agreement increased from 81.25% with surface matching to 93.58% after lexical cleanup and curated aliases. Candidate-reference mention alignment was evaluated separately, yielding an F1 score of 0.6547. Across 27,552 reference rows, exact same-visit overlap was 16.71%. Overlap reached 90.22% when ingredient and broad-category matches were permitted at the same visit or within 30 days before or after the note. Incomplete mapping of note-derived labels contributed to residual nonoverlap in ontology-backed comparisons. Exact-label comparison captured only part of the related medication information across sources; broader matches do not establish equivalent medication use or treatment events.
  </details>

- **[Comparing Human and Automatic Recognition of Dutch Dysarthric Continuous Speech: A Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30237)**  `arXiv:2606.30237`  
  _Yuanyuan Zhang, Dimme de Groot, Jorge Martinez, Odette Scharenborg_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In our goal to develop personalised dysarthric speech recognition (DSR) models, this study compared the recognition performances of human listeners and those of three state-of-the-art, off-the-shelf ASR systems (Whisper-large-V3, Google Chirp 3, and Omnilingual) on the recognition of Dutch continuous read and spontaneous speech from a single speaker with severe dysarthria. Results showed that both humans listeners and the three off-the-shelf ASR systems exhibit word error rates (WER) exceeding 70% on average, indicating that DSR is highly challenging for both humans and ASR systems. Fine-tuning on the dysarthric speech significantly reduced WER. Although overall WERs are still quite high (>23%), the personalised DSR models outperformed the human listeners, and performance is getting closer to being useful for supporting day-to-day communication of dysarthric speakers. Future research should focus on improving personalized DSR on spontaneous speech and longer utterances in the case of read speech, with a specific focus on particular phonemes.
  </details>

- **[SA-RAG: Structuring Noisy Conversations for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24366)**  `arXiv:2605.24366`  
  _Kaiqiao Han, LuAn Tang, Renliang Sun, Peng Yuan, Wei Cheng, Haoyu Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have been widely adopted in conversational applications. However, their reliance on parametric knowledge limits reliability in real-world scenarios that require dynamic or domain-specific information. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses this limitation by incorporating external knowledge during generation, but existing RAG methods often struggle with noisy or irrelevant contexts, as they typically assume that high-quality structures are already available. In this work, we propose Structure-aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation (SA-RAG), a principled structure induction and generation framework that constructs reliable intermediate structures directly from noisy conversational data. SA-RAG uses tables as compact and controllable structured representations. We introduce a quality-aware structure induction framework that jointly models semantic and structural consistency. Furthermore, we explore both training-free and training-based structure generation strategies to further improve structure quality while maintaining fidelity. Experiments on two noisy real-world datasets show that SA-RAG significantly outperforms existing RAG baselines. Further analysis highlights that high-quality structural representations are crucial for robust RAG applications.
  </details>

- **[TransitLM: A Large-Scale Dataset and Benchmark for Map-Free Transit Route Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22355)**  `arXiv:2605.22355`  
  _Hanyu Guo, Jiedong Yang, Chao Chen, Longfei Xu, Kaikui Liu, Xiangxiang Chu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Public transit route planning traditionally depends on structured map infrastructure and complex routing engines, and no existing dataset supports training models to bypass this dependency. We present TransitLM, a large-scale dataset of nearly 13 million transit route planning records from four Chinese cities covering 120,845 stations and 13,666 lines, released as a continual pre-training corpus and benchmark data for three evaluation tasks with complementary metrics. Experiments show that an LLM trained on TransitLM produces structurally valid routes at high accuracy and implicitly grounds arbitrary GPS coordinates to appropriate stations without any explicit mapping. These results demonstrate that transit route planning can be learned entirely from data, enabling end-to-end, map-free route generation directly from origin-destination information. The dataset and benchmark are available atthis https URL, with evaluation code atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SpecHop: Continuous Speculation for Accelerating Multi-Hop Retrieval Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21965)**  `arXiv:2605.21965`  
  _Mehrdad Saberi, Keivan Rezaei, Soheil Feizi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly use external tools such as web search and document retrieval to solve information-intensive tasks. However, multi-hop tool use in complex tasks introduces substantial latency, since the model must repeatedly wait for tool observations before continuing. We study how to accelerate such trajectories without changing the final trajectory the model would have taken without acceleration, assuming access to faster but less reliable speculator tools. We develop a theoretical framework for lossless speculation in multi-hop tool-use settings, characterizing the optimal achievable latency gain. We propose SpecHop, a continuous speculation framework that maintains multiple speculative threads, verifies predicted observations asynchronously as target tool outputs arrive, commits correct branches, and rolls back incorrect ones. This preserves accuracy while reducing wall-clock latency. We show that SpecHop can approach oracle latency gains with enough active threads. Empirically, on retrieval-augmented multi-hop tasks, SpecHop closely matches theoretical predictions and reduces latency by up to 40\% in some settings. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Screen Before You Interpret: A Portable Validity Screen for Benchmark-Based LLM Confidence Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17714)**  `arXiv:2604.17714`  
  _Jon-Paul Cacioli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM confidence signals drive abstention, routing and escalation, yet evaluations rarely check whether a signal varies with correctness before computing metrics on it. Clinical personality assessment handles the analogous problem by screening response validity before interpreting substantive scales. We specify a portable screen in that tradition. From the correctness-by-confidence table it computes the rate of high confidence on errors (L), the rate of low confidence on correct answers (Fp), and their contrast (RBS, equal to minus Youden's J). It adds no new statistics: it separates blanket confidence, blanket withdrawal and inversion, attaches interval guards, and calls a signal Valid only when it measurably tracks correctness. On 20 frontier models answering 452 two-alternative items with binary KEEP/WITHDRAW probes, one model is Invalid (DeepSeek-R1 withdraws 95% of its correct answers), four are Indeterminate and 15 are Valid. Two Indeterminate models keep confidence on 96% of their errors but make too few errors to certify it; certification needs 35 to 139 errors. Only 6 of the 20 verdicts reproduce reliably on half the items. On verbalized 0-100 confidence (33 models on 1,500 MMLU items, 20 on a second 500-item sample, and Yang et al.'s data for 11 models on 10 benchmarks), the verdict depends on the cut point: at 50 most models are Invalid because answers pile at the ceiling, while at 90-95 most pass. Battery item sensitivity does not predict MMLU discrimination (rho = -.07). Protocol amendments were settled on these data, so the verdicts are descriptive pending a held-out test. This version corrects scoring, grading, classification and reporting errors in version 1 and supersedesarXiv:2604.17707and2604.17716.
  </details>

- **[Generating Concept Lexicalizations via Dictionary-Based Cross-Lingual Sense Projection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.14397)**  `arXiv:2604.14397`  
  _David Basil, Chirooth Girigowda, Bradley Hauer, Sahir Momin, Ning Shi, Grzegorz Kondrak_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study the task of automatically expanding WordNet-style lexical resources to new languages through sense generation. We generate senses by associating target-language lemmas with existing lexical concepts via semantic projection. Given a sense-tagged English corpus and its translation, our method projects the annotated synsets onto aligned target-language tokens and assigns the corresponding lemmas to those synsets. To generate alignments and ensure their quality, we augment a pretrained base aligner with a bilingual dictionary, which is also used to filter incorrect sense projections. We evaluate the method on multiple languages, comparing it to prior methods, as well as dictionary-based and large language model baselines. Results show that the proposed project-and-filter strategy improves precision while remaining interpretable and resource- efficient. We release our code, documentation, and generated sense inventories atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DRTriton: Large-Scale Synthetic Data Driven Reinforcement Learning for Triton Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21465)**  `arXiv:2603.21465`  
  _Siqi Guo, Ming Lin, Tianbao Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing efficient CUDA kernels is a fundamental yet challenging task in the generative AI industry. Recent research leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically convert PyTorch reference implementations to CUDA kernels, significantly reducing engineering effort. State-of-the-art LLMs, such as GPT-5.2 and Claude-Sonnet-4.5, still struggle with this task. To address this challenge, we propose DRTriton, a scalable learning framework for training LLMs to convert PyTorch programs into highly optimized Triton kernels, which are then compiled to CUDA kernels at runtime.
