- **[Electrocardiographic Imaging via Posterior Sampling under Operator Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17851)**  `arXiv:2611.17851`  
  _Ricarda Buttmann, Andreas M. Lehrmann_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electrocardiographic imaging (ECGI) reconstructs heart-surface potentials from body-surface potentials, an ill-posed inverse problem. Diffusion posterior samplers combine a learned prior with an explicit forward operator that they assume to be accurate. In ECGI this operator is only an estimate, because the body changes with respiration and posture, and body composition such as subcutaneous fat is rarely known for an individual patient. The resulting operator discrepancy is structured and depends on the heart signal. We introduce OpDis-PS, a diffusion posterior sampler that models this discrepancy instead of treating it as signal-independent noise. OpDis-PS learns how the discrepancy depends on the signal, folds this into a corrected forward operator, and reconstructs with a fixed prior across all operator conditions. We study two realistic sources of discrepancy, changing lung conductivity and an added subcutaneous-fat layer. Across both, OpDis-PS reconstructs more accurately than classical regularization and learned predictors, with its largest gains on held-out cardiac pathologies.
  </details>

- **[Federated Physics-Informed Thermal Field Prediction Across Unseen Geometries in Distributed Additive Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17794)**  `arXiv:2611.17794`  
  _Amirul Islam Saimon, Zhenyu, Kong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In additive manufacturing (AM), each change in part geometry requires a new thermal simulation, which makes design evaluation over many candidates costly. Geometry-aware models reduce this cost by predicting thermal fields on unseen parts with a single learned model, but they assume that diverse geometries are available centrally. This assumption is restrictive when each manufacturing site holds only a limited set of proprietary part designs that it cannot share. We propose Fed-PIGANO, a federated framework that trains physics-informed geometry-aware neural operator (PI-GANO) models at individual sites and aggregates their geometry encoders and prediction networks without sharing part geometries. We evaluate the framework on a 2D steady-state thermal benchmark for metal AM, with geometries from five polygonal families distributed across three sites under four scenarios that range from identical to strongly different family compositions. Over seven replications, Fed-PIGANO achieves mean relative L2 errors of 1.83% to 2.04% across all 12 scenario-site cases, compared with up to 20.46% for local-only training, and consistently yields lower PDE residuals. Its prediction accuracy is comparable to that of a centralized model trained on the pooled geometries of all sites. These results demonstrate that federated learning lets manufacturing sites benefit from each other's geometries without sharing them, enabling rapid thermal prediction on new part designs for tasks such as shape optimization, inverse design, and uncertainty quantification.
  </details>

- **[Direction or Magnitude? Learning Scalars over Task-Calibrated Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17784)**  `arXiv:2611.17784`  
  _Ziqing Wen, Xiaohui Song, Jiahuan Wang, Ping Luo, Junlin Zeng, Dongsheng Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) aims to preserve adaptation quality while reducing training costs. We propose SCALAR, which achieves performance comparable to LoRA with substantially fewer trainable parameters, lower peak training memory, and higher throughput. SCALAR constructs a fixed input basis from task activations and assigns a random output dictionary to each input direction. Fine-tuning learns only layer-specific scalar coefficients, reducing gradient and optimizer-state storage. Grouped computation aggregates output atoms before applying the adapter to token activations, while dictionary sharing across compatible layers reduces fixed-basis storage. Experiments on commonsense reasoning, mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge evaluation after instruction tuning demonstrate effective adaptation at small trainable budgets. On commonsense reasoning, SCALAR achieves accuracy comparable to LoRA with about 4% of its trainable parameters. System measurements on Qwen2.5-7B show 20% lower peak training memory and 6% higher throughput than LoRA. The combination of adaptation quality, a compact trainable state, and training efficiency makes SCALAR a promising approach to resource-constrained fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Antidistillation is Cheap: Lightweight Distillation-Resistance via Stylistic Corruption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17772)**  `arXiv:2611.17772`  
  _Anh Tu Ngo, Anupam Chattopadhyay_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation (KD) is a highly effective paradigm for transferring capabilities from large language models (LLMs) to smaller, more specialized student models. In commercial deployment where frontier models are restricted behind black-box APIs, sequence-level distillation remains a potent vector for competitors to clone proprietary capabilities at a fraction of the development cost. Existing defenses mostly rely on surrogate models to manipulate next-token sampling, which increases computational overhead and real-time inference latency. In this work, we propose a lightweight defense that dynamically injects style-steering vectors into the teacher model's activations upon malicious queries. This framework offers exceptional flexibility, allowing hosts to customize target defensive styles and dynamically blend them to maximize stylistic variance. Crucially, this inference-time intervention preserves semantic utility and fluency during malicious queries, misleading adversaries into believing that they are extracting pristine teacher outputs, while standard consumers continue to receive original, unmodified responses. Our experiments demonstrate that this framework successfully disrupts student learning, achieving substantial anti-distillation performance while incurring negligible overhead and preserving text naturalness. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Logically Consistent Accurate Forecasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17735)**  `arXiv:2611.17735`  
  _Kate Yuan, Rohan Alur, Manish Raghavan, Devavrat Shah_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic forecasting systems based on large language models (LLMs) are now competitive with expert human forecasters, but these systems still suffer from a critical limitation: the inability to produce forecasts that are logically consistent. For example, forecasts for related events often fail to satisfy the axioms of probability, which in turn implies that related forecasts cannot simultaneously be correct and undermines their utility for decision makers. Enforcing consistency is not trivial -- naive approaches (e.g., projection onto the set of consistent forecasts) can degrade accuracy. We argue that the key to overcoming the apparent tradeoff between consistency and accuracy lies in the reasoning which supports each forecast. We finetune a supervisory language model that leverages this reasoning to jointly reconcile inconsistencies while improving the accuracy of related forecasts. On ForecastBench, our method improves the Brier score of a retrieval-augmented GPT-5.1 forecaster by 9.3% while reducing measures of structural inconsistency by 82-90%. We interrogate our method via a comprehensive series of baselines and ablations, and find that no alternative method matches our gains in consistency and accuracy simultaneously.
  </details>

- **[Pattern-Based Matrix Analysis Under Permutations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17674)**  `arXiv:2611.17674`  
  _Andrei Ermakov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-rank approximation captures one important notion of matrix simplicity: a matrix is simple if it is well described by a low-dimensional linear model. This paper studies a complementary notion. We consider matrices that become structurally simple only after permuting rows and columns, so that their mass concentrates in a prescribed geometric pattern such as a band, block-diagonal, arrowhead, or related form. We introduce a pattern distance that measures how close a matrix is to a given pattern after optimal reordering, giving a common framework for structural problems such as latent ordering, community detection, and bandwidth minimization. The problem is NP-hard in general; we derive an alternating algorithm based on Linear Assignment subproblems. We further introduce a pattern-plus-low-rank decomposition that combines permutation-structured fitting with a low-rank residual, whose permutation step is pattern fitting on the residual; its gains across pattern families persist at a matched parameter budget and are substantially reduced under a density-matched random-mask control. We evaluate the framework on synthetic recovery tasks, real correlation and graph adjacency matrices, and transformer attention heads, showing that a single universal algorithm recovers qualitatively different forms of matrix structure.
  </details>

- **[Classifier-Guided Latent Diffusion for Small-Molecule Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17650)**  `arXiv:2611.17650`  
  _Ali Forooghi, Luis Rueda, Alioune Ngom_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models have emerged as a promising class of generative models for molecular design because of their ability to learn complex chemical distributions and support conditional or guided generation. In this work, we investigate a latent diffusion pipeline for property-guided small-molecule generation. The proposed framework represents canonicalized SMILES strings using a frozen ChemBERTa encoder, trains a Transformer-based decoder to reconstruct SELFIES strings from molecular latent embeddings, and learns an unconditional denoising diffusion model in the ChemBERTa latent space. To enable property-directed generation, a differentiable latent property predictor is used to guide the diffusion sampling process. Generated SELFIES strings are converted to SMILES, validated using RDKit, and evaluated using molecular validity, uniqueness, novelty, and predicted property scores. We study guidance toward two drug-relevant binary endpoints: blood--brain barrier permeability and inhibition of human $\beta$-secretase 1. The results suggest that latent diffusion over pretrained molecular embeddings can generate chemically valid and novel molecules while enabling controllable steering toward desired molecular properties.
  </details>

- **[Joint World Action Models through Differentiable Trajectory Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17626)**  `arXiv:2611.17626`  
  _Mintae Kim, Koushil Sreenath_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Joint world action models (WAMs) generate future actions and states together, modeling world-action trajectories rather than actions alone. However, trajectory imitation does not ensure that these trajectories are task-effective or consistent with dynamics. We present \textbf{world action trajectory optimization (WATO)}, which optimizes generated actions and future states jointly for control. WATO uses both as references in a dynamics-constrained trajectory optimization problem and differentiates the task loss through the optimized trajectory to train the generator. This makes generated trajectories task-aware and dynamics-consistent while using trajectory optimization only during training. At deployment, WATO directly generates candidate trajectories and ranks them with a world model, without iterative trajectory optimization. Across $7$ visual control environments, WATO improves average success from $79.9\%$ for the imitation joint WAM to $91.7\%$, outperforming strong model-based, goal-conditioned RL, and generative control baselines. Compared with cross-entropy method (CEM), WATO is on average $11.5\times$ faster and uses $600.5\times$ fewer world model evaluations at deployment.
  </details>

- **[Offline KL-Regularized Reinforcement Learning with Stochastic Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17603)**  `arXiv:2611.17603`  
  _Andrew Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  KL-regularized reinforcement learning is the objective behind RL fine-tuning of language models, and its data are often collected by the reference policy itself. However, beyond bandits and deterministic transitions, little is known about how much offline data it needs. In this work, we study finite discounted Markov decision processes with unknown stochastic transitions, $K$ state-action pairs and effective horizon $H$. For data that cover the regularized optimum with concentrability $C\geq 4$, every estimator needs $\Omega(KCH^3/(\epsilon\max\{\epsilon,\beta\}))$ samples on some instances when $\epsilon$ is small, where $\beta$ is the temperature. Under a fixed design, pessimistic soft value iteration with a variance-aware penalty attains this rate with high probability, up to logarithmic factors, when $\beta\geq 1.4H$. When the data come from the reference policy itself, we show that it covers the optimum up to a power law whose exponent grows as $H/\beta$; in the same temperature range, this power law alone gives the estimator a sample complexity polynomial in $1/\epsilon$, with no coverage assumption. For $\beta\geq H$ the power can also increase the sample size needed by a factor polynomial in $1/\epsilon$. Finally, the plug-in estimator, which is minimax optimal with a generative model, can fail on reference data at small temperatures when many actions are rarely seen, while the pessimistic estimator does not.
  </details>

- **[LightTAR: Light and Fast Target-Aware Representations for Multimodal Tabular Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17600)**  `arXiv:2611.17600`  
  _Eilam Shapira, Alan Arazi, Shoham Grunblat, Roi Reichart_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal tabular learning involves classification and regression on tables combining structured numerical and categorical features with unstructured text and images. These unstructured fields are commonly represented by pretrained encoder embeddings reduced with principal component analysis (PCA). This reduction prioritizes input variance but may overlook information relevant to predicting the target. We introduce LightTAR, a simple, inexpensive method that adapts dimensionality reduction to the prediction task. LightTAR augments the standard PCA representation with a few target-aware features extracted jointly from the final and an intermediate encoder layer using regularized partial least squares (PLS). We evaluate LightTAR with 21 tabular models, including tree ensembles, neural networks, and tabular foundation models. LightTAR improves average predictive performance for all models and, averaged across models, improves prediction on almost every MulTaBench dataset. These gains come with a small increase in total runtime relative to PCA representations. LightTAR integrates directly into existing encoding pipelines, improving tabular learners' predictive performance without modifying the encoder or the learner.
  </details>

- **[Beyond a Single Sign: BiTE for Branch-Preserving Binary Threshold Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17576)**  `arXiv:2611.17576`  
  _Siddharth Gupta, Mikail Yayla, Akash Kumar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Binary neural networks (BNNs) reduce model storage and replace many arithmetic operations with efficient bitwise computations, but the single-sign activation limits their representational capacity. Each preactivation direction is compared with one threshold and reduced to one of two values, discarding variation within each sign region. We propose \textbf{BiTE} (Branch-Preserving Binary Threshold Expansion), which evaluates each feature direction against $K$ ordered thresholds and retains the resulting binary responses as separate inputs to the following learned operator. BiTE can distinguish up to $K+1$ ordered activation states while allowing the following operator to learn a separate transformation for each threshold response. It also enables a controllable trade-off between the number of independently constructed feature directions and the number of ordered responses extracted from each direction. On BinaryViT, BiTE improves mean final-epoch Top-1 accuracy from $74.60\pm0.30\%$ to $75.83\pm0.15\%$ on CIFAR-10 and from $42.80\pm0.46\%$ to $44.07\pm0.32\%$ on CIFAR-100, with nearly unchanged trainable parameter counts. A matched uniform-collapse control reaches $75.33\pm0.15\%$ on CIFAR-10, supporting the value of retaining the threshold responses separately relative to the evaluated scalar collapse. Binary VGG-16 experiments further show a favorable observed parameter--accuracy trade-off, including a configuration that achieves $0.64$ percentage points higher mean accuracy with $21.8\%$ fewer parameters than conventional width scaling. Overall, these results support branch-preserving threshold expansion as an effective way to improve binary representations and the observed parameter--accuracy trade-off.
  </details>

- **[A Synthetic Benchmark for Target-Centric Causal Discovery in Industrial Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17556)**  `arXiv:2611.17556`  
  _Angelo Ziletti_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal discovery promises to find which recorded variables drive a process target such as product quality, yield or energy use. Process data with known causes are rare, and in existing benchmarks the difficulty of identifying the causes of a target is either fixed by the recorded data or set for the whole graph. We present a synthetic benchmark for target-centric causal discovery in industrial processes. Its generator is a two-level structural causal model inspired by batch production, assembled from components that each model one phenomenon of process data, such as a control loop or a delayed effect. The graph is built around the target: without sensor error, every non-cause can be distinguished from the direct causes by conditioning on them or by time order. Each component is set independently, so the difficulty can be varied one condition at a time. We evaluate eight methods, from association measures to effect estimation, with an error analysis by the role of the selected candidate; all degrade as the difficulties combine, each in a way consistent with its assumptions.
  </details>

- **[Identifying Latent Neural Dynamics with Recognition-Parametrised Gaussian Process Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17487)**  `arXiv:2611.17487`  
  _Arielle Rosinski, Qianqian Feng, Lior Fox, Maneesh Sahani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural circuits are densely interconnected systems, and many neural computations are thought to be mediated by the dynamical evolution of activity within these recurrent networks, a hypothesis often referred to as "computation through dynamics". Under this view, understanding computation within real neural populations depends on building models of the dynamical rules governing the temporal evolution of recorded high-dimensional neural activity. Most commonly, such models depend on explicit generative parameterisations of (i) the (nonlinear) intrinsic dynamical flow field and stochasticity, (ii) the (nonlinear) mapping from dynamical state to neuronal activity, and (iii) the variability of individual neural responses given that dynamical state. Misspecification of any of these components can bias estimates of dynamics and latent trajectories. In this paper, we circumvent generation-related issues by introducing the Recognition-Parametrised Gaussian Process dynamics (RP-GPdyn) model. RP-GPdyn models the dynamical flow field using a nonparametric Gaussian Process-defined transition function and models the relationship of dynamics to neural activity implicitly using the Recognition-Parametrised Model (RPM) framework. We show that this generation-free approach uncovers meaningful, behaviourally relevant latent variables and dynamics from both synthetic and experimental datasets.
  </details>

- **[ERGo: Energy-Efficient Hybrid Graph Neural Network Training on Processing-In-Memory Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17478)**  `arXiv:2611.17478`  
  _Pratyush Dhingra, Chibuike E. Ugwu, Janardhan Rao Doppa, Partha Pratim Pande_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Processing-in-memory (PIM) has been proposed as an alternative computing paradigm for training Deep Neural Networks, including Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). Despite these advancements, training GNN workloads on PIM devices necessitates off-chip memory access. This off-chip access is expensive in terms of performance and energy, thereby impacting the overall energy efficiency of the training process on PIM platforms. In this paper, we propose a novel hybrid training framework called ERGo that automatically switches from a full-parameter phase to a parameter-efficient phase during the training process with negligible loss in predictive accuracy. The parameter-efficient phase employs low-rank representations of GNN weights, effectively reducing the number of trainable parameters. This helps in reducing the off-chip access during the end-to-end training process on PIM architectures, leading to notable improvements in both performance and energy efficiency. ERGo outperforms the conventional full-parameter GNN training on a PIM-based platform by up to 3.15x in speedup and enhances energy efficiency by 10.5x. Furthermore, ERGo offers additional advantages when utilized on heterogeneous architectures incorporating non-volatile memory (NVM) and static random-access memory (SRAM). Training on the heterogeneous NVM-SRAM architecture typically utilizes NVM for the forward-pass and SRAM for the backward-pass. However, NVM cells suffer from low device endurance and repeated write operations due to weight updates can lead to poor lifetime. ERGo improves the lifetime of NVM devices by freezing the weights and preventing weight updates in the parameter-efficient phase. Experimental results demonstrate that ERGo improves the lifetime of heterogeneous NVM-SRAM architecture by up to 33x in comparison to full-parameter implementation.
  </details>

- **[Do Mixture-of-Experts Routers Benefit from Ensembling?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17472)**  `arXiv:2611.17472`  
  _Viktor Veselý, Sebastian Jitaru_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensembles of neural networks have repeatedly been shown to outperform single networks. Ensemble theory attributes this gain to the accuracy of the individual members and the diversity among them. Mixture-of-experts models already combine many experts, but a single router decides how the experts are weighted for each input. Motivated by ensemble theory, we apply ensembling to the routing step: an ensemble of routers, trained jointly with the experts, proposes the expert weights. We systematically evaluate this approach using underparameterized networks on two image-classification and two tabular-regression tasks. We analyse each experiment with a performance-diversity decomposition of the router ensemble, which, to our knowledge, has not previously been developed for routers. We report four findings. First, more routers lower the validation loss. Second, training routers to explicitly maximize expert diversity improves the mean performance on three of the four tasks. Third, explicitly maximizing router diversity has no effect on performance. Fourth, combining router weights with the arithmetic mean works better than combining them with the geometric mean. Routing benefits mildly from ensembling, but this benefit is offset by the computational cost of training additional routers.
  </details>

- **[Improved Regret via Variance-Aware Policy Optimization in Adversarial MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17469)**  `arXiv:2611.17469`  
  _Ofir Schlisselberg, Alon Cohen, Yishay Mansour_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study episodic adversarial MDPs with unknown dynamics and full-information rewards and achieve regret with leading term $\widetilde O(\sqrt{H^3SAK})$. Here $H$ is the horizon, $S$ the total number of states across decision layers, $A$ the number of actions, and $K$ the number of episodes. This improves the two incomparable prior leading terms by factors $\sqrt{S/H}$ and $\sqrt{H^3}$, leaving a factor $\sqrt H$ to the lower-bound scale. We give a reduction from unknown to known dynamics for \emph{downstream-dependent} algorithms, whose policy at step $h$ is computed only from information at steps $h+1$ and onward. This reduces the remaining leading-order gap to finding a downstream-dependent known-dynamics algorithm with $\widetilde O(H\sqrt K)$ regret, which would yield the tight $\widetilde O(H\sqrt{SAK})$ leading term for unknown dynamics. We instantiate the reduction with VOPO, a policy optimization (PO) algorithm. Its improved guarantees follow from a variance-aware analysis enabled by a novel bonus, which we call the Stability-Matching Bonus (SMB). VOPO achieves $\widetilde O(\sqrt{H^3K})$ regret under known dynamics with full information; its $\sqrt H$ gap to the target $H\sqrt K$ rate is inherited by our unknown-dynamics guarantee. We also show that with known dynamics and trajectory bandit feedback, VOPO achieves $\widetilde O(\sqrt{H^2SAK})$ regret. Both known-dynamics guarantees improve the corresponding state-of-the-art PO bounds by a factor $\sqrt H$. A matching lower bound establishes that the full-information rate is tight in one parameter regime.
  </details>

- **[Availability-Aware Historical Feedback Learning for Electric Arc Furnace Temperature and Chemical Composition Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17463)**  `arXiv:2611.17463`  
  _Ahmed Shoyeb Raihan, Farzana Islam, Ashish Nimbarte, Imtiaz Ahmed_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electric arc furnace (EAF) steelmaking plays an increasingly important role in recycled and lower-carbon steel production. However, variability in charged materials, energy input, and operating conditions makes accurate prediction of molten steel temperature and final chemical composition challenging. Intermittent or delayed measurements limit the information available for timely prediction. This study develops an availability-aware historical-feedback learning framework for predicting EAF endpoint temperature and the final concentrations of twelve chemical elements. Eight industrial data sources are integrated into three progressively enriched predictor representations comprising 19 static, 39 temporal, and 47 history-informed features. Historical outcomes are incorporated only when eligible under an assumed 60-minute measurement-release delay. Using 19,738 temperature heats and 3,341 chemistry heats, twelve tuned regression algorithms are evaluated through chronological forward validation. The proposed historical-feedback representation reduces root mean squared error (RMSE) in 147 of 156 matched algorithm-target comparisons. For temperature prediction, ExtraTrees achieves an RMSE of 12.290 degrees C, representing a 5.54 percent reduction compared with its temporal-only counterpart. Historical-feature ablation and feedback-delay sensitivity analyses demonstrate the importance of recent outcome information and its availability. In a separate retrospective later-period evaluation, lightweight adaptive residual correction reduces temperature RMSE from 12.502 to 12.275 degrees C without retraining the underlying model. Exploratory deep learning comparisons and target-specific feature-attribution analyses provide additional insights into predictive behavior. The results establish historical outcome availability and sequential feedback as important considerations for EAF endpoint prediction.
  </details>

- **[Unfinished-Bench: Measuring Rule Violations by Persistent Agents in Realistic Work Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17460)**  `arXiv:2611.17460`  
  _Seunghyun Lee, Jaehui Hwang, Dongyoon Han, Sangdoo Yun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents are increasingly equipped with persistence toward task completion to run delegated work autonomously for long periods, under many unstated constraints from internal documents and other people's authority. When completion requires violating a rule, the agent must decide alone, yet benchmarks of instruction-rule conflicts state the rule in the prompt or keep it hidden and do not evaluate persistence. We present Unfinished-Bench, a benchmark that gives models persistence in autonomous runs without a user, in realistic work environments with embedded rules. In each of its 97 scenarios across 7 organizations and 7 risk categories, the goal and the rule cannot both hold. We find that persistence raised violations of a rule in an internal document from 32.2% to 43.6%, five times the 8.5% for the same rule stated by the user. Of the document-rule violations, 98.3% occurred after the rule was displayed, and in 89% of a judged sample the agent first wrote down the rule. Violations of rules placed in the environment without user oversight can lead to serious incidents, so persistence-driven risks need to be evaluated. Benchmark documentation:this https URL
  </details>

- **[WETW: Learning Pair-Specific Weighted Euclidean Distances for Efficient DTW Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17412)**  `arXiv:2611.17412`  
  _Christos Panourgias, Qitong Wang, George Tzagkarakis, Themis Palpanas_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) is the standard distance for similarity search over time series that are misaligned in time, but its cost is quadratic in the series length and lower bounds often prune few candidates, so search over large collections of long series is slow. Learned approximations embed the series to make comparisons cheap, yet compare every pair with the same fixed rule, usually the Euclidean distance, although DTW aligns each pair differently. We observe that, once a pair is aligned, DTW is a Euclidean distance over coordinates selected for that pair. Building on this view, we propose Weighted Euclidean Time Warping (WETW), a learned distance that adapts the comparison of fixed-length embeddings to each pair. A small symmetric network predicts one weight per embedding di- mension and pair without computing an alignment, which halves the error of the best static distance on the same embeddings. The embeddings come from the Weighted Euclidean SEries Encoder (WESEE), whose multi-scale Temporal Feature Extractor keeps the timing information that global pooling removes and raises recall@1 from 0.90 to 0.95, although WETW works with any encoder. We also show that fitting DTW distances is not enough for search, as regression alone misorders the closest neighbours, and that ranking regularization raises recall@1 from 0.79 to 0.95. Our filter-and-verify pipeline, WETW-FV, uses WETW to build a shortlist and exact DTW to check it, so the search is approximate but every returned distance is exact. Over eight datasets, WETW-FV is 11.4x faster than the fastest exact method among the UCR Suite, lower bounds and MESSI-DTW, at a mean recall@10 of 0.93, while the best learned baseline reaches 0.59 at the same speed. On 48K-point series, WETW- FV remains 40x faster than the UCR Suite at a recall@10 of 0.91.
  </details>

- **[Alignment with Reflect-Reasoning: Learning How To Follow Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17398)**  `arXiv:2611.17398`  
  _Henry Bell, Caroline Zhang, Dhaval Potdar, Mohammed Mobasserul Haque, Brandon Fain_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The appropriate governing norms and principles for the alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) often depend on cultural context and deployment application. However, most alignment techniques focus on training models to conform to human preferences or constitutional principles specified at training time and subsequently fixed. In practice, many deployers rely on system prompting to implement contextual norms and guardrails at inference time. Prior work has shown that post-generation reflective reasoning with critique and revision can improve conformance compared to simple system prompting, but such methods are limited to the reasoning abilities inherent in the trained model. We introduce Reflect-Reasoning, a training method that teaches models how to reason about alignment to principles, rather than simply which principles to follow. Across five models, Reflect-Reasoning consistently outperforms both system prompting and explicit prompting strategies for in-context reflective reasoning. These improvements extend and generalize to entirely out-of-distribution domains of medical and financial principles. We additionally demonstrate that Reflect-Reasoning reduces the occurrence of rare but serious principle violations. Finally, we show that Reflect-Reasoning does not erode the safety engineering of a model and is robust against jailbreaking attempts using adversarial constitutions. Overall, our results demonstrate that Reflect-Reasoning is a scalable method for training constitutional alignment as a generalizable reasoning capability, enabling models to adapt to novel principles and produce transparent reasoning traces without additional fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Time Flies When You Parallelise: Pareto-efficient Time Series Foundation Models with Linear Recurrences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16934)**  `arXiv:2611.16934`  
  _Torben Berndt, Lingyi Yang, Federico Nardi, Yi Heng Lim, AJ Shears, Benjamin Walker_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series foundation models have demonstrated strong zero-shot forecasting performance, but computational efficiency remains a central architectural challenge. Temporal self-attention incurs quadratic cost in the number of temporal tokens, while nonlinear recurrent state transitions restrict parallel computation along the temporal dimension. We introduce ci-proto, a family of time series foundation models based on Mamba-3, whose linear state recurrences support chunk-wise parallel-in-time computation during training, and inference costs that scale linearly with sequence length. The architecture alternates recurrent temporal processing with cross-variate attention, supporting multivariate probabilistic forecasting with past-only and future-known covariates. Across fev-bench's 100 forecasting tasks, ci-proto XL sets a new state of the art among time series foundation models. The five ci-proto variants also make up the entire Pareto frontier for mean scaled quantile loss and mean absolute scaled error against both parameter count and median runtime.
  </details>

- **[Detection Is Not Early Warning: Evaluating Token-Level Alarms on Language Model Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16803)**  `arXiv:2611.16803`  
  _Marco De Negri, Luca Sartori, Lorenzo Baggi, Moritz Reihs, Eduard Frank Ďurech, Mennatallah El-Assady, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many failures of language models begin at a specific token, the onset. A monitor that reads the model during generation can classify the finished response, detect the failure once it is under way, or warn before it begins, and only a warning leaves time to stop or steer the generation. Monitors are usually evaluated with response-level metrics such as AUROC, which ignore when the monitor fires. We introduce PREWARN, an evaluation protocol for any failure with an annotated onset. It calibrates each monitor's threshold on validation data to an error rate chosen for the application, runs the monitor, and scores its alarms relative to the onset on all three tasks. As a case study we apply it to repetitive degeneration in three open language models, with early warning as the main task, using linear probes on activations and six untrained baselines. Response-level metrics saturate on this failure, and a rule that reads only the response length scores almost perfectly, while probes that AUROC ranks as equal warn very differently under PREWARN. Probes warn before the onset on most degenerate responses of two of the three models, low-rank adapters below the probe more than double the share of tokens just before the onset that it flags, largely by flagging degenerate responses long before the onset, and no untrained baseline warns much more often than random alarms. We release the probes with calibrated thresholds atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Rectified Looped Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16265)**  `arXiv:2611.16265`  
  _Musheng Li, Jiawei Zhang, Ziyuan Liu, Yuantao Gu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped Transformers increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block, offering a parameter-efficient approach to language modeling. However, most existing works directly reuse each loop's output as the next's input, which can lead to hidden-state scale growth and perturbation amplification as recurrence deepens. We propose Rectified Looped Transformer (RLT), which redesigns feedback between loops using an interpolation schedule inspired by flow-based modeling. At each loop, RLT forms the next input by linearly interpolating between the current state and its prediction. Such interpolation distributes state changes across loops, encouraging a smoother trajectory from the initial input to the final output. Theoretical analysis proves that, under appropriate assumptions, RLT keeps state magnitudes uniformly bounded across loops and reduces the worst-case perturbation amplification. We evaluate RLT on language modeling corpora and downstream benchmarks. At matched training and inference depths, RLT consistently achieves lower perplexity than a standard looped Transformer, while exhibiting greater robustness to input perturbations and variance in inference depth. Consistent with the theoretical results, latent trajectory analysis shows that RLT maintains more stable hidden-state norms across loops, which also facilitates extrapolation-based decoding strategies. Notably, RLT achieves these gains without requiring additional trainable parameters or extra recurrent block evaluations, thus providing a simple yet more stable feedback mechanism for looped Transformers.
  </details>

- **[JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16174)**  `arXiv:2611.16174`  
  _Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Ting-Yao Hu, Sanjoy Chowdhury, Fatih Ilhan, Oncel Tuzel, Raviteja Vemulapalli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.
  </details>

- **[CastWorld: Benchmarking Context-Aware Agentic Time Series Forecasting Beyond Predictive Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15262)**  `arXiv:2611.15262`  
  _Bokai Pan, Ruijie Yang, Mingyue Cheng, Jiahao Wang, Xiaoyu Tao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic time series forecasting extends conventional approaches beyond one-shot numerical prediction toward autonomous, context-aware interaction. However, existing benchmarks offer limited integrated support for diverse contextual information, forecasting-specific adaptive interactions, and systematic evaluation of forecasting agents. In this paper, we introduce CastWorld, an agentic benchmark for context-aware time series forecasting. First, its context-rich dataset suite spans 8 domains and 25 real-world datasets, covering external variables, events, and historical cases under a unified context specification. Second, a lightweight, modular forecasting-native harness provides 49 core tools and a state-adaptive loop that tracks forecast versions and enables iterative evidence gathering and forecast revision. Intermediate forecasts can expose information gaps, while newly acquired evidence guides subsequent revisions and final version selection. Third, model-level evaluation assesses forecast quality, process execution, behavioral trustworthiness, and resource efficiency through decision trajectories and forecast-version histories. Also, systems-level evaluation compares predictive accuracy across forecasting systems. Experiments reveal distinct decision behaviors among agents with comparable accuracy. Additional interactions do not necessarily improve forecasts, and agents may fail to select their best previously generated predictions. Allowing evidence acquisition during forecast refinement improves task-aware forecast scores across all three evaluated models. CastWorld provides a reusable foundation for evaluating agentic time series forecasting under controlled conditions.
  </details>

- **[Profile-Based Auditing of Domestic Violence Survey Selection Across South Asia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15106)**  `arXiv:2611.15106`  
  _Mohammad Abu Obaida Mullick, Rezaur Rahman Ratul, Mymuna Nourin, Md. Motaharul Islam_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domestic violence (DV) data in the Demographic and Health Surveys (DHS) are collected through a specialized module whose household-level sampling and privacy requirements create a distinct observation process. We ask whether demographic, socioeconomic, and household characteristics form reproducible population profiles and whether these profiles contain information about selection into the DV module across South Asia. Using DHS Individual Recode data for 796,374 women aged 15-49 across Afghanistan, India, Myanmar, Nepal, and Pakistan, we harmonize 61 numeric and 8 categorical features, reduce the profile space with PCA, and identify six interpretable profiles. We then evaluate 22 machine-learning models under primary-sampling-unit-aware holdout validation. The best model, HistGradientBoosting, achieves ROC-AUC 0.9167 and PR-AUC 0.9612, versus 0.8475 and 0.9203 for a design-only baseline. Adding woman-level characteristics yields the largest incremental gain, while explicit interaction terms add little out-of-sample performance. Country-specific analyses reveal substantial heterogeneity, and leave-one-country-out evaluation shows that transferability of the incremental profile signal is limited. These results demonstrate that interpretable ML can audit differential coverage in sensitive survey modules beyond the formal sampling design.
  </details>

- **[Conformal Risk Control for QoT-Aware Lightpath Feasibility Under Technology-Driven Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13893)**  `arXiv:2611.13893`  
  _Kiarash Rezaei, Omran Ayoub, Paolo Monti, Carlos Natalino_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence and machine learning (AI/ML)-based regression models support operational decision-making in optical networks but typically expose only point predictions, which carry no statistically controlled information about their reliability. This paper proposes a post-hoc conformal risk control (CRC) framework that keeps a pretrained AI/ML regressor fixed, provides a finite-sample guarantee on uncertainty band miscoverage, and maps the band to operational decisions through policy rules. The guarantee applies to interval miscoverage, while decision-error rates are evaluated empirically. We instantiate the framework for quality of transmission (QoT)-aware lightpath (LP) feasibility assessment, in which a regressor predicts optical signal-to-noise ratio (OSNR) under domain shifts caused by fiber, amplifier, and switching upgrades. Feature shift contribution scores identify the LP features whose relation to QoT changes the most across a technology transition, and two policies translate the bands into feasibility decisions, allowing operators to tune the trade-off between conservatively rejecting or aggressively accepting LP realizations. Experiments on open space-division multiplexing (SDM) QoT datasets over the CONUS topology show that, for a target coverage of 90%, the uncertainty bands meet the target in all 170 trials spanning 17 domain shifts and ten calibration-test partitions, whereas a source-only quantile regression (QR) baseline meets it in none. Under severe shifts, the efficiency-oriented (EO) policy empirically improves zero-shot (ZS) feasibility-decision accuracy from about 85% to above 99%, approaching a transfer learning (TL) policy while avoiding retraining. The framework provides finite-sample control of uncertainty band miscoverage for regression-based optical network decision support, with potential applications beyond QoT-aware LP feasibility assessment.
  </details>

- **[Stochastic Projections and Rank-Aware Learning Rates for Federated LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13331)**  `arXiv:2611.13331`  
  _Saber Malekmohammadi, Kevin Kuo, Virginia Smith, Golnoosh Farnadi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning large-scale foundation models across resource-constrained, distributed clients presents significant computational and communication challenges. While merging Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) with Federated Learning (FL) enables rank-based flexibility, the interdependence of the adaptation matrices ($\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$) often complicates optimization. In this work, we theoretically demonstrate that LoRA fine-tuning with Gaussian initialization and a frozen $\mathbf{A}$ matrix acts as a stochastic approximation of full fine-tuning, where batch gradients are subject to a random, rank-dependent perturbation. Motivated by this insight, we propose freezing and periodically resampling $\mathbf{A}$ matrices at the client level. This strategy decouples the $\mathbf{A}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ gradients, diminishes LoRA low-rank constraints and reduces both computational and uplink communication overheads by 50\%, allowing for the reallocation of resources toward higher adaptation ranks. Furthermore, we show that in this frozen-$\mathbf{A}$ regime, the adaptation rank $r$ directly governs the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the perturbed gradients. Specifically, we find that the SNR increases linearly with $r$, implying that clients' learning rates must be dynamically scaled with their adaptation ranks for higher utility. Building on these foundations, we introduce SPRA-LoRA (Stochastic Projection Rank-Aware LoRA), a federated rank-aware fine-tuning strategy that utilizes frozen, resampled $\mathbf{A}$ matrices and rank-dependent learning rates for clients. We also prove its convergence with heterogeneous client resources. Our experiments across benchmark datasets demonstrate that SPRA-LoRA consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with half the overhead on clients.
  </details>

- **[Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799)**  `arXiv:2611.12799`  
  _Tingcheng Bian, Kangye Gong, Jinchang Luo, MingQuan Cheng, Feiran Liu, Ni Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.
  </details>

- **[How Much Is Left Matters: False Positives and False Negatives in Partial-Prompt Contamination Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10307)**  `arXiv:2611.10307`  
  _Abhishek Anand_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Partial-prompt completion, where a model receives the first part of a benchmark problem and is checked for whether it reproduces the rest, is a widely used test for benchmark contamination. We audit this test on seven open-weight models using MATH-500 and recent AIME 2025/2026 problems, which serve as true negatives. Memorization flags depend strongly on how much text remains: for every model, problems with at most 8 leftover words are flagged several times more often than problems with at least 25 (31.5% vs. 5.5% for Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), and half to three-quarters of all ROUGE-based flags come from short leftovers. On true negatives, a ROUGE-L >= 0.75 threshold flags up to 6.7% of problems at a 60% prefix, and at an 80% prefix even exact match flags 8.6% (pooled over models; 6.2% on AIME 2026, which postdates every model), against 0% at 40-60%. The test also misses contamination upstream: models distilled from Qwen2.5-Math, whose base is heavily flagged in prior work, show almost no model-specific recall. Calibrating each model against models from other families removes most flags while isolating a consistent Qwen2.5-specific signal at every prefix length. We recommend length-stratified reporting, prefixes of at most 60%, style-matched post-cutoff controls, and cross-family calibration.
  </details>

- **[FedSharp: Byzantine Robust Decentralized Federated Learning Under Noisy Channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05546)**  `arXiv:2611.05546`  
  _Ojaswi Sinha, Amin Farajzadeh, Animesh Yadav, Melike Erol-Kantarci_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decentralized federated learning (DFL) eliminates the central aggregation server but remains vulnerable to non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) data, Byzantine attacks, and channel noise. We show that channel noise accumulates as models propagate across nodes and becomes increasingly significant as the learning rate decays. Moreover, accumulated noise enables Byzantine attackers to exploit loss-based model selection by mimicking the average of the honest models. To address these challenges, we propose FedSharp (screened, hop-aware, averaged, ranked, proximal), a robust DFL framework. In FedSharp, each node keeps the latest models received from its 2b predecessors, which guarantees at least b honest candidates when at most b nodes are Byzantine. FedSharp screens suspicious models using their deviations from the coordinate-wise median, averages the b lowest-loss candidates to reduce the fresh channel-noise variance by a factor of b, and applies hop-aware gradient-norm regularization and proximal training to mitigate accumulated noise and non-IID drift. Experiments on a 100-node ring with 33 Byzantine nodes show that FedSharp achieves 93.5 to 94.9 percent accuracy on MNIST, compared with 29.4 to 38.1 percent for a state-of-the-art single-model-selection benchmark, and 32.8 to 48.8 percent on CIFAR-10, compared with 10.0 to 30.5 percent, across Gaussian and hidden attacks under IID and non-IID data. On MNIST, FedSharp comes within 1.3 percentage points of the benchmark operating without channel noise, and exceeds it under the hidden attack.
  </details>

- **[An Axiomatic Framework for Reinforcement Learning in Open Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03301)**  `arXiv:2611.03301`  
  _Jianhong Wang, Yang Li, Samuel Kaski, Jonathan Lawry_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open multi-agent systems, where only a subset of agents are controllable, are central to real-world domains such as smart grids, yet lack a principled foundation for reinforcement learning algorithm design. We study this setting through n-agent ad hoc teamwork (NAHT), a recent framework for learning in open multi-agent environments. We propose an axiomatic framework that derives value function learning from the Shapley axioms (Efficiency, Symmetry and Linearity), treating them as structural constraints rather than heuristic design choices. This formulation builds on a novel NAHT decomposition, which characterises the fundamental difference between open and fully controllable multi-agent systems, and establishes a cooperative game-theoretic foundation for learning. We further show that enforcing these axioms on learning individual value functions recovers the Shapley value for Dec-POMDPs, providing a principled bridge between reinforcement learning and classical cooperative game theory. Building on this framework, we introduce Shapley Machines and Banzhaf Machines, two generic algorithmic templates that can be instantiated with standard reinforcement learning methods. When applied to strong base algorithms such as IPPO and POAM, our methods consistently improve learning efficiency and generalisation to unseen agent types and conventions across NAHT benchmark tasks. The code of experiments is published onthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Mode Dynamics: A Framework for Neural Network Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40143)**  `arXiv:2610.40143`  
  _Hossein Honarvar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Mode Dynamics, a framework that connects coordinated parameter motion during neural network learning to changes in model outputs. Starting from a recorded parameter trajectory, we decompose its updates into collective modes and map each mode to its state-dependent output effect. The framework organizes this link through three mechanisms. Decomposition identifies collective modes in parameter motion and their time-varying amplitudes. Reconstruction tests whether the effects induced by those modes recover local and accumulated output change. Concentration quantifies how behavioral contribution is distributed across modes. Controlled scalar regression and next-token experiments directly test these mechanisms. They show that small sets of leading modes reconstruct behavior, induced effects depend on the training path, and modal complexity varies with task and data. In a controlled induction task, suppressing the leading modes during training impairs held-out accuracy, whereas matched controls do not. We then evaluate the framework on held-out image and language-model outputs using CIFAR-100 autoencoding and a public Pythia-70M pretraining trajectory. Across these settings, the results connect compact modal subspaces of recorded parameter motion to evolving behavior.
  </details>

- **[PATTERN Can Be Solved from Memory: Testing Graph Neural Networks on Unseen Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15020)**  `arXiv:2610.15020`  
  _Amin Najafgholizadeh, Mohsen Afsharchi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  PATTERN asks graph neural networks to find a planted subgraph in a new host graph. The hosts change, but the benchmark draws just 100 patterns and reuses them across training and test splits. A lookup that extracts these patterns from the training labels achieves 96.6% class-weighted accuracy on the official test split, above every published result, without fitting a model or consulting the generator. On unseen patterns, the same lookup scores 50.6%. A matcher that also uses the generator reaches 99.2% on the official split. Among five graph networks we retrained, GRASS and PPGT go from the top two on the official split to the bottom two on unseen patterns. Each loses 8.1 percentage points even when familiar and unseen patterns are matched in size. Logistic regression on two-hop node statistics scores above every run of the five networks on unseen patterns. For CKGConv, the advantage follows the training patterns: it reappears with each of three independently drawn pattern libraries and disappears when every training and validation graph gets a new pattern. Generator-based inference also scores 82.3% on CLUSTER, compared with a published best of 81.6%. On Minesweeper's released board, four of eight planned chains produced samples within the time budget; none converged, but their pooled predictions reached 99.86% ROC AUC, above the published best of 99.55%. PATTERN's official split tests its intended task of finding familiar patterns in new graphs. We recommend reporting the same checkpoint's score on unseen patterns alongside it.
  </details>

- **[Parameterization method of reservoir properties for ensemble-based data assimilation using intermediate latent space of StyleGAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39626)**  `arXiv:2609.39626`  
  _Marcio A. Sampaio, Paulo H. Ranazzi, Martin J. Blunt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensemble smoothers are the most successful and efficient techniques currently available for history matching. However, because these methods rely on Gaussian assumptions, their performance is severely degraded when the prior geology is described in terms of complex facies distributions (non-Gaussian). In this way, for these methods, we need to apply efficient parameterization techniques. Currently, the most efficient methods for performing parameterization are deep learning models. However, given the variety of existing deep learning models, studies have not identified which is most suitable for use with ensemble-based methods, although some important models had already been evaluated. Based on a recent literature review, the most promising models selected were VAE-GAN, Latent Diffusion, and StyleGAN models. As a novel aspect of this work, data assimilation with the second generation of StyleGAN (StyleGAN2) model was performed using the latent z-space and intermediate w-space, separately. They were applied in two 2D case studies: one categorical (three facies) and the other continuous. The results demonstrated that all three models are highly efficient, with the StyleGAN2 model standing out for generating samples with geological realism and achieving excellent data matching in the cases studied. Our findings show that performing data assimilation with StyleGAN2 using the intermediate space (w-space) yielded better results than the traditional application in the latent space (z-space). This is due to the fact that ESMDA uses linear updates and the w-space is much more linear and disentangled than the highly entangled z-space, thereby ensuring that the updated vectors remain close to realistic geological patterns. These results were validated using main geostatistical and history matching metrics.
  </details>

- **[When a Flatness Proxy Is Not a Function: Robustness Certificates and Training Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38540)**  `arXiv:2609.38540`  
  _Vicente Opazo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A valid curvature upper bound need not justify either a robustness certificate or an intervention on an intrinsic predictor property. We demonstrate this distinction for a last-layer relative-flatness proxy used in both settings. First, empirical-risk stationarity does not eliminate pointwise first-order loss terms: at a finite global empirical-risk minimum, the retained certificate expression underestimates a loss increase by over $210\times$. We derive a globally valid, gauge-invariant feature-space repair. Second, common-row softmax shifts preserve predictions and the exact contraction while making the proxy unbounded. Even standard reference-class choices double it on average relative to the centered representation. For a single fixed-feature example with at least three classes, scalar retuning generically cannot align the induced probability updates. Row centering gives the orbit-minimized bound and restores value and full-model gradient invariance under this symmetry. Across 45 paired one-step tests on algorithmic and image models, amplified shifts separate raw-regularized predictors while quotient-regularized predictors remain aligned. Long-horizon CIFAR-10 experiments show substantial, reversible suppression of generalization, while evidence for selective delay after memorization is less consistent. Together, these results show that validity as a curvature upper bound does not by itself justify either inversion into a robustness certificate or differentiation into an intrinsic training intervention.
  </details>

- **[From Data to Program: Fast & Direct Generative Program Inference from Empirical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35348)**  `arXiv:2609.35348`  
  _Simon Klüttermann, Xueying Ding, Leman Akoglu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating probability densities from a finite set of samples typically requires dataset-specific model fitting. We introduce PRODiGI, a pretrained data-to-program model that infers an explicit, executable generative program in a single forward pass. Pretrained on synthetic datasets paired with their ground-truth programs, PRODiGI accommodates diverse generative families and data dimensionalities through template prediction and non-autoregressive program parameter decoding. Its inferred programs support direct sampling, density and score evaluation, and inspection independently of the pretrained model. We further introduce program-space fine-tuning, which refines differentiable program parameters by maximizing the likelihood of empirical data while keeping model parameters intact. Experiments show that PRODiGI-FT achieves better density and score metrics than existing pretrained models, while offering multi-fold speedups over its closest competitors. Program-space fine-tuning further reduces generation MMD by 90%. By turning empirical data into explicit, reusable programs, PRODiGI introduces a new direction for fast, interpretable tabular generative modeling.
  </details>

- **[ReCo: When to Relocate Sensor Kits under Deployment Constraints -- A NILM Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35075)**  `arXiv:2609.35075`  
  _Haokun Chen, Yu Tong, Yehai Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many sensing tasks obtain training labels only by deploying instruments in the field. With a limited number of sensor kits, a collection deadline, and measurement downtime at every move, the collector must repeatedly decide whether to stay at the current site or relocate. We study this decision in non-intrusive load monitoring (NILM), which estimates the power drawn by individual appliances from a home's main meter and is trained on data from homes temporarily fitted with appliance-level sub-meters. In NILM, appliance usage varies with the appliance, season and climate, and the value of new data depends on how diverse the combinations of target operation and background load are. To address this, we propose a constraint-based relocation framework and instantiate it for NILM as ReCo (Relocation by Coverage gain). ReCo counts new operating regimes in a joint target--background feature space, forecasts each home's future gain from the data collected so far, and each night weighs the gain of staying against the gain of moving elsewhere after the downtime. In replayed deployments on the Plegma dataset under two kit counts and two downtime costs, ReCo outperforms fixed-dwell and count-based schedules and a threshold rule using the same metric in every setting. Its advantage is not explained by collecting more days alone and is consistent with allocating the days to more valuable homes and periods.
  </details>

- **[Hidden not Deleted: How Networks Suppress Entangled Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27593)**  `arXiv:2609.27593`  
  _Akash Samanta, Manish Pratap Singh, Debasis Chaudhuri_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a neural network stops reporting a feature, has it forgotten the feature or merely hidden it? Shared representations complicate the answer: removing one feature's direction can also damage another feature. We study this problem in a small autoencoder that compresses and reconstructs inputs, allowing individual feature contributions to be traced directly. With the representation fixed, restoring a single bias partially recovers target detection by exposing a signal hidden behind the output's activation threshold. Tests on target-absent inputs distinguish this recovery from interventions that simply increase output. The same bias intervention becomes less effective when the representation is allowed to change. We find that training only the other outputs can weaken the target's contribution, even without a loss on its own output. A conditional theoretical analysis explains how interference with retained predictions creates this pressure. Yet more flexible predictors still detect residual target information. These findings show how recovery depends on feature geometry and on which parameters are trained, and why a silent output is insufficient evidence of forgetting.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Embedding Transfer: Architectural Interfaces, Iso-Parameter Scaling, and Preconditioner-Governed Consolidation in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18078)**  `arXiv:2609.18078`  
  _Zeyu Jia_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Grokking, or delayed generalization after memorization, incurs substantial training steps across algebraic tasks. Cross-operator transfer from a grokked donor accelerates target acquisition, yet post-grokking AdamW continuation triggers test-accuracy relapse as unannealed updates drift across residual interfaces and decaying second moments amplify late gradients. Across depths one to three on modular arithmetic ($\mathbb{Z}_{113}$), non-Abelian $S_5$ conjugation, and real-sensory MNIST modular arithmetic, we separate representational transfer from preconditioner-governed consolidation across thirteen experimental batteries. First, co-transferring internal blocks accelerates one-layer and two-layer modular takeoff beyond boundary interfaces, while graded prefix pinning bounds depth-three drift under unannealed continuation. Second, an iso-parameter $S_5$ scaling grid separates attention head dimension from MLP width: widening two-head attention to dimension $64$ reaches $98.54\%$ cold accuracy at $427\mathrm{k}$ parameters and $85.95\%$ boundary-only transfer, while final-slot representations refine conjugation directly across blocks without an intermediate binary-product bottleneck. Third, Plateau-Gated Leaky Consolidation pairs validation-envelope step damping with leaky second-moment decay, reaching $84.85\%$ zero-leakage validation-gated risk-constrained accuracy across five pre-saturation two-layer $S_5$ suites while cutting step budget relative to hard anchoring. Fourth, transferring a grokked visual trunk and blocks to image-disjoint MNIST targets reaches $92.27\%$ and $90.76\%$ accuracy, prunes odd centroid harmonics, and reaches $90.42\%$ risk-constrained accuracy across $60/60$ branches without hyperparameter retuning.
  </details>

- **[Fusion-Free Multimodal Classification with Heterogeneous Deep Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05019)**  `arXiv:2607.05019`  
  _Ilya Burenko, Dmitry Vetrov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multimodal classification, late-fusion approaches classify concatenated modality-specific features extracted by unimodal neural networks. When modality imbalance is pronounced, various regularization techniques have been proposed to balance the learning process and overcome the inferior performance of late-fusion networks. In contrast, this work demonstrates that multimodal data can be effectively classified without any explicit modality fusion, using deep ensembles of unimodal networks. We systematically compare deep ensembles to late-fusion networks at equal parameter count and show that ensembles consistently outperform state-of-the-art late-fusion methods designed to address modality imbalance when trained from random initialization. This advantage also holds over intermediate-fusion techniques we evaluated and over hybrid methods that combine unimodal and multimodal classifiers. We propose and empirically validate a method for selecting the number of models per modality in an ensemble, avoiding computationally expensive exhaustive search. Under extreme modality imbalance and small ensemble sizes, the heuristic indicates that ensembles of unimodal models trained solely on the stronger modality are preferable. As the ensemble scales up, incorporating models from the weaker modality becomes beneficial. Both predictions align with our empirical findings. However, the heuristic becomes less reliable when the diversity of ensemble members is low. To systematically explore the challenges of optimizing multimodal models, we propose a synthetic multimodal framework that allows control over both the number of modalities and their predictive strength. Our findings are consistent across synthetic and real-world datasets. Finally, by fitting scaling laws to bimodal datasets, we estimate the asymptotic performance of ensembles.
  </details>

- **[Input Pathways Shape Few-Shot, Not Zero-Shot, Binding in Tiny Transformers: A Fully-Enumerable Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04926)**  `arXiv:2607.04926`  
  _Yoshiyuki Ootani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does the way information reaches a transformer -- as symbolic tokens, a clean per-factor "oracle" code, or an entangled perceptual vector -- shape whether it binds that information compositionally? We study ~6-10K-parameter transformers on finite factored worlds enumerated exhaustively, so every measurement covers the whole input space and the informative routes are information-matched (exact Bayes ceiling 1.0). Four findings. (1) Endpoint invariance: on held-out binding queries no informative route reaches converged zero-shot composition -- each ends at or below chance, so the failure reflects inductive bias under a lookup-sufficient objective, not missing information. It also holds with the state in its own sequence position rather than added to every token. (2) Readability governs few-shot binding: when a small fraction of the held-out type is leaked into training, sample efficiency tracks code readability. The least readable entangled code is worst (-0.147 pooled, under both schemes); a dimension-matched control and a graded readability sweep isolate readability from input dimension. (3) The injection site, not the pathway: v1 read the remaining ordering among readable routes as pathway parameter sharing; that is withdrawn. The compared pathways are block-decomposable, hence one function class, so sharing was never manipulated; the ordering comes from the initialization scale of the broadcast-added state and vanishes when it gets its own position (+0.057 -> -0.006, 20 paired seeds/cell). (4) Failure anatomy: symbolic routes lose the answer at the readout; index routes mis-bind -- the answer stays decodable, yet an input intervention shows the output tracks the wrong slot. The central positive claim is readability; the others bound what a pathway comparison can establish. Code, manifests, and per-seed logs are released.
  </details>

- **[QVal: Evaluating Dense Supervision Signals for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.32034)**  `arXiv:2606.32034`  
  _Sergio Hernández-Gutiérrez, Matteo Merler, Ilze Amanda Auzina, Joschka Strüber, Ameya Prabhu, Matthias Bethge_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly act over long horizons, where outcome-only rewards say little about which intermediate actions were good. Dense supervision methods address this by scoring intermediate steps, but they are usually evaluated through the downstream performance of a full training pipeline. This is expensive, confounds signal quality with other training choices, and means that methods are rarely compared on common ground. We introduce QVal, a training-free testbed that measures the $Q$-alignment of a dense signal: whether it orders the candidate actions in each state the same way as their returns under a reference policy do. We benchmark 21 methods from seven families with six open-weight backbones in four environments. Simple prompting baselines are the most $Q$-aligned on average, and performance clusters strongly by family. Code-generated signals do well only in small state and action spaces; embedding-based signals are weak; self-distillation signals are anti-aligned; and no method is $Q$-aligned in open-ended environments. When five of these signals guide an actor at test time by choosing among four proposed actions, $Q$-alignment strongly correlates with the gain in task success. QVal is designed to be easily extensible to new environments and methods, enabling researchers to compare new signals cheaply before committing to a training run.
  </details>

- **[Generative Molecular Morphing for Flexible-Size Design via Unbalanced Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07239)**  `arXiv:2606.07239`  
  _Malte Franke, Stefan P. Schmid, Zarko Ivkovic, Kjell Jorner, Andreas Krause_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The success of generative molecular design depends on a model's steerability toward high-reward samples. Because many molecular properties are intrinsically linked to molecular size, accurately capturing the joint distribution of properties and the number of atoms is essential. However, current diffusion and flow-based models fix the number of atoms, which ultimately limits their ability to navigate this complex relationship. To address this, we introduce Morph, a flexible-size generative model for conditional and unconditional 3D molecular design based on geometric graphs. By dynamically adapting size, Morph can seamlessly integrate existing structural priors, like scaffolds, and significantly enhances property steering. We show that Morph matches current fixed-size state-of-the-art models while significantly increasing sampling flexibility. We demonstrate out-of-distribution generation in regimes where previous models fail, paving the way for enhanced generative modeling for molecular design.
  </details>

- **[AMUSE: Anytime Muon with Stable Gradient Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22432)**  `arXiv:2605.22432`  
  _Jueun Kim, Baekrok Shin, Jihun Yun, Beomhan Baek, Minhak Song, Chulhee Yun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern deep learning commonly relies on AdamW with prescribed learning rate schedules, but recent works challenge both components: Schedule-Free optimization removes explicit schedules via iterate averaging, and Muon improves the update geometry by orthogonalizing momentum for matrix parameters. Despite Muon's strong empirical performance, its underlying mechanism remains partially understood. We study Muon through the river-valley loss landscape, where useful training progress occurs along a flat, low-curvature bulk subspace (the river), while high-curvature dominant directions form steep valley walls that induce oscillations. We empirically show that while Muon's orthogonalization accelerates river progress by increasing the bulk component, it also amplifies dominant-direction noise, causing oscillatory trajectories. Building on this, we propose Anytime MUon with Stable gradient Evaluation (AMUSE), which integrates Muon's rapid bulk progress with the stabilizing effect of Schedule-Free averaging. AMUSE uses a time-varying interpolation coefficient that initially evaluates gradients near the fast Muon sequence for rapid adaptation, then gradually shifts toward the stable averaged sequence to suppress valley-wall oscillations. As a result, AMUSE requires no learning rate schedules and supports anytime training. Across vision tasks and large language model pretraining, AMUSE consistently improves the performance-iteration Pareto frontier over (Schedule-Free) AdamW and Muon.
  </details>

- **[Federated Learning by Utility-Constrained Stochastic Aggregation for Improving Rational Participation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18020)**  `arXiv:2605.18020`  
  _M Yashwanth, Arunabh Singh, Ashok Nayak, Sai Kiran Bulusu, Anirban Chakraborty_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated Learning (FL) algorithms implicitly assume that clients passively comply with server-side orchestration, sharing local model updates upon the server's request. However, this overlooks an important aspect of real-world cross-silo environments: clients are often rational agents who may prioritize their own interests, such as local model performance, over that of the global model. In settings with significant statistical heterogeneity, rational clients may opt out of the federation if the perceived benefits of collaboration fail to meet their local utility thresholds. Such attrition degrades global model performance and can lead to the collapse of the federated training process. To avoid such a scenario, in this work, we introduce FedUCA (Federated Learning by Utility-Constrained Stochastic Aggregation for Improving Rational Participation), a non-monetary framework that establishes a non-payment channel through which clients communicate their willingness to participate via their utilities. The server acts as an optimizer, identifying aggregation parameters that satisfy clients' individual rationality (IR) constraints to sustain the global model performance. FedUCA achieves this through a stochastic aggregation mechanism over Dirichlet-sampled mixture strategies, which induces an implicit utility surplus that helps satisfy participation constraints. Supported by theory and extensive experiments, FedUCA's focus on participation feasibility improves client retention and global model performance.
  </details>

- **[Shortcut Mitigation via Spurious-Positive Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13340)**  `arXiv:2605.13340`
