  _Phuong Quynh Le, Jörg Schlötterer, Sari Sadiya, Gemma Roig, Christin Seifert_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Shortcut mitigation strategies commonly rely on training data annotations, group-balanced held-out data or the presence of all groups, i.e., all combinations of (spurious) attributes and classes, in the training data. However, these requirements are rarely met in practice. We instead propose a method for targeted model analysis to identify a small set of instances in which the model relies on spurious attributes. Using that set and following ``this feature should not be used for prediction'' reasoning, we identify highly relevant neurons in an intermediate layer and regularize their impact. This ensures that models learn to depend on informative features rather than being right for the wrong reasons, thereby improving robustness without requiring additional balanced held-out data or annotations.
  </details>

- **[When Adaptation Fails: A Gradient-Based Diagnosis of Collapsed Gating in Vision-Language Prompt Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09549)**  `arXiv:2605.09549`  
  _Yunxuan Fang, Ziwei Zhang, Xinhe Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adaptive prompting mechanisms have been proposed to enhance vision-language models by dynamically tailoring prompts to inputs. However, in frozen few-shot prompt learning with CLIP-style backbones, we systematically observe that adaptive gates and prompt-selection modules often collapse: they produce nearly constant outputs, contribute negligible gradient signals, and frequently fail to outperform fixed prompts. To further explore this issue, we present a systematic diagnostic study to uncover the underlying causes and conditions of adaptation failure. Through controlled experiments across datasets and multiple prompt learning architectures, we identify two recurring failure modes: gradient magnitude imbalance and gate degradation. Our findings invite a re-examination of indiscriminately adding architectural complexity in parameter-efficient learning and clarify when prompt-level adaptive gating is, and is not, effective in this regime.
  </details>

- **[Diagnosing and Mitigating Domain Shift in Permission-Based Android Malware Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09028)**  `arXiv:2605.09028`  
  _Md Rafid Islam_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine learning-based Android malware detectors often fail in real-world deployment due to domain shift, where models trained on one data source perform poorly on applications from another. This paper presents a comprehensive study on the generalizability and interpretability of permission-based detectors under cross-domain conditions. Using two complementary datasets (PerMalDroid and NATICUSdroid) and five ensemble classifiers, we first establish an intra-domain baseline, where models achieve over 92% accuracy, and then quantify a severe asymmetric performance drop. While models trained on PerMalDroid generalize well to NATICUSdroid (86% accuracy), the reverse direction sees a drastic drop to 73% accuracy. Explainable AI analysis reveals bimodal feature distributions and shows that feature importance is highly unstable, with key permissions losing or gaining influence across domains. The predictive feature sets for different domains are fundamentally mismatched, as models rely on different, dataset-specific permissions. Most importantly, an ablation study demonstrates that for most models, training on a noisy feature set leads to poor generalization, confirming that domain-specific artifacts are a greater obstacle than missing features. To mitigate this, we validate a hybrid training strategy based on the intersection of common features and successfully recover cross-domain performance, achieving 88% accuracy on PerMalDroid and maintaining 97% on NATICUSdroid. These findings highlight the importance of explainable, cross-domain-robust malware detection systems and provide a practical pathway toward improving real-world deployment of permission-based Android malware detectors.
  </details>

- **[Retrieval of Coastal Biogeochemical Parameters From Near-Surface Hyperspectral Remote Sensing Reflectance Using Physics-Aware Meta-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05623)**  `arXiv:2605.05623`  
  _Yiqing Guo, Nagur R. C. Cherukuru, Eric A. Lehmann, S. L. Kesav Unnithan, Tim J. Malthus, Gemma Kerrisk, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hyperspectral in situ sensing has shown promise in retrieving aquatic water quality (WQ) parameters, such as total suspended solids (TSS), dissolved organic carbon (DOC), and total chlorophyll-a (TChl-a), for cost-effective management of coastal WQ. However, generalising such retrieval algorithms across water bodies remains challenging, as the relationship between remote sensing reflectance ($R_{rs}$) and WQ parameters can vary considerably from one region to another due to regional distinctions in environmental conditions and biogeochemistry that lead to different WQ ranges and bio-optical properties. In this study, we propose a two-stage physics-aware meta-learning framework for retrieving coastal WQ parameters from near-surface $R_{rs}$ observations. Unlike purely data-driven approaches trained solely on paired observations, the proposed framework incorporates physical knowledge through a two-stage learning process. In the first stage, a region-agnostic base model is pretrained using meta-learning on synthetic WQ--$R_{rs}$ samples generated by a bio-optical forward model parameterised with an in situ bio-optical spectral library. In the second stage, the pretrained model is fine-tuned using local samples to adapt to regional conditions. To evaluate the proposed approach, we collected in situ hyperspectral $R_{rs}$ and WQ measurements from five geographically distinct sites in Australian coastal waters.
  </details>

- **[Time series causal discovery with variable lags](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04081)**  `arXiv:2605.04081`  
  _Bruno Petrungaro, Anthony C. Constantinou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal Bayesian Networks (CBNs) are a powerful tool for reasoning under uncertainty about complex real-world problems. Such problems evolve over time, responding to external shocks as they occur. To support decision-making, CBNs require a cause-and-effect map of the variables under consideration, known as the network's structure. Learning the graphical structure of a causal model from data remains challenging; learning it from time-series data is even harder because dependencies may arise at different time lags. We construct a method that jointly learns a temporal ordering and a dominant lag for each selected ordered variable pair. Our contribution is a decomposable BIC-style objective score for mixed data that combines node-specific sample sizes with a penalty favouring recent lags. We optimise this objective using a Tabu search augmented with change-lag moves and greedy per-child lag tuning, while its node-wise decomposition supports cached and parallel neighbourhood evaluation. We establish acyclicity and complexity results and derive recovery limits when effects are distributed across multiple lags. In simulations, we show that the proposed method outperforms an inflexible lag-1 score-based learner in most evaluated settings, whereas most specialised methods achieve higher recovery accuracy on data that satisfy their assumptions. On a real-world UK COVID-19 dataset, the method learnt an expected short-lag-skewed structure; nevertheless, 46.8% of selected edges had lags greater than one, showing that longer delays can be retained when their improved fit offsets the penalty. Under its no-contemporaneous-edges and causal-sufficiency assumptions, the method provides a computationally tractable subroutine for edge-specific single-lag optimisation, complementing score-based causal discovery methods.
  </details>

- **[BoostLoRA: Growing Effective Rank by Boosting Adapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27308)**  `arXiv:2604.27308`  
  _Raviteja Anantha, Nick Levato, Layne C. Price_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods face a tradeoff between adapter size and expressivity: ultra-low-parameter adapters are confined to fixed low-rank subspaces, capping performance even with extended training. We propose BoostLoRA, a gradient-boosting framework that overcomes this limit by iteratively training and merging minimal adapters on the examples the current model gets wrong. A ROTATE SVD basis strategy assigns each round to an orthogonal subspace, so cumulative effective rank grows linearly with the number of rounds while each adapter remains ultra-low-rank. After merging, adapters are discarded, leaving zero inference overhead. On Qwen2.5-3B, BoostLoRA reaches 89.1% on GSM8K and 68.8% on MATH-500, surpassing both the best single-shot ultra-low parameter adapter (TinyLoRA) and full fine-tuning; on code generation it reaches 57.2% on MBPP and 80.4% on HumanEval while full fine-tuning drops below the zero-shot baseline. We also demonstrate cross-architecture transfer on protein binding classification with ESM2-650M and cross-entropy training. BoostLoRA is, to our knowledge, the first PEFT method whose effective rank grows with training, separating per-round parameter cost from total representational capacity.
  </details>

- **[Pruning Extensions and Efficiency Trade-Offs for Sustainable Time Series Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07953)**  `arXiv:2604.07953`  
  _Raphael Fischer, Angus Dempster, Sebastian Buschjäger, Matthias Jakobs, Urav Maniar, Geoffrey I. Webb_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series classification (TSC) at scale requires balancing predictive performance against resource consumption, which is subject to the given data, model, and hardware. This paper introduces a multi-dimensional evaluation framework that allows for explicitly benchmarking and exploring the resource-versus-quality trade-offs in TSC. To improve efficiency, we present a pruning strategy with bounded error for leading hybrid classifiers - Hydra and Quant - and moreover propose Hydrant, a novel extension that integrates interval representations with random convolutions and dictionary learning. With over 4000 evaluated configurations across 20 MONSTER datasets, 10 methods, and three compute setups, we systematically analyze how model design, hyperparameters, and hardware choices impact practical TSC performance. Our results show that pruning can significantly reduce energy consumption by up to 70% while only marginally lowering predictive quality. As such, our contributed evaluation framework, methodological extensions, experimental results, and software repository advance TSC toward sustainable and reproducible practice.
  </details>

- **[Resolving AdaBoost Cycling with LLMs: A Computer-Assisted Counterexample](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07055)**  `arXiv:2604.07055`  
  _Erik Y. Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We develop a computer-assisted counterexample to the open question of whether exhaustive AdaBoost always converges to a finite cycle, posed by Rudin, Schapire, and Daubechies in COLT 2012. The construction is based on a block-product of two small AdaBoost instances whose two factors share an exact period-2 orbit for their five-step branch maps, but whose linearized return maps have dominant eigenvalues with an irrational logarithmic ratio. This irrationality forces the sequence of instances that win each round of updates to have an irrational asymptotic frequency, which precludes convergence to a finite cycle. The resulting counterexample has 2,500 examples, weak-learning margin $1/5$, and a uniform initial distribution, and it holds under every maximizing tie rule. All finite computations in the proof are certified by rational arithmetic in SymPy, with SageMath verifying the main inequalities. We also document the collaborative workflow that led to the construction, in which GPT-5.4 Pro produced the key technical arguments; Claude Opus 4.6 summarized and critiqued different versions of that work, proposing new directions to pursue; and the authors orchestrated the process by selecting directions to prioritize, resolving ambiguities, refining each argument, and verifying the final solution. Later conversations with GPT-5.6 Sol and GPT-6 Astra substantially simplified the solution. We present this as one of the first in-depth case studies of LLM-assisted mathematical research, resolving a long-standing open problem from theoretical machine learning, and detail the advantages and challenges of such research.
  </details>

- **[Generative machine learning for protein design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.26378)**  `arXiv:2603.26378`  
  _Senura Hansaja Wanasekara, Manoj M Wagle, Minh-Duong Nguyen, Xiaochen Liu, Nguyen H. Tran, Manolis Kellis, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing proteins with specific biological functions is of considerable interest for both the development of new therapeutics and the engineering of enzymes, yet achieving the intended activity remains challenging. Generative artificial intelligence (AI) has emerged as a powerful tool to explore the vast space of possible designs by learning from protein sequence and structural data. Here, we review 59 generative approaches across six protein design tasks, including sequence generation, backbone generation, structure-conditioned sequence design, joint sequence-structure design, motif scaffolding, and binder and antibody design. We outline common generative approaches and protein representations and discuss how structural constraints and functional information are utilized during generation. This is followed by a discussion of the computational and experimental approaches used to evaluate designed proteins and what such assessments reveal about their properties. Finally, we discuss current limitations in designing proteins beyond well-represented families and incorporating experimental feedback into the design process. We expect that rapid advances in generative modeling will enable more reliable design of a wider range of protein structures and functions over the coming years.
  </details>

- **[FlashSampling: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15854)**  `arXiv:2603.15854`  
  _Tomas Ruiz, Zhen Qin, Yifan Zhang, Xuyang Shen, Yiran Zhong, Mengdi Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sampling from a categorical distribution is mathematically simple, but in large-vocabulary decoding, it often triggers extra memory traffic and extra kernels after the LM head. We present FlashSampling, an exact sampling primitive that fuses sampling into the LM-head matmul and never materializes the logits tensor in HBM. The method is simple: compute logits tile-by-tile on chip, add Gumbel noise, keep only one maximizer per row and per vocabulary tile, and finish with a small reduction over tiles. In tensor-parallel decoding, FlashSampling replaces the all-gather of logits with streaming peer-to-peer writes: This overlaps GPU-to-GPU communication with computation and HBM loads across up to 8 GPUs, with near-ideal scaling at large batch sizes. Our kernel is exact because argmax decomposes over partitions; grouped variants for online and tensor-parallel settings are exact by hierarchical factorization of the categorical distribution. FlashSampling demonstrates kernel-level speedups on decode workloads across 4 different datacenter GPUs (H100, H200, B200, B300), and in end-to-end vLLM experiments, it reduces time per output token by up to $10%$ on the models we test. These results show that exact sampling, with no approximation, can be integrated into the matmul itself, consolidating the bandwidth-bound sampling step in an efficient epilogue.
  </details>

- **[Efficiently Aligning Draft Models via Parameter- and Data-Efficient Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.09527)**  `arXiv:2603.09527`  
  _Luxi Lin, Zhihang Lin, Zhanpeng Zeng, Yuhao Chen, Qingyu Zhang, Jixiang Luo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference but suffers from performance degradation when target models are fine-tuned for specific domains. A naive solution is to retrain draft models for every target model, which is costly and inefficient. To address this, we introduce a practical parameter- and data-efficient framework named Efficient Draft Adaptation, abbreviated as EDA, for efficiently adapting draft models. EDA introduces three innovations: (1) a decoupled architecture that utilizes shared and private components to model the shared and target-specific output distributions separately, enabling parameter-efficient adaptation by updating only the lightweight private component; (2) a data regeneration strategy that utilizes the fine-tuned target model to regenerate training data, thereby improving the alignment between training and speculative decoding, leading to higher average acceptance length; (3) a sample selection mechanism that prioritizes high-value data for efficient adaptation. Our experiments show that EDA effectively restores speculative performance on fine-tuned models, achieving superior average acceptance lengths with significantly reduced training costs compared to full retraining. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ESENSC: A Polynomial-Time Axiomatic Alternative to SHAP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00496)**  `arXiv:2603.00496`  
  _Kazuhiro Hiraki, Shinichi Ishihara, Takumi Kongo, Junnosuke Shino_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose ESENSC, a computationally efficient and theoretically grounded alternative to SHAP. ESENSC is a deterministic, closed-form attribution rule that requires only linearly many coalition-value evaluations, satisfies the null-player property, and admits an axiomatic characterization based on efficiency, a restricted form of differential marginality, and explicit computationalthis http URL, on tabular prediction tasks using neural network and XGBoost models, ESENSC shows close agreement with exact SHAP in both attribution magnitude and feature rankings while requiring substantially less computation. Under the evaluated settings, it compares favorably with widely used sampling-based SHAP approximations. While exact SHAP requires exponentially many coalition evaluations, ESENSC scales linearly with the number of features. These results show that ESENSC offers a favorable trade-off between agreement with exact SHAP and computational cost, providing a practical and theoretically grounded alternative for feature attribution in high-dimensional applications.
  </details>

- **[ROCKET: Rapid Optimization via Calibration-guided Knapsack Enhanced Truncation for Efficient Model Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11008)**  `arXiv:2602.11008`  
  _Ammar Ali, Baher Mohammad, Denis Makhov, Dmitriy Shopkhoev, Magauiya Zhussip, Stamatios Lefkimmiatis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present ROCKET, a training-free model compression method that achieves state-of-the-art performance in comparison with factorization, structured-sparsification and dynamic compression baselines. Operating under a global compression budget, ROCKET comprises two key innovations: First, it formulates layer-wise compression allocation as a multi-choice knapsack problem, selecting the optimal compression level for each layer to minimize total reconstruction error while adhering to a target model size. Second, it introduces a single-step sparse matrix factorization inspired by dictionary learning: using only a small calibration set, it sparsifies weight coefficients based on activation-weights sensitivity and then updates the dictionary in closed form via least squares bypassing iterative optimization, sparse coding, or backpropagation entirely. ROCKET consistently outperforms existing compression approaches across different model architectures at 20-50\% compression rates. Notably, it retains over 90\% of the original model's performance at 30\% compression without any fine-tuning. Moreover, when applying a light fine-tuning phase, recovery is substantially enhanced: for instance, compressing Qwen3-14B to an 8B-parameter model and healing it with just 30 million tokens yields performance nearly on par with the original Qwen3-8B. The code for ROCKET is atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[NEAT: Neighborhood-Guided, Efficient, Autoregressive Set Transformer for 3D Molecular Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05844)**  `arXiv:2512.05844`  
  _Daniel Rose, Roxane Axel Jacob, Johannes Kirchmair, Thierry Langer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer-based autoregressive models offer an efficient alternative to diffusion- and flow-based approaches for generating 3D molecules. One challenge remains: standard transformer architectures require a sequential ordering of tokens, which is not inherently defined for the atoms in a molecule. Prior works have addressed this by using canonical atom orderings. However, these approaches are not permutation invariant w.r.t. atoms and bias next-token prediction towards ordering conventions. We overcome this limitation by introducing a novel neighborhood-guided training strategy. Our model, NEAT (Neighborhood-Guided, Efficient, Autoregressive Set Transformer) treats molecular graphs as sets of atoms and learns an order-agnostic distribution over admissible tokens at the graph boundary, thereby ensuring atom-level permutation invariance. NEAT achieves state-of-the-art generation quality on the QM9 and GEOM-Drugs datasets, enables seamless completion of molecular prefixes, and offers a significant speed advantage over existing baselines.
  </details>

- **[CS-54k: A Paper-Grounded Corpus and Benchmark for AI Research QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.20279)**  `arXiv:2510.20279`  
  _Penghao Wang, Yuhao Zhou, Mengxuan Wu, Ziheng Qin, Bangyuan Zhu, Shengbin Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being used to support computer science research. To contribute reliably, these systems must be able to work with information distributed across research papers and across different aspects of the research process. However, large-scale resources that systematically organize such information into structured supervision for both model evaluation and post-training remain limited. To address this gap, we propose a systematic paper-to-QA construction pipeline that organizes information from AI research papers into structured QA pairs supported by source evidence. Using this pipeline, we construct CS-54k, a 54k-pair corpus derived from 14,474 openly licensed AI research papers. The corpus covers eight recurring paper components, including research domain, prior work, existing challenges, motivation, findings and assumptions, methods, experimental settings, and experimental results. We further derive two complementary subsets: CS-4k, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs on paper-derived research QA, and CS-50k, a companion corpus for post-training. Extensive evaluations on CS-4k reveal substantial performance differences across existing LLMs and paper components, with experimental settings and results remaining particularly challenging. Moreover, post-training on CS-50k substantially improves Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct on CS-4k, demonstrating the practical utility of structured, paper-derived supervision. Together, CS-4k and CS-50k provide a unified and scalable resource for benchmarking and improving LLMs on research paper question answering.
  </details>

- **[Interpret Policies in Deep Reinforcement Learning using SILVER with RL-Guided Labeling: A Model-level Approach to High-dimensional and Multi-action Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.19244)**  `arXiv:2510.19244`  
  _Yiyu Qian, Liyuan Zhao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep reinforcement learning (RL) achieves remarkable performance but lacks interpretability, limiting trust in policy behavior. The existing SILVER framework (Li, Siddique, and Cao 2025) explains RL policy via Shapley-based regression but remains restricted to low-dimensional, binary-action domains. We propose SILVER with RL-guided labeling, an enhanced variant that extends SILVER to multi-action and high-dimensional environments by incorporating the RL policy's own action outputs into the boundary points identification. Our method first extracts compact feature representations from image observations, performs SHAP-based feature attribution, and then employs RL-guided labeling to generate behaviorally consistent boundary datasets. Surrogate models, such as decision trees and regression-based functions, are subsequently trained to interpret RL policy's decision structure. We evaluate the proposed framework on two Atari environments using three deep RL algorithms and conduct human-subject study to assess the clarity and trustworthiness of the derived interpretable policy. Results show that our approach maintains competitive task performance while substantially improving transparency and human understanding of agent behavior. This work advances explainable RL by transforming SILVER into a scalable and behavior-aware framework for interpreting deep RL agents in high-dimensional, multi-action settings.
  </details>

- **[Graph Your Own Prompt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23373)**  `arXiv:2509.23373`  
  _Xi Ding, Lei Wang, Piotr Koniusz, Yongsheng Gao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose Graph Consistency Regularization (GCR), a novel framework that injects relational graph structures, derived from model predictions, into the learning process to promote class-aware, semantically meaningful feature representations. Functioning as a form of self-prompting, GCR enables the model to refine its internal structure using its own outputs. While deep networks learn rich representations, these often capture noisy inter-class similarities that contradict the model's predicted semantics. GCR addresses this issue by introducing parameter-free Graph Consistency Layers (GCLs) at arbitrary depths. Each GCL builds a batch-level feature similarity graph and aligns it with a global, class-aware masked prediction graph, derived by modulating softmax prediction similarities with intra-class indicators. This alignment enforces that feature-level relationships reflect class-consistent prediction behavior, acting as a semantic regularizer throughout the network. Unlike prior work, GCR introduces a multi-layer, cross-space graph alignment mechanism with adaptive weighting, where layer importance is learned from graph discrepancy magnitudes. This allows the model to prioritize semantically reliable layers and suppress noisy ones, enhancing feature quality without modifying the architecture or training procedure. GCR is model-agnostic, lightweight, and improves semantic structure across various networks and datasets. Experiments show that GCR promotes cleaner feature structure, stronger intra-class cohesion, and improved generalization, offering a new perspective on learning from prediction structure. [Project website](this https URL) [Code](this https URL)
  </details>

- **[Structured Basis Function Networks: Loss-Centric Multi-Hypothesis Ensembles with Controllable Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.02792)**  `arXiv:2509.02792`  
  _Alejandro Rodriguez Dominguez, Muhammad Shahzad, Xia Hong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ensemble learning and multi-hypothesis prediction are two complementary paradigms for improving predictive performance and robustness. This work introduces the Structured Basis Function Network (s-BFN), a loss-aware ensemble framework that unifies these paradigms through a supervised aggregation mechanism motivated by the loss-induced Bregman-centroid geometry of prediction space. The approach constructs a structured dataset of base predictions and trains a parametric combiner directly against the observed labels, with the centroidal formulation serving as a geometric reference rather than an exact centroid solver, while a diversity-control parameter regulates specialization among predictors. The proposed framework is evaluated across regression and classification tasks, including tabular data, image recognition, and large-scale temporally structured video classification. Empirical results show that s-BFN provides strong performance across the evaluated regimes and is competitive with the aggregation baselines considered in the study. The strongest gains are observed in higher-dimensional and temporally structured settings, where aggregation capacity and controlled diversity are jointly beneficial, while simpler tabular regimes exhibit earlier saturation. These results highlight a capacity-complexity-diversity trade-off rather than a strictly monotonic relationship between diversity, ensemble size, and accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Robust Surrogate Modeling for Explainable Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01925)**  `arXiv:2508.01925`  
  _Zhuomin Chen, Jingchao Ni, Hojat Allah Salehi, Xu Zheng, Dongsheng Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have achieved remarkable performance in a wide range of graph-related learning tasks. However, explaining their predictions remains a challenging problem, especially due to the mismatch between the graphs used during training and those encountered during explanation. Most existing methods optimize soft edge masks on weighted graphs to highlight important substructures, but these graphs differ from the unweighted graphs on which GNNs are trained. This distributional shift leads to unreliable gradients and degraded explanation quality, especially when generating small, sparse subgraphs. To address this issue, we propose explaining a robust surrogate whose explanations provide a faithful proxy for those of the original model. Specifically, we train this surrogate model through an iterative re-weighting strategy that alternates between identifying explanation subgraphs and adapting the model to the resulting weighted graph distribution, supporting stable explanation refinement while preserving the validity of the soft-mask relaxation. We evaluate our method on multiple benchmark datasets using different GNN backbones and explanation methods. Experimental results show that our method consistently improves explanation quality and can be flexibly integrated with different architectures.
  </details>

- **[AbFlowNet: Optimizing Antibody-Antigen Binding Energy via Diffusion-GFlowNet Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12358)**  `arXiv:2505.12358`  
  _Abrar Rahman Abir, Haz Sameen Shahgir, Md Rownok Zahan Ratul, Md Toki Tahmid, Greg Ver Steeg, Yue Dong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complementarity Determining Regions (CDRs) are critical segments of an antibody that facilitate binding to specific antigens. Current computational methods for CDR design utilize reconstruction losses and do not jointly optimize binding energy, a crucial metric for antibody efficacy. Rather, binding energy optimization is done through computationally expensive Online Reinforcement Learning (RL) pipelines rely heavily on unreliable binding energy estimators. In this paper, we propose AbFlowNet, a novel generative framework that integrates GFlowNet with Diffusion models. By framing each diffusion step as a state in the GFlowNet framework, AbFlowNet jointly optimizes standard diffusion losses and binding energy by directly incorporating energy signals into the training process, thereby unifying diffusion and reward optimization in a single procedure. Experimental results show that AbFlowNet outperforms the base diffusion model by 3.06% in amino acid recovery, 20.40% in geometric reconstruction (RMSD), and 3.60% in binding energy improvement ratio. ABFlowNet also decreases Top-1 total energy and binding energy errors by 24.8% and 38.1% without pseudo-labeling the test dataset or using computationally expensive online RL regimes.
  </details>

- **[Bidirectional Information Flow (BIF) - A Sample Efficient Hierarchical Gaussian Process for Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11294)**  `arXiv:2505.11294`  
  _Juan D. Guerra, Thomas Garbay, Numa Dancause, Guillaume Lajoie, Marco Bonizzato_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical Gaussian Process (H-GP) models divide problems into different subtasks, allowing for different components to address each part, making them well-suited for problems with inherent compositional structure. However, existing H-GP frameworks typically employ one-way information sharing - either top-down or bottom-up - which limits sample efficiency and slows convergence. We propose Bidirectional Information Flow (BIF), which establishes continuous two-way communication. BIF retains the modular structure of hierarchical models - the parent conditions its own posterior on child summaries, treating them as structured priors - while introducing top-down feedback to softly decompose environment observations from the parent into sub-responses. This mutual exchange improves sample efficiency, enables robust training, and allows modular reuse of learned subtask models. We prove analytically the regret of a GP with a learned kernel scales linearly with the mismatch to the true kernel, tightening in the hierarchical case to the sum of child-level errors. Ablation shows removing the downward pathway collapses child $R^2$ by up to 58%. Across synthetic, neurostimulation, and HPO benchmarks, BIF achieves up to $4\times$ higher parent $R^2$ and roughly 100% AUC improvement over vanilla GPBO, and outperforms or is the runner-up in 38/40 tests compared to the state-of-the-art baselines while supporting modular child transfer to novel composite tasks.
  </details>

- **[Robust Long-Tailed Learning under Label Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2108.11569)**  `arXiv:2108.11569`  
  _Tong Wei, Jiang-Xin Shi, Min-Ling Zhang, Yu-Feng Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-tailed learning aims to enhance the generalization performance of underrepresented tail classes. However, previous methods have largely overlooked the prevalence of noisy labels in training data. In this paper, we address the challenge of noisy labels in long-tailed learning. We identify a critical issue: the commonly used small-loss noisy label detection criterion fails to perform effectively in long-tailed class distributions. This failure arises from the inherent bias of deep neural networks, which tend to misclassify tail class examples as head classes, leading to unreliable loss calculations. To mitigate this, we propose a novel small-distance criterion that leverages the robustness of learned representations, enabling more accurate identification of correctly-labeled examples across both head and tail classes. Additionally, to improve training for tail classes, we replace discrete pseudo-labels with label distributions for examples flagged as noisy, resulting in significant performance gains. Based on these contributions, we introduce the Robust Long-Tail learning framework, designed to train models that are resilient to both class imbalance and noisy labels. Extensive experiments on benchmark and real-world datasets demonstrate that our approach outperforms previous methods, offering substantial performance improvements.
  </details>

### Multiagent Systems 🌐

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[When Should a Virtual Patient Ask? RISP-Guided Proactive Information Seeking in Multi-Agent Patient Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18183)**  `arXiv:2611.18183`  
  _Jingyao Cai, Shuaijun Liu, Abdul Rehman, Xiaowan Li, Qintian, Thomas Dolby, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Virtual patient (VP) systems support nursing communication training by providing simulated encounters in which learners practise explaining care and responding to patient concerns. Although large language models (LLMs) support flexible dialogue, current approaches give less explicit attention to how patients' information needs develop and guide the content and timing of their questions. We draw on the Risk Information Seeking and Processing (RISP) model, which relates information seeking to perceived risk and the gap between perceived knowledge and desired understanding. Guided by this perspective, we propose the RISP Multi-Agent System (RISP-MAS), a VP system for patient-initiated information seeking. The system links ongoing assessment of the patient's perceptions and information needs to coordinated decisions about whether to seek information, when to ask and which uncertainty to address. Evaluation of nursing student-VP interactions suggested that RISP-MAS exhibited plausible patient information-seeking behaviour, as indicated by expert assessments, while student feedback reflected positive interaction experiences. This work extends patient simulation by connecting a psychological account of information needs to explicit decisions about question initiation. The approach offers nursing students opportunities to practise recognising unresolved patient concerns and tailoring explanations to patients' information needs.
  </details>

- **[Only as Safe as the Weakest Agent: A Break That Appears Only When the System Is Multi-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17520)**  `arXiv:2611.17520`  
  _Keegan Wang, Anantika Mannby_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Major industry multi-agent systems like Codex and Claude Code can be broken by attacks that have long been unsuccessful against modern frontier models. AI Safety as a discipline has largely focused on finding the bounds and vulnerabilities of AI models used in isolation. Yet as humans increasingly deploy these models as agents and multi-agent systems, the same attacks that had been thought to be patched when using a model in isolation can break these same models under specific circumstances. We show that these system breaks are not purely theoretical, but can break Claude Code, Codex, and a reference harness, all unmodified and used with their own sub-agent mechanisms. We show the attack by asking agents to answer questions from a store of files on disk in which we have planted a forged record. Searching the store themselves, the strong model orchestrators commit almost zero harmful revisions in over 1,000 trials. However, if that same orchestrator model delegates to a cheaper sub-agent, which is what these harnesses are built to do, the same models on the same facts fail on 20/180, 44/180, 75/180 and 553/900. Thus, even when the system is driven by a strong model, the system's safety lands near the weak reader's, not the orchestrator's. Simulations over 18 models show the same effect in the delivery channel where five aggregators, who on their own were almost never unsafe, repeat a corrupted sub-agent's error in 751/757 trials. The safety of real-world agentic systems that use strong orchestrator agents and weaker sub-agents, and multi-agent systems in general, is defined not by the safety of the strong agent but by the safety of the weakest agent.
  </details>

- **[From Emergence to Reversibility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09054)**  `arXiv:2611.09054`  
  _Zhengye Han, Linda Ng Boyle_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems become capable through institutional mechanisms, such as rules that route information to agents that act on it. These mechanisms are also natural places to restrict the system. Yet as agents learn, share memory, delegate, and repair failures, the collective can find other paths to the same effects. A restriction on the original mechanism can then keep operating but no longer stops them, while routine performance and compliance still passes. We propose \textbf{Reversible Multi-agent Systems} as a research agenda: collectives that grow while an authorized supervisor can still restrict specified effects, keep them restricted as agents adapt, and reconstitute useful cooperation under renewed authority. What is reversed is the ability to produce a specified effect; restrictions must hold even if learned knowledge persists. The goal is to preserve effective intervention, not a particular mechanism: at every step of growth, some authorized intervention must keep a specified prohibited effect rare while maintaining required service. We identify three research problems: tracking what capabilities depend on, handing off control before an old mechanism loses its effect, and separating legitimate repair from unauthorized reconstruction. We outline how to evaluate control across growth paths, back control claims with evidence, and allocate the rights to restrict and restore.
  </details>

- **[CPM-MultiAgent: A CPM-Grounded Appraisal Framework for Simulating Dynamic Patient Emotional Evolution in Healthcare Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07824)**  `arXiv:2607.07824`  
  _Jingyao Cai, Shuaijun Liu, Xiaowan Li, Yutong Guo, Abdul Rehman, Qintian, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) enable patient simulation for healthcare communication training, where learners practice responding to clinical information, concerns, and emotional reactions. However, existing patient simulation and affective dialogue systems often represent patient emotions as static attributes or focus on recognizing emotions and generating empathetic responses. They provide limited support for modeling how a simulated patient's emotional state evolves in response to a healthcare professional's communication. Changes in patient emotion constitute interactional feedback that learners must recognize and respond to across dialogue turns. We draw on the Component Process Model (CPM), a psychological theory that explains emotion as a dynamic process shaped by event appraisal. We propose CPM-MultiAgent, a CPM-grounded multi-agent framework for dynamic patient emotion simulation in healthcare communication training. The framework uses an emotion taxonomy informed by medical literature and expert input, and updates emotional states through healthcare-related trigger identification, structured CPM appraisal, and emotion-state integration, accounting for the patient's profile, clinical context, and prior emotional state. We evaluate the framework through student-led conversations and controlled trigger cases, combining nursing-expert assessments, LLM-judge comparisons, component ablations, backbone checks, and student feedback.
  </details>

### Robotics 🤖

<details open><summary>Click to Collapse</summary>

- **[Training Reasoning Models in Simulation to Natively Use Real Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18562)**  `arXiv:2611.18562`  
  _Jiaming Tang, Zhuoyang Zhang, Junxian Guo, Fangzhou Zhao, Yilong Zhao, Zekai Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot-use agents (RUAs) have emerged as a promising approach to general-purpose robot control. However, strong performance has so far been achieved only by frontier proprietary models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.2, whereas open-weight models remain substantially less capable. We show that an open-weight 27B reasoning model can be trained to use real robots natively through reinforcement learning, entirely in simulation and without demonstrations. Like a computer-use agent (CUA), the model observes the environment, reasons in a chain of thought, and generates the next end-effector pose, without any harness. We build a Post-training Engine for Agentic Robotics (PEAR) and use it to train the model on simulated robot tasks at scale with robot-use reinforcement learning (RURL), mirroring how frontier models acquire computer use. We find that the skills acquired in simulation generalize well beyond the training tasks: they transfer to an unseen robot, to the real world, and to novel instructions. After training on LIBERO and RoboDojo in simulation, the model succeeds in 60% of our trials on a real dual-arm robot, whereas the base model succeeds in none. On LIBERO, training raises the success rate from 1.6% to 74.4%, approaching GPT-6 Astra and GPT-6.1 Sol at maximum reasoning effort. Compared with $\pi_{0.5}$, a vision-language-action model fine-tuned on demonstrations, our model is substantially more robust to changes in the task itself. On LIBERO-Pro, $\pi_{0.5}$ succeeds in only 0.75% of episodes when assigned a new task in a familiar scene whereas our model succeeds in 57.4%. Finally, the model retains its general capabilities, with no measurable degradation on nine reasoning, coding and vision benchmarks. These results highlight robot-use reinforcement learning as a promising component of foundation-model post-training.
  </details>

- **[Modeling the Frictional Contact of Continuum Robots with Environments through Linear Complementarity Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18546)**  `arXiv:2611.18546`  
  _Jia Shen, Junhyoung Ha, Yue Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate friction modeling in continuum robots is essential for precise motion control and path planning. However, current approaches make predictions only for the mechanism's internal friction that is limited by the boundary forces of the Coulomb friction cone, which fail to characterize the static friction and motion hysteresis. In this paper, we propose a novel friction model based on complementarity constraints under the Coulomb friction assumption. This model predicts various friction effects caused by environmental interactions, including both static and sliding frictions and hysteresis behavior, as well as the corresponding robot deformation induced by these interactions, which are crucial for the practical motion planning and control of continuum robots. Additionally, real-time implementation of this model can be achieved, meeting the demand for computational efficiency in robotic applications. Experimental validations demonstrated that this model is able to reduce the maximum tip position error from 45.4 mm in the frictionless model to 5.85 mm in our friction-included model with a 287.9 mm-long tendon-driven continuum robot.
  </details>

- **[Disturbance-Aware Informative Planning in Spatiotemporal Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18505)**  `arXiv:2611.18505`  
  _Kaleb Ben Naveed, Haejoon Lee, Anouck Girard, Dimitra Panagou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous robots are widely used for persistent monitoring of spatiotemporal environments, where quantities of interest (QoI) change across space and time and require repeated measurements. In practice, robot motion can be affected by external disturbances that vary across the environment. When such external disturbances are not considered in trajectory optimization, it can lead to inaccurate predictions of robot motion and, consequently, the information acquired about the QoI during execution. However, estimating disturbances throughout the environment can be inefficient, as disturbances in regions with little overlap with monitoring trajectories have limited impact on monitoring performance. We therefore propose a two-stage informative planning framework for unknown, spatially varying but time-invariant disturbances, combining informed disturbance sampling with disturbance-aware trajectory optimization. First, we identify where QoI measurements are needed and generate alternative paths to these locations using the environment geometry. We then prioritize disturbance measurements based on their expected reduction of disturbance uncertainty along these paths. Second, rather than optimizing information acquisition based on nominal robot motion, we incorporate the learned disturbance belief into trajectory optimization to account for its effects on robot motion and information acquisition. Across four simulation scenarios, the proposed method reduces disturbance-learning time by 25.0--52.3 percent compared with environment-wide estimation and identifies paths that appear costly under nominal dynamics but become preferable when disturbances are considered.
  </details>

- **[In Situ Monitoring and Predictive Modeling of Harmful Algal Blooms with Autonomous Surface Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18501)**  `arXiv:2611.18501`  
  _Nenyi Kweku Nkensen Dadson, Jean Paul Gonzalez, Daniel P. Arabie, Ryan J. Cedel, Dorin Boldor, Corina Barbalata_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harmful Algal Blooms (HABs) pose significant environmental, economic, and public-health challenges in coastal and inland water bodies, creating a need for scalable methods to collect spatially distributed water-quality data. This paper presents an autonomous surface vessel-based sensing system for in-situ water-quality monitoring related to harmful algal bloom detection. The system integrates a BlueBoat Autonomous Surface Vessel (ASV), a motorized winch for depth-controlled sonde deployment, a multiparameter water-quality sonde, side-scan sonar, and an event-camera payload. Autonomous waypoint missions were used to collect measurements across selected sampling regions and multiple depths, enabling both horizontal and vertical characterization of environmental conditions. Field deployments were conducted in Lake Pontchartrain, Louisiana, with additional measurements collected in False River, Louisiana to increase dataset diversity. The collected measurements included algal pigment indicators, nutrient concentration, turbidity, temperature, and other water-quality parameters relevant to bloom formation. In addition, complementary sonar and event-camera data were collected during vehicle operation to provide contextual environmental information. A machine-learning classification approach was also evaluated using the collected water-quality measurements to support HAB and non-HAB classification. The results demonstrate the feasibility of using a modular ASV platform for repeatable, multi-modal water-quality data collection and support future development of autonomous monitoring systems.
  </details>

- **[Toward Resilient Self-Driving Laboratories through Telerobotic Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18496)**  `arXiv:2611.18496`  
  _Ziheng Wu, Adrian Vecina Tercero, Andre A. Fatehi, Abhik Sarkar, Brian Giera, Eric B. Duoss, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) accelerate scientific research by integrating robotics, automated experimentation, and machine learning to execute experiments autonomously. Biology and chemistry have adopted these systems. However, increasing SDL complexity heightens susceptibility to unexpected faults in automated workflows, highlighting the need for efficient recovery mechanisms. In this study, we investigate telerobotic interventions to enhance SDL resilience against unexpected failures. We developed a teleoperation interface enabling human operators to resolve disruptions during automated tasks. The interface enables precise control of a six-degree-of-freedom (DOF) robotic arm (UR3e) using a 3D mouse (3Dconnexion SpaceMouse Pro). Two velocity-based controller strategies were evaluated: (i) a coupled mode mapping all six DOFs to the 3D mouse's central knob and (ii) a decoupled mode separating translational and rotational motions. To evaluate the framework, teleoperators intervened across three SDL fault scenarios. In the first study, comparative trials showed that the decoupled controller was associated with shorter completion times and reduced rotational motion. In a second study, teleoperators performed an aiming task with and without reference-path guidance. Users were more target-driven and risk-tolerant without guidance but showed behavioral differences under different guidance strategies. These findings suggest that controller configuration and reference-path design influence operator performance during SDL fault recovery, informing teleoperation interface optimization. This work demonstrates the potential feasibility of teleoperation as a human-in-the-loop recovery mechanism for SDL workflows, combining robotic precision with human adaptability and problem-solving. These findings also provide a foundation for future studies in shared autonomy and semi-autonomous intervention.
  </details>

- **[When Will You Hand It Over? A Hidden Start Delay Makes Self-Play Handover Robust to Unfamiliar Partners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18491)**  `arXiv:2611.18491`  
  _Mohammad Khoshnazar, Mike Degany, Deyuan Qu, Qing Yang, Matyas Szanto_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cooperative self-play can teach a humanoid to receive an object without any human data, but the partner it meets after training is never the one it trained with; we show that a fluent self-play handover policy breaks with unfamiliar partners, identify the main cause, and repair it. In a physics-based parcel handover between a Unitree G1 and a learned giver, both trained by alternating PPO self-play, the receiver completes 95% and 90% of handovers with its own partner (two training seeds) but only 31% with scripted partners it has never met. Paired interventions isolate the cause: under a shared reward the learned giver always starts its offer at once, so the receiver learns a single rendezvous, and delaying its own partner by one second cuts success from 95% to 6% (87% to 34% in the second seed). We introduce hidden start-delay randomisation (HSD), a one-line rule on the learned partner that the receiver never observes. After 300 further updates, success with unseen scripted partners rises from 31% to 80% in both seeds, against 35--41% for a budget-matched control. The gain holds on a fresh scenario seed, on eleven held-out partner conditions and on partners that start later than any training delay; it cuts high-force contact three- to five-fold and needs no retraining of the partner.
  </details>

- **[Heavy Metal Acrobatics: Learning Dynamic Control for Skid-Steer Loaders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18365)**  `arXiv:2611.18365`  
  _Pierre-Jean Lorentz, Pascal Egli, Edo Jelavic, Cliff Li, Ramitha Sundar, Harikrishnan Suresh, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automation of heavy machinery offers the opportunity to improve productivity in mechanized industries such as construction, mining, and material handling. Yet, automation of such machines is challenged by their complex dynamics, in part because of hydraulic actuation that involves fluids, flexible elements, and nonlinear delays. A concomitant challenge is the size and weight of these machines, which can make experiments on the physical systems exceedingly dangerous. Here, we show that \ac{RL} in simulation, followed by transfer of the learned policies from simulation to reality, can successfully automate acrobatic maneuvers that push a heavy construction machine to the limits of its actuation and have only previously been accomplished by a small number of elite human operators in official world-record performances. We trained policies that perform autonomous acrobatic maneuvers on a John Deere 332G, an operational 5-tonne skid-steer loader. The policies can autonomously swing the machine up onto two wheels and, while balancing on two wheels, drive over rough terrain, trace out specified trajectories, spin around, and swing the bucket back and forth before landing it safely back onto four wheels. This work demonstrates that \ac{RL} and sim-to-real transfer enable dynamic autonomous control of heavy machinery, directly contributing to greater automation in heavy equipment sectors. Videos:this https URLThis is the author's version of the work. It is posted here by permission of the AAAS for personal use, not for redistribution. The definitive version was published in Science Robotics on 11 November 2026, DOI:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Envisioning Agentic Robotics: A New Era of Robotic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18359)**  `arXiv:2611.18359`  
  _Grid Robotics, Qingfeng Lan, Duo Li, Siyao Li, Yiheng Lyu, Shijian Lu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The release of GPT-6 Astra marks the emergence of a new paradigm in robotics: agentic robotics. Within weeks, frontier models with agentic harnesses matched or exceeded task-specific policies on many embodied benchmarks. Instead of training a dedicated policy for each family of tasks, an agentic robotic system applies a foundation model to open-world embodied tasks. It operates in a closed loop: observing the environment, interpreting user intent, reasoning about the task, invoking skills and tools, verifying outcomes, and adapting its strategy. Despite these advances, the community has yet to converge on a shared definition, a reliable evaluation framework, or a research roadmap. We argue that Embodied System 2, realized by a foundation model together with an embodied harness, forms the decision-making core of agentic robotics. Its central challenge is to translate general-purpose reasoning into effective and verifiable actions under physical constraints such as limited reaction time, mechanical limits, unpredictable environment changes, and irreversible consequences. Motivated by this perspective, we propose an evaluation framework that measures both model capabilities and closed-loop system performance. Furthermore, we identify the principles that carry over from digital to physical agents and the challenges that must be addressed to achieve reliable physical autonomy. On top of the Embodied System 2 and the evaluation framework, we envision a research roadmap toward sustained autonomy: the ability to perform work safely and reliably with progressively broader responsibility, from executing individual primitives to fulfilling standing roles.
  </details>

- **[PROOF: Perceptive Robustness via Out-of-Distribution Fallback for Legged Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18305)**  `arXiv:2611.18305`  
  _Siyuan Wang, Dongjie Zhu, Jinxuan Lu, Wenjun Xu, Jiankun Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-based perceptive locomotion has enabled legged robots to achieve agile and terrain-aware locomotion across complex environments. However, the reliability of such systems heavily depends on visual observations, which are inherently susceptible to occlusion, reflective surfaces, and illumination variations, often resulting in catastrophic locomotion failures under degraded perception conditions. In this paper, we present PROOF (Perceptive Robustness via Out-of-Distribution Fallback for Legged Locomotion), a perceptive locomotion framework that enables robust locomotion under severe visual disturbances. Our method adopts a two-stage teacher-student learning paradigm that explicitly models perception reliability: the teacher learns both perception-driven and proprioceptive fallback behaviors, while the student predicts exteroceptive features with uncertainty and suppresses them under out-of-distribution inputs. We evaluate our method on a Unitree Go2 quadruped equipped with a RealSense D435 depth camera in real-world settings. Under normal visual conditions, our method enables the robot to successfully perform high-dynamic parkour maneuvers, including traversing large gaps, elevated platforms, and narrow beam terrains. Under severe visual degradation, such as heavy occlusion and strong illumination changes, the robot continues to maintain stable and robust locomotion performance.
  </details>

- **[Objectives as Policies: Vision-Language Model Cost Programs for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18298)**  `arXiv:2611.18298`  
  _Zihao Lu, Trong Gia Hung Nguyen, Fuquan Wang, Yifan Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong semantic and spatial reasoning capabilities in 3D environments. Recently, connecting VLMs with model-predictive controllers (MPC) has emerged as a paradigm for combining strong semantic reasoning with physics-based planning. However, connecting these two for contact-rich robotic manipulation requires a task representation that bridges the two and captures how physical interactions can be reasoned by VLMs. In this work, we introduce Objectives as Policies (OaP), a framework that connects a pretrained VLM to a shared sampling-based MPC through an editable objective program. Each program specifies a sequence of stages for completing a task, combining running and terminal costs from a cost library with stage completion conditions. Given a natural-language task instruction and scene observation, we build a simulation-based digital twin through real-to-sim, and the VLM generates an objective program that is executed by the MPC in the simulation. The program is iteratively updated by the VLM based on MPC rollouts, before being deployed in the real world. We evaluate our framework in both simulation and the real world on four tabletop manipulation tasks, and show that it outperforms strong baselines. Additional videos and examples are available on the project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[NPU-Accelerated GNSS Signal Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18186)**  `arXiv:2611.18186`  
  _Taro Suzuki_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On small single-board computers (SBCs), GNSS signal acquisition is a major CPU load. This paper proposes using the neural processing unit (NPU) of an SBC not for machine learning but as an accelerator for the parallel code search in GNSS signal acquisition. The discrete Fourier transform (DFT) used in acquisition is decomposed by the four-step DFT into matrix products of small DFTs and expressed by $1\times1$ convolutions, element-wise products, and transposes, which NPUs execute efficiently. Furthermore, we propose a method that divides the DFT, the non-coherent integration, and the peak detection between the CPU and the NPU according to the configuration of the NPU (an NPU with its own memory connected via PCIe, or an NPU sharing memory with the CPU). The proposed method is implemented in the open-source software GNSS receiver Pocket SDR and evaluated on a Raspberry Pi 5 combined with the external AX8850 NPU and on the NPU built into the SoC (RK3588) of the ROCK 5C, a small SBC of comparable size. Compared with acquisition by an optimized CPU implementation, the acquisition of short codes on the Raspberry Pi 5 with the AX8850 is 3.0-3.8 times faster. In the evaluation of the whole receiver, the number of tracking channels that reach their first lock within 30 s with the NPU is 1.5 times that with the CPU, and the time until 90% of the channels are locked is reduced from 66 s to 26 s. Furthermore, because the CPU load of acquisition is reduced, the number of tracking channels that can be processed in real time increases from about 175 to about 230. These results show that using the NPU on SBCs with limited CPU resources is effective for achieving both GNSS signal acquisition and tracking. The implementation is publicly available as open source under the name PocketSDR-NPU.
  </details>

- **[ESI: An Embodied Self-Improvement Flywheel for LLM-VLA Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18176)**  `arXiv:2611.18176`  
  _Yundi Cai, Zihan Wang, Chenxi Han, Yi Cheng, Houde Liu, Zilei Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Independently pretrained language models and vision-language-action (VLA) policies provide complementary building blocks for embodied agents, but connecting them does not automatically produce coordinated behavior. The VLA may generate actions that diverge from the LLM's intended subtask and, upon regaining control after a correction, undo the promising state restored by the LLM. We introduce ESI (Embodied Self-Improvement), an automated flywheel that uses environmental interaction to align reasoning and action. ESI treats the modular agent as the unit of post-training, repeatedly refining the prompts that guide the LLM, the shared skill interface that expresses its intent, and the VLA parameters that realize that intent, then redeploying these components together. Our key insight is that collaboration itself can supply supervision for improving this interface. The LLM's subtask decisions provide semantic supervision for what the policy should execute, while its effective corrections and subsequent student continuations provide action-level supervision for recovery and handoff. The student also learns from its own effective execution, allowing locally useful experience to contribute even when the overall episode fails. On ten selected RoboDojo tasks, ESI achieves a mean Score of 62.03 and a mean success rate of 49.41% using an LLM-VLA agent without generating task-specific control code. On both metrics, it outperforms the published reference results of all hybrid embodied agents, including the Agent+WAM system GO-3 Nano Preview (47.27 Score and 36.60% success), as well as all compared code-based methods.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Language Models as Spatial Priors for Object-Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18123)**  `arXiv:2611.18123`  
  _Jun Miura_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic exploration methods for object-goal navigation use large language models (LLMs) to score how strongly a target object is associated with a room type or with a nearby object. This paper considers obtaining from the LLM a spatial prior instead: a distribution over the position of the target in a room. We evaluate whether two open-weight LLMs, Qwen3.5-27B and Gemma-4-31B-it, can provide such a prior. Using real room layouts from CubiCasa5K and ScanNet, we measure the likelihood the models assign to real and perturbed layouts, and the layouts they generate. The object frequencies and room sizes of the generated rooms follow the room type, but the proximity of the objects to the walls does not follow the differences between room types found in real rooms. The likelihood distinguishes real layouts from perturbed ones partly through their geometry and partly through the agreement between the coordinates and a field that states the wall each object touches; for the movable furniture of ScanNet, it is higher for layouts with all objects piled near the room center than for the real layouts in most rooms. When an object is given in the prompt, the models place a related object relative to it. When the wall of each object is given, they place most objects against that wall, but do not keep all objects inside a given room rectangle. As a prior for object search, the generated placements reduce the searched area relative to a uniform prior, but by less than a third of the reduction obtained with a prior built from real layouts, and by less than two thirds when both priors are spatially smoothed. These results support the use of LLMs for object-room associations and for writing coordinates under constraints determined by another component, followed by a geometric check, but not as a source of placement priors where real layouts are available.
  </details>

- **[PoliMop: Policy-in-the-Loop Motion Planning in 3D Spaces for Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18110)**  `arXiv:2611.18110`  
  _Yael Fassbind, Valentin N. Hartmann, Huang Su, Yijiang Huang, Stelian Coros_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reaching a goal state in a complex environment often requires a robot to overcome challenging terrain and obstacles. Which routes are accessible depends on the robot's physical design, the control policy it uses, and how it modifies its surroundings. We present a kinodynamic rapidly-exploring random tree (RRT) planner that uses physics simulation to exploit skills encoded in the control policy, which allows it to find a path in the scene with respect to the robot's capabilities. The planner samples states from a reduced state space in 3D around the scene and expands the RRT toward each sample using policy rollouts. We guide sampling with a preplanned path for a simple mass-point model to focus exploration on promising regions leading toward the goal state, reducing planning time by up to 63%. Simulation experiments with quadrupeds, humanoids, and wheeled robots demonstrate that our approach supports drop-in integration of various robots and control policies, exploiting their physical and policy capabilities without modifying the underlying planning algorithm. Predefined environmental modifications, such as placing a plank across a gap to build a bridge, will automatically be picked up by the planner as the robot's reachable space expands.
  </details>

- **[A Generalizable Approach to Safety-Aware Robotic Behavior Planning with an Explicit Constraint Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18026)**  `arXiv:2611.18026`  
  _Xianxu Wu, Shang-Ching Liu, Zhaocong Liang, Hengxiang Chen, Yujian Ma, Shenwen Deng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language and vision-language models have substantially advanced robotic behavior planning from natural-language instructions and visual observations. However, safe manipulation in tabletop environments remains challenging because the safety constraints required for a task depend on the surrounding scene and may not be explicitly specified in the instruction. We propose a safety-aware behavior planning framework based on an explicit Constraint Space that stores reusable textual safety rules. Rather than retrieving rules solely by semantic similarity, we formulate constraint selection as task- and environment-conditioned applicability learning. A lightweight query-conditioned dual-tower model identifies the constraints applicable to the current context, which are then provided together with the original task and image to a multimodal behavior planner. To handle incorrect constraint selections and previously unseen hazards, we introduce a multimodal Safety Inspector that evaluates generated primitive plans, reconstructs the complete applicable constraint set, and provides corrective planning feedback. Validated inspection feedback is further used for test-time adaptation of constraint applicability and parameter-efficient adaptation of the Safety Inspector through LoRA, while newly identified constraints are incorporated into the Constraint Space. The resulting framework integrates explicit safety knowledge representation,context-aware constraint selection, corrective safety inspection, and test-time knowledge adaptation for safety-aware robotic behavior planning.
  </details>

- **[Reusing Locomotion Training Recipes Across Humanoids via Body-Aligned Virtual Joints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18003)**  `arXiv:2611.18003`  
  _Sol Choi, Mingon Kim, Seokin Seo, Jongeun Choi, Donghyeon Kim, Yisoo Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has enabled robust humanoid locomotion, yet training recipes (rewards, architectures, hyperparameters) remain hard to reuse across robots with different native joint coordinates, since policies output native joint angles while action scales, bounds, gains, and penalties are defined in those robot-specific coordinates. Existing transmission models convert native targets into actuator commands but do not standardize the coordinate interface exposed to the policy. We introduce a body-aligned virtual joint space upstream of this mapping: policy actions and observations follow a common anatomical convention, and a deterministic kinematic layer maps virtual targets to native ones, leaving the existing transmission and low-level control stack unchanged. Under a single unmodified recipe on three humanoids with different leg-axis layouts, it is the only interface that tracks accurately, keeps the feet aligned with the travel direction, and trains stably on all three, and on Unitree G1 hardware it reduces heading drift by nearly a factor of six.
  </details>

- **[Per-Agent-Safe Navigation of Globally Driven Robot Collectives via Affine Field Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17859)**  `arXiv:2611.17859`  
  _Seunghwan Jang, Steven Ceron, Wei Xiao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safe navigation of robot collectives requires coordinating motion and deformation so that the collective can traverse constrained environments while ensuring each robot's safety. Existing controllers for swarmalator collectives largely rely on local control, where each robot receives an individual command, which causes the control and communication requirements to grow with the number of agents. We introduce a broadcast control formulation in which a single spatially affine field governs both the motion and deformation of the collective. The scalar component of the field regulates collective size, and its spatial gradient generates collective translation through non-reciprocal interactions. Both components enter the collective dynamics directly, which allows per-robot wall, actuation, and cohesion constraints to be enforced using control barrier functions within a single quadratic program for the whole collective. The resulting controller continuously adapts the collective size to the local environment, and enables safe passage through corridors narrower than the unconstrained collective without pre-planned resizing. We further derive how the available actuation budget determines the reachable speed and minimum passable corridor width, and we demonstrate that two independently driven collectives can simultaneously traverse a shared junction using distinct fixed phase labels without inter-collective coordination.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Prediction Gain: LASR as a Metric for Action-Grounded Latents in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17775)**  `arXiv:2611.17775`  
  _Yu Zhou, Changsong Lei, Yixiang Wei, Kairui Wen, Huankun Sheng, Ming Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models that support planning, control, and interpretable reasoning require latent representations that genuinely encode action. Yet the large-scale video data from which such models are learned overwhelmingly lack action annotations. Latent-action models infer action-like variables from observation sequences, enabling action-aware learning from unlabeled videos or trajectories. A fundamental concern, however, remains: a latent that improves future prediction may simply encode the current state, episode progress, task identity, or other predictive shortcuts. Existing evaluation practices---reconstruction loss, rollout fidelity, action recovery, or downstream performance---cannot isolate this distinction. We introduce the Latent Action Support Ratio (LASR), a dimensionless metric that measures, under offline evaluation on held-out data, whether a learned latent provides predictive support for action-related targets beyond the current state and non-action alternatives. LASR provides two complementary summaries: GeoLASR captures typical support, while WorstLASR identifies the weakest case. We instantiate the LASR benchmark with recorded actions and corresponding physical state changes as targets, episode-held-out splits, one predictor type for all comparisons, and positive and negative controls. Experiments on recorded robot manipulation data and a matched multi-task panel demonstrate that LASR detects known action information, rejects representative non-action shortcuts, and enables graded comparisons among imperfect latent representations. We further use LASR to train and audit a lightweight residual adapter, obtaining descriptively better point estimates whose uncertainty is assessed with paired comparisons. LASR thus provides a principled offline protocol for measuring action-related predictive support in world-model latents.
  </details>

- **[When Does Cross-View Consistency Matter in Multi-View Appearance Augmentation? A Controlled Study for Visuomotor Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17751)**  `arXiv:2611.17751`  
  _Zixuan Jiang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-view visual augmentation often seeks consistency across cameras, but it is unclear which forms of consistency matter for downstream control. We use simulation-state replay to manipulate cross-view coupling while preserving the per-camera perturbation distributions, physical trajectories, and action labels. Across seven LIBERO manipulation tasks, marginal-matched changes in global appearance, ghost injection, and target color have small average costs for a late-fusion Diffusion Policy: point estimates are near zero or negative, and the largest upper endpoint of the 90% posterior credible intervals is 2.55 percentage points. In contrast, geometrically anti-coupled observations reduce success by 3.30 percentage points relative to coupled observations (90% CrI [0.64, 6.28]), with displacement magnitude, direction marginals, and the number of affected views matched. Camera interventions reveal substantial dependence on wrist-view geometry, while feature analysis shows appearance sensitivity patterns associated with the factors randomized during training. ACT shows a higher target-color cost estimate than DP, with uncertainty that includes zero and sensitivity to task composition. Within the tested tasks and policy configurations, these findings motivate selective consistency constraints: appearance coupling can sometimes be relaxed, whereas task-relevant geometry and cross-view spatial correspondence warrant protection.
  </details>

- **[ERA: Error-Resistant Autonomous Object Navigation with Predicted Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17730)**  `arXiv:2611.17730`  
  _Shunyang Huang, Shuo Wang, Wenyuan Xie, Mingyu Lin, Yue Ding, Hongtao Lu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depth and 3D foundation models can predict geometry from RGB images, allowing zero-shot object navigation without a depth sensor. However, when predicted depth is mapped as if it came from a sensor, view-dependent errors accumulate in the map, remove exploration goals, and end the search before the agent can observe and correct them. We propose ERA, a training-free object navigation system that replaces the depth sensor with depth predicted from RGB images and poses. ERA follows a paradigm we call Move with Error: movement provides new views that correct map errors, and the corrected map enables further movement. It realizes this paradigm with revisable occupancy and with exploration from reachable viewpoints, which continues the search when no frontier remains, and verifies the target across views before stopping. On three benchmarks, ERA achieves state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods without depth sensors. Its map and exploration components also raise VLFM's success from 10.8% to 61.7% under the same predicted depth. We further deploy ERA on a real robot.
  </details>

- **[Ego2Flow: Benchmarking the Traffic-System Impact of Autonomous Driving on Maps Distilled from Real-World Roads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17720)**  `arXiv:2611.17720`  
  _Yu Dong, Weiying Shen, Hangyu Li, Shu Han, Hong Li, Xiangbo Gao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  End-to-end autonomous driving is predominantly evaluated through ego-vehicle safety and task completion. Its deployment in urban traffic, however, creates sustained interactions among vehicles whose collective effects remain insufficiently evaluated. We introduce Ego2Flow, a closed-loop benchmark for assessing the traffic-system impact of autonomous driving on maps distilled from real-world cities. To construct realistic 3D urban environments for sustained closed-loop traffic simulation, we develop an agent-guided map distillation framework that integrates real-world maps and observations with complementary geographic information.
