Through an iterative feedback mechanism and a traffic information graph that jointly guide scene generation, the framework preserves lane topology, critical road geometry and decision-relevant visual conditions while simplifying nonessential details and completing unobserved regions. Using our proposed framework, we generate and publicly release six CARLA maps from the Argoverse 2 dataset, covering the corresponding real-world regions and enabling paired comparisons of driving behavior under real and simulated observations. On these maps, Ego2Flow evaluates how autonomous driving policies affect traffic efficiency, safety, stability and energy consumption across diverse road structures. Compared with existing construction methods, our framework achieves the lowest average displacement error between planning outputs from paired real and simulated observations. We further benchmark representative driving policies to characterize their system-level impact on urban traffic. Project Page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[EViDS: An Environment--Vehicle Dynamics Integrated Simulation Platform for Intelligent Chassis Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17660)**  `arXiv:2611.17660`  
  _Lu Xiong, Yiming Zhou, Xuge Hu, Yi Zhang, Haoran Lv, Chen Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The development of intelligent chassis increasingly requires the integration of environmental and perception information with high-fidelity vehicle dynamics to support environment-aware intelligent chassis control. However, existing autonomous-driving simulators and professional vehicle-dynamics tools are generally developed for different purposes, making it difficult to integrate environmental information, vehicle dynamics, and chassis algorithms. This paper proposes EViDS, an Environment-Vehicle Dynamics Integrated Simulation Platform for Intelligent Chassis Development. EViDS integrates CARLA-based environment and sensor simulation with CarSim-based high-fidelity vehicle dynamics. Reference-pose-based state alignment and multi-rate interaction maintain spatial and temporal consistency, while ray-casting-based tire-road interaction transfers environmental information to the vehicle-dynamics model. Scenario configuration, synchronized data acquisition, and algorithm interfaces further support intelligent chassis development. The platform is evaluated through environment-dynamics interaction tests with vehicle-dynamics consistency comparisons, together with intelligent chassis applications. Results show that EViDS preserves vehicle-dynamics characteristics, reliably transfers environmental information, and supports online closed-loop intelligent chassis control, providing a unified simulation basis for environment-aware intelligent chassis development and validation.
  </details>

- **[Imitation Policies Learn a Proprioceptive Anchor: A Predictive Law for Perception Noise and Its Closed Loop Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17658)**  `arXiv:2611.17658`  
  _Christopher Malili_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Imitation policies for robot manipulation are often trained on noisy object positions, and they learn to discount the noisy reading. We show how much they discount it, and why. Because demonstrators steer toward the object, the robot's own state carries information about where the object is. A trained policy combines this proprioceptive anchor with the reading and weights the reading by $\alpha = V/(V + s^2)$, where $s^2$ is the reading noise and $V$ the anchor's error variance. $V$ belongs to the learner and is far smaller than a linear model of the demonstrations implies, which is why classical errors-in-variables (EIV) regression overestimates $\alpha$. Measured once, $V$ predicted ten held-out sensitivities across two robomimic tasks, two learners and four new noise levels, with mean absolute error 0.025 against 0.37 for classical EIV, and a parameter-free bias test rules out the simpler explanation that the policies just overestimate the noise. The discount does not carry over to deployment, where the policy produces its own state: closed-loop sensitivity to a persistent reading offset exceeded $\alpha$ in all 15 policies that passed the registered gates, although the stricter registered prediction, that all would follow the offset fully, held for 8 of them. Post hoc, lower $\alpha$ went with lower success, steeply for diffusion, and a registered prospective test confirmed this for diffusion on Can at two new noise levels: the measured $\alpha$ predicted success before any rollout, within 0.03 (95% intervals $\pm 0.08$), although training noise predicts it equally well. Every test was registered before its data, including the classical benchmark that failed, and all other analyses are labelled post hoc; all results are in simulation with synthetic Gaussian noise.
  </details>

- **[AI-guided robotic in situ fabrication of curvature- and tissue-adaptive wireless bioelectronics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17638)**  `arXiv:2611.17638`  
  _Hiroyuki Tetsuka, Jiaju Ma, Naomi Kumano, Minoru Hirano_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalized bioelectronic devices for wound therapy require not only conformal fabrication on complex body surfaces but also functional adaptation of their electrical characteristics to individual wound geometries. Here, we present an AI-guided robotic in situ printing platform for fabricating wireless wound electrotherapy devices directly on transparent wound dressings. The system integrates a 3D vision camera, a six-degree-of-freedom robotic arm, direct-ink-writing, and geometry-aware RF design optimization. AI-based wound segmentation is used to determine wound dimensions and local surface curvature, while the reconstructed three-dimensional geometry is incorporated into the electromagnetic design of the receiving coil and interdigitated electrodes. In contrast to conventional approaches that use surface geometry only for trajectory generation, the proposed method accounts for both anatomical curvature and tissue dielectric loading to compensate for geometry-induced shifts in resonant behavior. A printable silver ink was characterized in terms of rheology, dimensional reproducibility, electrical resistance, and electromechanical stability, and was used to fabricate multilayer wireless devices directly on curved polyurethane dressings. Wireless power transfer, impedance characteristics, and electric-field delivery were evaluated experimentally and numerically. Finally, robotic in situ fabrication was demonstrated on an anthropomorphic tissue-equivalent forearm phantom, where the measured dielectric properties of the phantom were incorporated into the RF optimization, enabling resonant operation near the target frequency of 25 MHz. This work establishes a perception-to-design-to-fabrication framework for geometry-adaptive wireless bioelectronics and provides a technological foundation for future physical-AI-enabled manufacturing of personalized on-body electrotherapy devices.
  </details>

- **[On the Utility of Pre-Trained Visual Representations for Policy Learning of Contact-Rich Insertion Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17631)**  `arXiv:2611.17631`  
  _Leon Kim, Dinesh Jayaraman, Michael Posa_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visuomotor imitation learning has become a predominant paradigm for real-world robotic manipulation, yet it remains data-inefficient and brittle to task-irrelevant visual shifts. Pre-trained visual representations promise to ease this burden by distilling priors from internet-scale data, but the ``right'' representation for robotics remains unresolved: existing studies span broad but sparse task distributions and reach conclusions that vary and even conflict, pointing to strong task and setting dependence. We examine this question by bringing a magnifying glass to a common contact-rich manipulation task: insertion. We compare representations from widely used vision foundation models against an explicit contact-centric representation on two closely related insertion tasks, perturb each along controlled axes of occlusion and physical tolerance, and test whether conclusions transfer to the real world. Task dependence proves to be more subtle than previous, broadly scoped evaluations revealed: the best representation differs even between our near-identical tasks, and small changes shift trends in unpredictable ways. Despite this variability, results across simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate the distinct value of spatially-grounded, and in particular, contact-centric information for contact-rich policy learning, while underscoring how difficult it is to estimate such cues reliably under realistic visual complexity and sensor noise. We hope these findings motivate further study of representations in contact-rich manipulation, along with advances in contact perception and its integration into visuomotor policy learning.
  </details>

- **[TouchTwin: Scaling Human Touch Data via Real-to-Sim-to-Real](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17619)**  `arXiv:2611.17619`  
  _Huiwen Han, Nuo Chen, Ziyao Zeng, Dayou Li, Hao Wang, Amir Hossein Rahmati, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human demonstrations are becoming the main source of data for dexterous manipulation, and real-to-sim-to-real pipelines multiply each demonstration in simulation. These pipelines rebuild the interaction from vision, which recovers how the hand moves. Which fingers touch, when, and how hard remain unobserved, because a hand pose shows where each finger stops on the surface rather than how far it is driven to close. This gap is a force gap: on held-out recordings, a hand policy that imitates the observed pose grips with 0.31x the recorded force, and a policy trained on physics replays of vision-tracked poses grips with 1.55x. TouchTwin closes the gap with measured human touch. Each selected TouchScale recording, captured with head and wrist RGB-D cameras and an 880-taxel glove, is rebuilt in MuJoCo. Coding agents write the objects as physics-tested programs, and a per-subject tactile hand replays the interaction with the recorded touch setting the contact and the closing force of each finger. In simulation, a policy trained on touch-grounded replays grips at 0.99x the recorded force and succeeds in 20/20 trials on held-out recordings and 35/36 unseen perturbations; a contact predictor trained on them misses 13% of real contact frames, against 32% for vision-based replays. Each replay also seeds perturbed demonstrations with simulated contact and force labels, rendered as realistic video in new views and scenes.
  </details>

- **[X-Nav Technical Report: Real-World Long-Horizon Indoor-to-Outdoor Navigation on a Humanoid Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17598)**  `arXiv:2611.17598`  
  _Jiaxin Huang, Chengxing Lin, Xiaoyu Luo, Sirui Chen, Jingyu Guo, Aolin Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon navigation from indoors to outdoors remains an open problem for humanoid robots: one instruction covers rooms, doors and roads, and the camera moves with the gait. Worken, AGIBOT's measure of productive robot work, depends on robots that reach their workplaces autonomously. We present X-Nav, to our knowledge the first system to take a bipedal humanoid through a language-instructed long-horizon mission from indoors to outdoors. X-Nav covers a pipeline from data to deployment. Its data engine collects navigation data in open-source scenes, in digital twins of deployment sites and on real robots. Its navigation model, MELIOR, takes only RGB images from the robot's built-in camera and comes in two versions: MELIOR-A predicts actions, and MELIOR-N predicts a navigation goal. Its agentic workflow, X-Navarch, turns a brief human instruction into sub-tasks and deploys MELIOR on the robot with explicit target navigation and obstacle avoidance. On the Lingxi X2, X-Nav completes a mission of about a hundred metres from an office lobby to a store in a neighbouring building; LiDAR and odometry are used for target approach and low-level control. The mission is completed with both versions of MELIOR: the robot follows a change of destination and walks through a door from the lobby to the road, and with X-Navarch it also passes static and dynamic obstacles on the route. During deployment, MELIOR is adapted with data collected in a digital twin of the site; the adaptation raises its closed-loop success rate in the twin, and the adapted model is then deployed on the robot at the real site. In addition, MELIOR achieves competitive results on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Object-to-Object Hazard Reasoning into 3D Risk Fields for Task-Conditioned Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17566)**  `arXiv:2611.17566`  
  _Mariana Sosa Guzmán, Yide Song, Amin Abyaneh, Gregory Dudek, Hsiu-Chin Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety in robotics does not solely depend on geometric constraints, but also on semantics: the given task, environment properties, and interactions between objects in the scene. We present a framework based on vision-language models (VLMs) to infer task- and scene-dependent safety constraints for robot manipulation. We provide the VLM a structured safety tool based on an RGB-D observation and a natural-language task instruction that first decides whether the task should be rejected since its execution may result in a non-recoverable hazardous outcome. Otherwise, it identifies preventable object-pair hazards and specifies the spatial relations that must be avoided. Open-vocabulary segmentation and depth information are then used to ground the detected objects in 3D and construct continuous risk fields that represent the corresponding hazardous regions. These fields are integrated with an obstacle avoidance module that corrects nominal robot motions during execution while accounting for the risk field. We evaluate the framework on preventable hazards, tasks requiring rejection, and safe variants in simulation and the real-world. Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Talk to Act: A Synthetic Multi-Turn Dialogue Benchmark for Human Intent Extraction and Action Planning in Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17549)**  `arXiv:2611.17549`  
  _Deepu Krishnareddy, Mohammad Haziq Khan, Michael Danner, Matthias Rätsch_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn conversational human-robot interaction (HRI) continues to be a challenging problem in robotics, as many existing systems rely on rigid, command-driven interfaces that can be unintuitive for users. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) provide a promising direction, given their ability to process natural language, generate structured outputs, and support tool-based reasoning, all of which are relevant for interpreting user intent in robotic settings. However, progress in this area is constrained by the limited availability of datasets and benchmarks that capture the nuances of real-world human communication, such as multi-turn dialogue, ambiguity, and context-dependent requests.In this work, we present a synthetically generated multi-turn dialogue dataset for HRI, annotated with action intents, object references, and task plans across a range of scenarios and user personas. Our primary focus is on evaluating the capability of small, open-weight language models for robotic tasks, particularly in settings where local deployment and efficiency are important. To this end, we frame intent extraction as a tool-calling problem where each predicted action corresponds to a structured function call with arguments grounded in the dialogue and benchmark models ranging from 270M to 8B parameters on their ability to perform intent extraction and action planning without task-specific fine-tuning.Our results show that these models can achieve reasonable performance on the task even at relatively small scales and without any task-specific fine-tuning. This suggests that compact, locally deployable models already provide a useful baseline for conversational robotic systems. We expect that task-specific fine-tuning and more realistic training data will enhance performance further and make such models increasingly suitable for real-world robotic applications.
  </details>

- **[Learning Latent Goal Costs for Visual World-Model Planning with an Expanding Curriculum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17529)**  `arXiv:2611.17529`  
  _Konstantinos Mylonas, Thrasyvoulos Spyropoulos_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image-goal planning with a frozen visual world model depends on how predicted observations are scored against the goal. Mean-squared error (MSE) between embeddings from a pretrained visual encoder can provide a useful signal near a goal, yet favor states across an obstacle that are visually similar to the goal and difficult to reach. We fit a small residual adapter on top of frozen I-JEPA embeddings to improve this latent goal cost. Starting near each goal, a planner collects trajectories from progressively wider regions of initial states. The adapter learns from the order of observations along successful trajectories, with additional comparisons inferred from failed rollouts. No task-specific progress labels are used for fitting, so the procedure can be applied to other goal-reaching tasks. DotWall and PointMaze are used in this work. In paired 50-case evaluations with identical starts, goals, world models, and planner settings, success increases from 54% to 98% in DotWall and from 82% to 96% in PointMaze. On 16 unseen DotWall layout-goal cases, the adapter fitted on one fixed layout improves agreement between latent-cost ordering and shortest legal-path ordering from 71.9% to 85.8%. The results indicate that planning experience can improve a frozen visual representation's goal cost without retraining the encoder or the world model.
  </details>

- **[Racing the Clock: Teaching VLNs Budget-Conditioned Object Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17442)**  `arXiv:2611.17442`  
  _Maitree Meher, Siming He, S. Shankar Sastry, Jitendra Malik, Claire Tomlin, Jonas Frey_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A rescue robot entering a building with thirty seconds on the clock should sprint toward the single most promising room; given ten minutes, it should instead explore a broader tour. Yet vision-language-navigation (VLN) models are typically evaluated under a fixed episode limit that the policy never observes, even though time-critical missions require behavior to adapt to the available budget. We study budget-conditioned object search: given a target category and an explicit action budget, an agent must find as many distinct target instances as possible before the budget expires. We investigate how to supervise VLNs for this task by comparing a privileged TSP expert, which plans near-optimal routes using the full map and true target locations, with a room-level Bellman expert that maximizes expected return under partial observability using high-level cross-scene semantic priors. Despite the TSP expert achieving substantially higher expert performance, its demonstrations are less effective for learning exploration: on held-out HM3D scenes, the Bellman-supervised VLN consistently outperforms the TSP-supervised VLN, with the advantage becoming more pronounced at longer budgets. At 2000 steps (500m), the Bellman-supervised VLN finds 71.4% of reachable target instances, compared with 65.5% for the TSP-supervised VLN and 44.8% for the pretrained VLN. These results highlight a distinction between optimal routing and optimal exploration: effective exploration supervision should optimize decisions under partial observability rather than imitate routes that are optimal only once the environment is fully known.
  </details>

- **[Agentic-Teacher: An Agentic Data Engine for Failure-Driven VLA Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17394)**  `arXiv:2611.17394`  
  _Gongyue Teng, Yuqing Xie, Yixian Zhang, Feng Gao, Shu'ang Yu, Jiaji Rao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving a pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) model requires demonstrations that address its weaknesses and provide effective supervision for learning. Coding agents can autonomously generate demonstrations through environment interaction, yet successful execution alone does not ensure effective supervision for VLA learning. We introduce Agentic-Teacher, an autonomous data engine for failure-driven VLA post-training. Given a VLA checkpoint, a task specification, and configured environment tools, the engine analyzes failed rollouts, selects intervention states, and iteratively develops closed-loop code policies through execution feedback. Validated policies become reusable demonstration generators that collect successful continuations from states where the VLA struggles. To make these demonstrations suitable for learning, Agentic-Teacher introduces temporally correlated action noise to broaden state-action coverage, removes idle frames while preserving gripper transitions, and filters trajectories using the frozen base model's flow-matching loss. The resulting demonstrations are combined with successful VLA rollouts for post-training, yielding an updated policy that operates without coding-agent assistance. Agentic-Teacher thus connects the weaknesses of a target VLA to both the generation and curation of its training data. In simulation, it improves $\pi_0$ success rates from 79.4% to 87.8% on LIBERO-10, from 80.4% to 90.6% on MetaWorld-10, and from 0% to 30.0% across three RoboCasa tasks.
  </details>

- **[HEIR: Harness Egocentric Intent for Human-Robot Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17238)**  `arXiv:2611.17238`  
  _Yichi Zhang, Bo Wang, Zhenghan Zhu, Yutong Han, Jiayin Zou, Gao Kejun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For robots to assist, serve, and collaborate with humans, they must not only interpret human requests conveyed through language and multimodal intentional cues, but also execute long-horizon tasks that interleave navigation and manipulation. In this paper, we introduce HEIR, a resource for studying how to Harness Egocentric Intent for Human-Robot Interaction. HEIR pairs human speech, measured gaze, and egocentric video with robot observations and teleoperated navigation-manipulation trajectories, while each session preserves successive episodes, with each request fulfilled through multiple execution subtasks. The captured data, together with human-authored annotations, support the study of intent grounding, execution planning, and task completion within a unified interaction setting. To demonstrate the value of HEIR, we conduct extensive offline experiments to evaluate how models benefit from additional human-centric context across these tasks, together with real-robot experiments that assess execution task completion. HEIR thus provides a valuable resource for studying user-centric robot interaction in naturalistic household environments. Our demo and dataset are available online.
  </details>

- **[VOKE: Visual Object Kinematics Estimator for Dexterous In-Hand Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16423)**  `arXiv:2611.16423`  
  _Shengjia Zhu, Wenqiang Xu, Lin Shao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-based dexterous in-hand manipulation requires knowing not only where an object is, but also how it moves. However, most robotic manipulation systems rely on off-the-shelf visual perception models whose outputs are limited to static object masks, geometry, or 6D poses, leaving downstream controllers to infer object dynamics implicitly from noisy visual observations. We present VOKE, a kinematics-aware perception framework that augments existing object and hand-object pose estimators to recover a complete object kinematic state comprising pose, linear and angular velocity, and linear and angular acceleration. VOKE contains two core perception modules and a downstream application study. First, a Pluggable Kinematics Module (PKM) attaches to the output of a standard pose estimator and predicts the object's linear and angular velocity and acceleration from temporal visual features. Second, a Kinematics-guided Pose Refinement Module (KPRM) converts this motion vector into a second-order, motion-prior reference pose and refines the per-frame pose estimates. We evaluate VOKE on DexYCB and OakInk, where it consistently improves object pose estimation across three different backbones and substantially reduces velocity and acceleration estimation error compared with pose differencing. As a downstream application, we use Kinematics-Augmented Reinforcement Learning (KARL) to compare a kinematics-augmented policy with a pose-history baseline. After training the policies in simulation, we transfer the kinematics-augmented policy to the physical robot without policy fine-tuning, demonstrating the complete perception-to-control pathway in the target domain.
  </details>

- **[OneSteer: Unlocking Steerable General Humanoid Motion Tracking via One-Step Diffusion Whole-Body Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41354)**  `arXiv:2610.41354`  
  _Yifei Huang, Yazhao Yang, Li Weijia, Tianle Shi, Ji Jin, Zhihui Cai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployment objectives often specify desired outcomes without providing revised trajectories, while parts of the original reference must still be followed. We present OneSteer, a one-step diffusion whole-body controller that combines general motion tracking with selective test-time steering. To accommodate new objectives, differentiable task costs guide optimization of the generator's input noise with frozen weights, while body-group reference masks relax selected tracking requirements. Predicted state-action plans provide a shared interface for task optimization and feedback-conditioned execution. For nominal tracking, the model trained from scratch achieves the lowest local tracking error among the evaluated baselines on successful held-out AMASS and BONES clips. For steering, one frozen distilled controller adapts across diverse deployment objectives in simulation, raising the clean passage rate under a 1.25 m bar from 2% to 90%. On a physical Unitree G1, OneSteer completes all 27 nominal tracking trials across nine diverse whole-body motion sequences without falling. With a steering cost derived from a signed distance field built using only onboard LiDAR, it also passes under a 1.1 m low bar in four of five attempts and steps over a 12 cm hurdle without falling in all three attempts. Beyond control, prediction variation in the model trained from scratch ranks likely tracking failures without physics rollouts. This scoring method is more than 10x faster than simulation-based scoring with five rollouts per clip and guides targeted fine-tuning. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EmbodiExp: Compositional Experience Transfer for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39180)**  `arXiv:2610.39180`  
  _Zhifeng Gu, Ying Chen, Rui Jiang, Lei Yi, Bing Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Experience from past tasks can help embodied agents perform new tasks, but it may not remain applicable or effective when conditions change. Tasks with different overall goals often share subgoals, providing a basis for cross-task experience transfer. We present EmbodiExp, a framework for constructing outcome-grounded subgoal experiences and composing them in new tasks. Each experience describes how to pursue a subgoal, when the guidance applies, and what outcome must be verified for completion. Verified outcomes from applying one experience can satisfy the conditions for applying another, allowing experiences from different past tasks to be composed online. By examining the actions and observed outcomes at the subgoal level, EmbodiExp retains experience from successful subgoals and uses explicit action failures to identify actions to avoid under specific conditions. Experiments on EmbodiedBench and VIGIL assess cross-task transfer with experiences kept fixed throughout held-out evaluation. EmbodiExp achieves success rates of 68.0% on EB-Habitat and 68.3% on EB-ALFRED, compared with 50.0% and 54.7% for the agent without stored experience.
  </details>

- **[IORA: Irregular Object Immobilization under Restricted Actuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30969)**  `arXiv:2610.30969`  
  _Sang-Hun Han, Changyeon Won, JunGyu Lee, Min-Gyu Park, Hae-Gon Jeon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Stable grasping under task-specific disturbances is important for robotic manipulation. However, most dexterous grasp synthesis methods are developed for highly actuated hands in free space, and low-DoF hands may not be able to realize the contact topologies required by these grasps. Meanwhile, support surfaces can provide useful contact forces, but are often treated only as geometric constraints. We present a support-aware grasp synthesis framework for object immobilization with low-DoF coupled hands. Given irregular object geometry, our method represents both hand and object local surface regions as graphs and searches for kinematically feasible matchings between them. We then refine the wrist pose and motor state for geometric contact feasibility and validates the refined grasps through physical rollouts. Starting from the validated grasp pose, an optional task-guided local search explores nearby grasp configurations by varying the wrist pose and motor state, and selects one according to the specified task condition. Experiments on diverse objects show that our method generates various grasp configurations that achieve high grasp success and strong disturbance resistance. Real-world experiments further demonstrate physically executable grasps which enable object immobilization in cooking scenario.
  </details>

- **[PingpongDuo: Agile Humanoid Table Tennis Doubles with Safe Team Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20579)**  `arXiv:2610.20579`  
  _Haoran Jiang, Yichao Zhong, Kai Zhang, Cheng Liu, Yixuan Pan, Zhenjie Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike singles, humanoid table tennis doubles requires teammates to strike in strict alternation while sharing a confined workspace. Rapid repositioning can compromise striking accuracy, while uncoordinated teammate movements introduce collision risks. We present PingpongDuo, a hierarchical framework for agile humanoid doubles with safe team coordination. At the team level, a planner jointly selects positioning targets and uses learned risk estimates to filter risky proposals, preferentially preserving the current striker's target. At the individual level, separate footwork and striking skills combine agile movement with precise racket control. The skills are guided by human motion references, including footwork collected from human doubles play, and trained from states produced by one another to support rapid transitions. A racket-arm residual further refines strikes after movement using return feedback from a simulator calibrated with real ball-flight and contact measurements. In simulation, PingpongDuo improves the ball-return success rate by 13.2% over the state-of-the-art baseline, while achieving approximately 2 cm positioning error during clearance. In real-world experiments, a robot completes a 2.15 m clearance-and-recovery cycle in 2.4 s with a peak speed of 2.23 m/s, while two robots sustain more than 10 consecutive strikes in strict alternation.
  </details>

- **[Fast Non-Parametric Heteroscedastic Imitation Learning With Geometric Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08650)**  `arXiv:2610.08650`  
  _Maximilian Mühlbauer, Arne Sachtler, Markus Knauer, Cem Küçükgenç, Yanlong Huang, Alin Albu-Schäffer, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When learning probabilistic policies from human demonstrations, data-efficient learning and fast adaptations to new scenarios are key requirements. One popular way to achieve intuitive and reliable adaptations is through non-parametric, typically kernel-based, methods. However, existing solutions either fail to account for the geometry of manifolds common in robotics, limiting data efficiency, or, when geometry-aware, provide unreliable uncertainty estimates or require retraining to adapt. We propose a non-parametric approach leveraging geometric priors in scenarios of data scarcity and heteroscedastic uncertainties for probabilistic modeling. We utilize the method to formulate policies based on time or robot state, where non-separable diagonal kernels allow capturing uncertainty relations between degrees of freedom for same-sized in- and outputs. Fast updates, requiring less than 3 ms for a trajectory involving both position and orientation are possible through an optimized formulation. Our approach supports both manifold-valued input and manifold-valued output with large orientation changes. Using task parameterization, adaptation to different object poses is easily possible. We evaluate the approach on a set of toy examples and on real robot manipulation tasks both in autonomous execution and in shared control scenarios.
  </details>

- **[The Missing Touch: Sensing and Displaying Localized Contact Geometry Brings Teleoperation Closer to Human Dexterity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19372)**  `arXiv:2608.19372`  
  _Rohan Kota, Gregory Reardon, J. Edward Colgate_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fundamental challenge in robotic teleoperation is enabling an operator to control a remote robot as effortlessly and intuitively as their own hands. Despite the growing use of teleoperation to collect demonstrations for training autonomous robot policies, teleoperated performance still falls significantly short of human dexterity, even for basic tasks. Here, we present evidence that a key factor contributing to this performance gap is the absence of localized tactile feedback. We equip a two-degree-of-freedom (DoF) bilateral force-feedback telemanipulator with a vision-based tactile sensor and a 32-pixel tactile display, allowing us to capture spatial contact patterns on the robot finger and render them on the operator's fingertip. In teleoperated button-discrimination and peg-rolling tasks, we demonstrate that reproducing spatially distributed contact information brings teleoperated movements closer to natural human behavior through fewer unnecessary motions and task completion steps, significantly reducing task completion time and producing more consistent demonstrations across trials and operators. We further show that these improvements in human demonstrations can propagate to autonomous policies: policies trained on tactile-enhanced demonstrations exhibited similar reductions in both task completion time and deviation from natural human behavior. Together, these results establish spatially distributed tactile feedback as an important component of dexterous robotic teleoperation and demonstrate its potential to improve safety-critical teleoperation and enable more humanlike demonstrations for training autonomous robot policies.
  </details>

- **[Courteous Anticipation: Improving Long-Lived Task Planning in Persistent Shared Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20289)**  `arXiv:2607.20289`  
  _Md Ridwan Hossain Talukder, Elizabeth Phillips, Gregory J. Stein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We consider a task planning scenario in which robots sharing a persistent environment are assigned tasks one at a time from a held-out sequence. Standard task planners, lacking foresight of future tasks and inconsiderate of others' constraints, solve each task in isolation, leaving terminal states that increase future cost for all; side effects that compound over lengthy task sequences. To reduce cost over the sequence, a robot must anticipate how its actions now may impact performance on future tasks for all robots sharing the environment. Therefore, we present courteous anticipatory planning, wherein a model-based planner proposes candidate plans and selects the one that jointly minimizes immediate cost and aggregated expected future cost across all robots, estimated via independent per-robot learned estimators. This factored formulation avoids combinatorial joint rollouts and supports modular deployment: adding a robot requires only training its own estimator. We evaluate in two persistent PDDL domains, a home environment with robots that have similar capabilities but different responsibilities, and a restaurant environment where robots' distinct capabilities create states that other robots lack the capability to resolve. During lengthy task sequences, our planner reduces total cost by 10.4% versus myopic and 4.5% versus selfish anticipatory planning in a two-robot home environment and by 20.5% and 6.7%, respectively, in a three-robot restaurant.
  </details>

- **[Long-Distance Real-World Navigation of the Legged-Wheeled Robot Go2-W Using Deep Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21387)**  `arXiv:2606.21387`  
  _Takaaki Matsuzawa, Kiyoshi Irie, Tomoaki Yoshida, Taro Suzuki, Yoshitaka Hara, Masahiro Tomono_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Legged-wheeled robots have been extensively studied for their potential to combine the efficient flat-ground mobility of wheels with the rough-terrain adaptability of legs. However, their application to long-distance autonomous navigation in real environments remains limited. This paper reports our efforts to build a deep reinforcement learning-based locomotion controller and an autonomous navigation system for the commercially available Go2-W legged-wheeled robot. The study focuses on the integrated system and the practical challenges associated with its sustained operation over long distances in real-world environments. For locomotion control, we extended our proprioception-only policy, previously developed for quadruped robots, to Go2-W, a 16-degree-of-freedom legged-wheeled robot. We additionally found that wheeled locomotion concentrates the load on the hip joints, resulting in heat concentration that hinders sustained travel. To address this issue, we developed a policy with periodic foot-lifting motion that distributes the joint loads and mitigates heat concentration. We evaluated the integrated system at the Tsukuba Challenge 2025. The robot autonomously traversed an approximately \SI{2.8}{\kilo\meter} route that included sidewalks, a park, and stairs without stopping owing to overheating.
  </details>

- **[Robots that Collaborate: Sequential Asymmetric Imitation for Learning Coupled Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16490)**  `arXiv:2606.16490`  
  _Ranpeng Qiu, Yincong Chen, Zihao Li, Yanan Zhou, Guoqiang Ren, Weiming Zhi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaborative mobile manipulation requires robots to coordinate with a partially observed partner while physically interacting through shared objects. This is difficult because failures often arise not from poor local skills, but from mistimed waiting, yielding, pulling, releasing, or repositioning. We study this problem with two bimanual mobile manipulators coupled through rigid and deformable objects. We propose Sequential Asymmetric Imitation (SAI), a single-teleoperator curriculum for learning coupled multi-robot behaviors without synchronized dual-operator demonstrations or explicit inter-robot communication. SAI trains Robot A from unilateral demonstrations with a compliant human partner, trains Robot B against the deployed Robot A policy, and then refines Robot A using sparse interventions near coordination failures. This staged process exposes the policies to increasingly realistic partner behaviors, including delay, phase mismatch,insufficient yielding, and interaction conflict. Across real-world dual-robot manipulation tasks, SAI improves task success, phase synchronization, and partner-contingent yielding over independent imitation and curriculum-ablation baselines. These results suggest that physically coupled collaboration can be learned through the structure of the imitation curriculum, rather than through synchronized multi-operator demonstrations or explicit coordination mechanisms. More videos on project page:this http URL
  </details>

- **[E0: Expressive Fine-Grained Discrete Action Prediction for Vision-Language-Action Models via Tweedie Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.21542)**  `arXiv:2511.21542`  
  _Zhihao Zhan, Jiaying Zhou, Likui Zhang, Qinhan Lyu, Hao Liu, Weizheng Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models offer a unified framework for robotic manipulation by integrating visual perception, language understanding, and control generation. However, existing VLA systems still struggle to generalize across diverse tasks, scenes, and camera viewpoints, and often produce coarse or unstable actions. We argue that these limitations are closely tied to the structural properties of actions in VLA settings, including the inherent multi-peaked nature of action distributions, the token-based symbolic reasoning of pretrained VLM/VLA backbones, and the effective finite resolution imposed by real-world robotic control. Motivated by these properties, we introduce E0, a tweedie discrete diffusion framework that formulates action generation as iterative denoising over quantized action tokens. By operating in a discrete action space with a principled diffusion process, E0 naturally aligns with token-based reasoning, supports fine-grained yet executable action control, and avoids the distributional mismatch of masking-based discrete diffusion. Experiments on LIBERO, VLABench, ManiSkill, and a real-world Franka arm demonstrate that E0 achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse environments, outperforming strong baselines by 8.1% on average.
  </details>

- **[STITCHER: Constrained Trajectory Planning in Complex Environments with Real-Time Motion Primitive Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14893)**  `arXiv:2510.14893`  
  _Helene J. Levy, Brett T. Lopez_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous high-speed navigation through large, complex environments requires real-time generation of agile trajectories that are dynamically feasible, collision-free, and satisfy state or actuator constraints. Modern trajectory planning techniques primarily use numerical optimization, as they enable the systematic computation of high-quality, expressive trajectories that satisfy various constraints. However, stringent requirements on computation time and the risk of numerical instability can limit the use of optimization-based planners in safety-critical scenarios. This work presents an optimization-free planning framework called STITCHER that stitches short trajectory segments together with graph search to compute long-range, expressive, and near-optimal trajectories in real-time. STITCHER outperforms modern optimization-based planners through our innovative planning architecture and several algorithmic developments that make real-time planning possible. Extensive simulation testing is performed to analyze the algorithmic components that make up STITCHER, along with a thorough comparison with three state-of-the-art optimization planners. Simulation tests show that safe trajectories can be created within a few milliseconds for paths that span the entirety of two 50 m x 50 m environments. Hardware tests with a custom quadrotor verify that STITCHER can produce trackable paths in real-time while respecting nonconvex constraints, such as limits on tilt angle and motor forces, with flight speeds up to 63 km/h.
  </details>

- **[A Stochastic Framework for Continuous-Time State Estimation of Continuum Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01381)**  `arXiv:2510.01381`  
  _Spencer Teetaert, Sven Lilge, Jessica Burgner-Kahrs, Timothy D. Barfoot_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  State estimation techniques for continuum robots (CRs) typically involve using computationally complex dynamic models, simplistic shape approximations, or are limited to quasi-static methods. These approaches can be sensitive to unmodeled disturbances acting on the robot. Inspired by a factor-graph optimization paradigm, this work introduces a continuous-time stochastic state estimation framework for continuum robots. We introduce factors based on continuous-time kinematics that are corrupted by a white noise Gaussian process (GP). By using a simple robot model paired with high-rate sensing, we show adaptability to unmodeled external forces and data dropout. The result contains an estimate of the mean and covariance for the robot's pose, velocity, and strain, each of which can be interpolated continuously in both space and time. This same interpolation scheme can be used during estimation, allowing for inclusion of measurements that are asynchronous to the estimated states. Our method's inherent sparsity leads to a linear solve complexity with respect to time and interpolation queries in constant time. We demonstrate our method on a CR with gyroscope and pose sensors, highlighting its versatility in real-world systems.
  </details>

- **[Foundation Model Driven Robotics: A Comprehensive Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10087)**  `arXiv:2507.10087`  
  _Muhammad Tayyab Khan, Ammar Waheed, Seung Ki Moon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid emergence of foundation models, particularly large language models and vision-language models, has introduced a transformative paradigm in robotics. The models offer powerful capabilities in semantic understanding, high-level reasoning, and cross-modal generalization, enabling significant advances in perception, planning, control, and human-robot interaction. This critical review provides a structured synthesis of recent developments and organizes applications into four directions: simulation-driven robotics, open-world operation, sim-to-real transfer, and adaptable robotic systems. Unlike existing surveys that emphasize isolated capabilities, this work highlights integrated, system-level strategies and evaluates their practical feasibility in real-world environments. Key enabling trends such as procedural scene generation, policy generalization, and multimodal reasoning are discussed alongside core bottlenecks, including limited embodiment, lack of multimodal data, safety risks, and computational constraints. Through this lens, this paper identifies both the architectural strengths and critical limitations of foundation model-based robotics, highlighting open challenges in real-time operation, grounding, resilience, and trust. The review concludes with a roadmap of proposed future research directions for bridging semantic reasoning and physical intelligence through more robust, interpretable, and embodied models.
  </details>

- **[Grid-based Submap Joining: An Efficient Algorithm for Simultaneously Optimizing Global Occupancy Map and Local Submap Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.12764)**  `arXiv:2501.12764`  
  _Yingyu Wang, Liang Zhao, Shoudong Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimizing robot poses and the map simultaneously has been shown to provide more accurate SLAM results. However, for non-feature based SLAM approaches, directly optimizing all the robot poses and the whole map will greatly increase the computational cost, making SLAM problems difficult to solve in large-scale environments. To solve the 2D non-feature based SLAM problem in large-scale environments more accurately and efficiently, we propose the grid-based submap joining method. Specifically, we first formulate the 2D grid-based submap joining problem as a non-linear least squares (NLLS) form to optimize the global occupancy map and local submap frames simultaneously. We then prove that in solving the NLLS problem using Gauss-Newton (GN) method, the increments of the poses in each iteration are independent of the occupancy values of the global occupancy map. Based on this property, we propose a poseonly GN algorithm equivalent to full GN method to solve the NLLS problem. The proposed submap joining algorithm is very efficient due to the independent property and the pose-only solution. Evaluations using simulations and publicly available practical 2D laser datasets confirm the outperformance of our proposed method compared to the state-of-the-art methods in terms of efficiency and accuracy, as well as the ability to solve the grid-based SLAM problem in very large-scale environments.
  </details>
